Summary:

The Income-Tax Act, 1961, Act No. 43 of 1961, came into force on April 1, 1962, and applies to the whole of India. The Act aims to consolidate and amend the law relating to income-tax and super-tax.

The document begins with the preliminary chapter, which includes the short title, extent, and commencement of the Act. It also provides definitions of various terms used throughout the Act. Some of the key definitions include:

* Advance tax: The advance tax payable in accordance with the provisions of Chapter XVII-C.
* Agricultural income: Income derived from land which is situated in India and is used for agricultural purposes, including rent or revenue derived from such land, income derived from the land by agriculture or the performance of any process to render the produce raised or received by the cultivator or receiver of rent-in-kind fit to be taken to market, and income derived from any building owned and occupied by the receiver of the rent or revenue of any such land or occupied by the cultivator or the receiver of rent-in-kind, of any land with respect to which, or the produce of which, any process mentioned in paragraphs (ii) and (iii) of sub-clause (b) is carried on.
* Amalgamation: The merger of one or more companies with another company or the merger of two or more companies to form one company, in such a manner that all the property of the amalgamating company or companies immediately before the amalgamation becomes the property of the amalgamated company by virtue of the amalgamation, all the liabilities of the amalgamating company or companies immediately before the amalgamation become the liabilities of the amalgamated company by virtue of the amalgamation, and shareholders holding not less than three-fourths in value of the shares in the amalgamating company or companies become shareholders of the amalgamated company by virtue of the amalgamation.
* Assessing Officer: The Assistant Commissioner or Deputy Commissioner or the Income-tax Officer who is vested with the relevant jurisdiction by virtue of directions or orders issued under sub-section (1) or sub-section (2) of section 120 or any other provision of this Act, and the Additional Commissioner or Additional Director or Joint Commissioner or Joint Director who is directed under clause (b) of sub-section (4) of that section to exercise or perform all or any of the powers and functions conferred on, or assigned to, an Assessing Officer under this Act.
* Assessment: The process of determining the tax liability of an assessee.
* Assessment year: The period of twelve months commencing on the 1st day of April every year.

The document also includes various schedules and forms that are relevant to the administration and enforcement of the Act. These include the First Schedule, which provides the rates of income-tax, the Fourth Schedule, which provides the rules for the approval of gratuity funds and superannuation funds, and various forms for the filing of returns, appeals, and other documents.

Summary:
The given document contains legal definitions related to Indian laws. Here are some of the key definitions:

1. Beneficial ownership: The document defines the previous year beneficially held by the government, a corporation established by a Central, State or Provincial Act, any company to which this clause applies or any subsidiary company of such company if the whole of the share capital of such subsidiary company has been held by the parent company or by its nominees throughout the previous year.
2. Co-operative society: A co-operative society is defined as a co-operative society registered under the Co-operative Societies Act, 1912 (2 of 1912), or under any other law for the time being in force in any State for the registration of co-operative societies.
3. Deputy Commissioner: A Deputy Commissioner is a person appointed to be a Deputy Commissioner of Income-tax under sub-section (1) of section 117 of the Income Tax Act, 1961.
4. Demerger: Demerger is defined as the transfer, pursuant to a scheme of arrangement under sections 391 to 394 of the Companies Act, 1956 (1 of 1956), by a demerged company of its one or more undertakings to any resulting company in such a manner that all the property of the undertaking, being transferred by the demerged company, immediately before the demerger, becomes the property of the resulting company by virtue of the demerger.
5. Director General or Director: A Director General or Director is a person appointed to be a Director General of Income-tax or a Principal Director General of Income-tax or, as the case may be, a Director of Income-tax or a Principal Director of Income-tax, under sub-section (1) of section 117 of the Income Tax Act, 1961.
6. Dividend: Dividend includes any distribution by a company of accumulated profits, whether capitalised or not, if such distribution entails the release by the company to its shareholders of all or any part of the assets of the company. It also includes any distribution to its shareholders by a company of debentures, debenture-stock, or deposit certificates in any form, whether with or without interest, and any distribution to its preference shareholders of shares by way of bonus, to the extent to which the company possesses accumulated profits, whether capitalised or not.

These definitions are crucial for understanding the provisions of various Indian laws related to taxation, companies, co-operative societies, and other legal matters.

* The term "accumulated profits" refers to all profits of a company up to the date of distribution or payment, and in the case of liquidation, up to the date of liquidation. However, it does not include any profits of the company prior to three successive previous years immediately preceding the previous year in which the acquisition took place if the liquidation is consequent on the compulsory acquisition of its undertaking by the government or a corporation owned or controlled by the government under any law for the time being in force.
* The term "concern" means a Hindu undivided family, or a firm or an association of persons or a body of individuals or a company. A person is deemed to have a substantial interest in a concern if he is beneficially entitled to not less than twenty per cent. of the income of such concern.
* The term "domestic company" means an Indian company or any other company which has made the prescribed arrangements for the declaration and payment, within India, of the dividends payable out of its income liable to tax under this Act.
* The term "document" includes an electronic record as defined in the Information Technology Act, 2000.
* The term "fair market value", in relation to a capital asset, means the price that the capital asset would ordinarily fetch on sale in the open market on the relevant date, and where the price is not ascertainable, such price as may be determined in accordance with the rules made under this Act.
* The term "firm" includes a limited liability partnership as defined in the Limited Liability Partnership Act, 2008.
* The term "foreign company" means a company which is not a domestic company.
* The term "income" includes profits and gains, dividend, the value of any perquisite or profit in lieu of salary, any special allowance or benefit specifically granted to meet expenses, any allowance granted to meet personal expenses or to compensate for the increased cost of living, the value of any benefit or perquisite obtained from a company, any sum chargeable to income-tax under various clauses of the Act, any capital gains chargeable under section 45, the profits and gains of any business of insurance or banking, winnings from lotteries, crossword puzzles, races, card games, and other games of any sort or from gambling or betting of any form or nature whatsoever, any sum received from employees as contributions to any provident fund or superannuation fund, any sum referred to in various clauses of the Act, and any compensation or other payment.
* The term "Income-tax Officer" means a person appointed to be an Income-tax Officer under section 117.
* The term "India" means the territory of India.

The document provided refers to various definitions related to Indian laws, specifically those mentioned in the Income Tax Act, 1961. Here are some key definitions:

* Territory of India: This refers to the territory of India as defined in article 1 of the Constitution, including its territorial waters, seabed, subsoil, continental shelf, exclusive economic zone, and any other maritime zone as defined in the Territorial Waters, Continental Shelf, Exclusive Economic Zone and other Maritime Zones Act, 1976. It also includes the air space above its territory and territorial waters.

* Inspector of Income-tax: This refers to a person appointed to be an Inspector of Income-tax under section 117 of the Income Tax Act, 1961.
* Interest: This refers to interest payable in any manner in respect of any moneys borrowed or debt incurred, including any service fee or other charge in respect of the moneys borrowed or debt incurred or in respect of any credit facility which has not been utilized.
* Interest on securities: This refers to interest on any security of the Central Government or a State Government, and interest on debentures or other securities for money issued by or on behalf of a local authority or a company or a corporation established by a Central, State or Provincial Act.

* Long-term capital asset and long-term capital gain: These refer to a capital asset which is not a short-term capital asset, and capital gain arising from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, respectively.

* Maximum marginal rate: This means the rate of income-tax (including surcharge on income-tax, if any) applicable in relation to the highest slab of income in the case of an individual, association of persons, or body of individuals as specified in the Finance Act of the relevant year.
* National Tax Tribunal: This refers to the National Tax Tribunal established under section 3 of the National Tax Tribunal Act, 2005.
* Person: This includes an individual, Hindu undivided family, company, firm, association of persons or body of individuals, whether incorporated or not, local authority, and every artificial juridical person, not falling within any of the preceding sub-clauses.
* Principal officer: This means the secretary, treasurer, manager or agent of the authority, company, association or body, or any person connected with the management or administration of the local authority, company, association or body upon whom the Assessing Officer has served a notice of his intention of treating him as the principal officer thereof.
* Profession: This includes vocation.
* Public sector company: This means any corporation established by or under any Central, State or Provincial Act or a Government company as defined in section 617 of the Companies Act, 1956.
* Public servant: This has the same meaning as in section 21 of the Indian Penal Code, 1860.
* Rate or rates in force: This refers to the rate or rates of income-tax specified in the Finance Act of the relevant year, and for the purposes of computation of the advance tax payable under Chapter XVII-C in a case falling under certain sections, the rate or rates specified in those sections or the rate or rates of income-tax specified in the Finance Act of the relevant year, whichever is applicable.

These definitions are important for understanding various provisions of the Income Tax Act, 1961, and other Indian laws.
Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India, 1961, defines various legal terms and concepts related to taxation. The term "assessment" refers to the determination of the total income of an assessee and the tax payable on that income. The Act differentiates between "regular assessment" and "best judgment assessment". Regular assessment is made under section 143(3) or section 144 of the Act, while best judgment assessment is made when the assessee fails to furnish a return of income. The term "assessee" refers to a person by whom any tax or any other sum of money is payable under the Act. "Capital gain" refers to the profit earned on the sale of a capital asset, and is classified as short-term or long-term based on the period of holding of the asset. The term "capital asset" includes property of any kind, whether movable or immovable, tangible or intangible, and includes options, rights, and interests in securities. The term "charitable purpose" refers to the promotion of family planning, rural development, or other objects of general public utility. "Deduction" refers to the amount allowed to be deducted from the total income of an assessee before arriving at the taxable income. "Income" includes profits and gains of any kind, whether earned or unearned, and includes income from business, profession, or employment. "Income-tax" refers to the tax levied under the Income Tax Act, 1961. "Principal Chief Commissioner or Chief Commissioner" or "Principal Commissioner or Commissioner" refers to the tax authorities responsible for administering and enforcing the provisions of the Act. "Recognised provident fund" refers to a provident fund recognized by the Principal Chief Commissioner or Chief Commissioner or Principal Commissioner or Commissioner in accordance with the rules contained in Part A of the Fourth Schedule of the Act, and includes a provident fund established under a scheme framed under the Employees’ Provident Funds Act, 1952. "Relative" in relation to an individual, means the husband, wife, brother or sister or any lineal ascendant or descendant of that individual. "Resulting company" means one or more companies (including a wholly owned subsidiary thereof) to which the undertaking of the demerged company is transferred in a demerger, and includes any authority or body or local authority or public sector company or a company established, constituted or formed as a result of demerger. "Resident" means a person who is resident in India within the meaning of section 6 of the Act. "Short-term capital asset" means a capital asset held by an assessee for not more than thirty-six months immediately preceding the date of its transfer. "Specified security" and "sweat equity shares" have the meanings assigned to them in the Explanation to clause (d) of sub-section (1) of section 115WB. "Undertaking" has the meaning assigned to it in Explanation 1 to clause (19AA). "Value" means the price actually paid or payable for the acquisition of a capital asset.
Summary:

This document pertains to the Income Tax Act, 1961, specifically discussing various definitions and provisions related to the charge of income tax, the scope of total income, and the residence of an individual in India.

1. Previous Year: The previous year is defined as the financial year immediately preceding the assessment year, except in the case of a newly set-up business or profession or a new source of income, where the previous year begins with the date of establishment or commencement of the source of income and ends with the financial year.
2. Charge of Income-tax: Income tax is charged for any assessment year at the specified rate or rates for the total income of the previous year, subject to the provisions of the Act. The tax is deducted at the source or paid in advance where it is deductible or payable under the Act.
3. Scope of Total Income:
a. For a resident individual, the total income includes income received or deemed to be received in India, income accrued or arisen or deemed to accrue or arise in India, and income accrued or arisen outside India if the individual is ordinarily resident and the income is derived from a business controlled in or a profession set up in India.
b. For a non-resident individual, the total income includes income received or deemed to be received in India and income accrued or arisen or deemed to accrue or arise in India.
4. Apportionment of Income between Spouses Governed by Portuguese Civil Code: In the case of spouses governed by the Portuguese Civil Code, their income under any head of income (excluding 'Salaries') is apportioned equally between them. The income so apportioned is included separately in their respective total income.
5. Residence in India: An individual is said to be a resident in India if they are in India for at least 182 days or more in a previous year or have been in India for a period or periods amounting to 365 days or more in the four years preceding that year and are in India for at least 60 days or more in that year. However, certain exceptions apply to this rule for individuals who are citizens of India or persons of Indian origin. They are considered residents if they are in India for at least 182 days or more in a previous year for the purpose of employment outside India or as a member of the crew of an Indian ship.
Summary:

The document to summarize pertains to the Income Tax Act of India, specifically focusing on the residency status of taxpayers and the income that is deemed to be received, dividend income, and income that is deemed to accrue or arise in India.

Under Section 6 of the Act, a Hindu undivided family, firm, or other association of persons is said to be a resident in India in any previous year if the control and management of its affairs is situated wholly in India. A company is said to be a resident in India if it is an Indian company or if its place of effective management is in India. Every other person is said to be a resident in India in any previous year if the control and management of his affairs is situated wholly in India.

Under Section 7, certain incomes are deemed to be received in the previous year, including the annual accretion in the previous year to the balance at the credit of an employee participating in a recognized provident fund, the transferred balance in a recognized provident fund, and the contribution made by the Central Government or any other employer to the account of an employee under a pension scheme.

Under Section 8, any dividend declared by a company or distributed or paid within the meaning of sub-clause (a) or sub-clause (b) or sub-clause (c) or sub-clause (d) or sub-clause (e) of clause (22) of section 2 shall be deemed to be the income of the previous year in which it is so declared, distributed, or paid.

Under Section 9, certain incomes are deemed to accrue or arise in India, including all income accruing or arising, whether directly or indirectly, through or from any business connection in India, through or from any property in India, through or from any asset or source of income in India, or through the transfer of a capital asset situate in India. The Explanation to this clause provides that in the case of a non-resident, no income shall be deemed to accrue or arise in India to him through or from operations which are confined to the purchase of goods in India for the purpose of export, or to a non-resident, being a person engaged in the business of running a news agency or of publishing newspapers, magazines or journals, no income shall be deemed to accrue or arise in India to him through or from activities which are confined to the collection of news and views in India for transmission out of India. The Explanation also provides that “business connection” shall include any business activity carried out through a person who, acting on behalf of the non-resident, has and habitually exercises in India, an authority to conclude contracts on behalf of the non-resident or habitually concludes contracts or habitually plays the principal role leading to the conclusion of contracts by that non-resident.

Explanation 2A clarifies that the significant economic presence of a non-resident in India shall constitute “business connection” in India and “significant economic presence” for this purpose, shall mean transaction in respect of any goods, services or property carried out by a non-resident in India including provision of download of data or software in India, if the aggregate of payments arising from such transaction or transactions during the previous year exceeds such amount as may be prescribed; or systematic and continuous soliciting of business activities or engaging in interaction with such number of users as may be prescribed, in India through digital means.

Explanation 5 clarifies that an asset or a capital asset being any share or interest in a company or entity registered or incorporated outside India shall be deemed to be and shall always be deemed to have been situated in India, if the share or interest derives, directly or indirectly, its value substantially from the assets located in India.

Summary:

The document outlines various provisions related to Indian tax laws. Specifically, it discusses the concept of "royalty" and "fees for technical services" under the Income Tax Act, 1961. The Act defines royalty as consideration for the transfer or use of patents, inventions, models, designs, secret formulas, processes, trade marks, or similar property, as well as for the imparting of any information concerning these items. The definition of fees for technical services includes any consideration for managerial, technical, or consultancy services, but excludes consideration for construction, assembly, mining, or similar projects and salaries.

The document also explains that certain types of income, such as pensions paid to individuals residing permanently outside India, may not be deemed to accrue or arise in India under certain circumstances. Additionally, the document provides an exception to the rule that a non-resident's income shall be deemed to accrue or arise in India under certain clauses of sub-section (1) of section 9.

Furthermore, the document includes provisions related to the taxation of eligible investment funds. Specifically, it states that the fund management activity carried out through an eligible fund manager acting on behalf of such fund shall not constitute business connection in India of the said fund. It also provides that an eligible investment fund shall not be said to be resident in India for the purpose of that section merely because the eligible fund manager, undertaking fund management activities on its behalf, is situated in India.

The eligible investment fund must meet certain conditions to qualify for this treatment, including being a resident of a country or specified territory with which an agreement has been entered into, having a minimum of twenty-five members, not investing more than 20% of its corpus in any entity, and not carrying on or controlling and managing any business in India. The eligible fund manager must also meet certain conditions, including being engaged in the activity of fund management and paying remuneration to the fund manager that is not less than the arm's length price of the said activity.

The provisions related to the taxation of eligible investment funds also include a provision that the conditions specified in clauses (e), (f) and (g) shall not apply in case of an investment fund set up by the Government or the Central Bank of a foreign State or a sovereign fund, or such other fund as the Central Government may specify.

Overall, the document provides detailed explanations and provisions related to various aspects of Indian tax laws, including the definitions of royalty and fees for technical services, the taxation of non-residents and eligible investment funds, and the conditions that must be met for certain types of income to be exempt from taxation in India.
Summary:

Section 10 of the Income Tax Act of India outlines the types of income that are not included in the total income of a person. Specifically, this section includes incomes such as agricultural income, certain sums received by an individual as a member of a Hindu undivided family, a partner's share in the total income of a separately assessed firm, income by way of interest on certain securities or bonds, income by way of interest on moneys in a Non-Resident (External) Account, income from interest on specified savings certificates, the value of travel concession or assistance, and remuneration received by certain individuals. The section includes various provisions and explanations related to these types of income. Additionally, the section includes a clause related to incomes of an eligible investment fund, which specifies conditions that must be met for such income to not be included in the total income. These conditions include the person not being an employee of the eligible investment fund or a connected person of the fund, the person being registered as a fund manager or an investment advisor, the person acting in the ordinary course of his business as a fund manager, and the person not being entitled to more than twenty per cent. of the profits accruing or arising to the eligible investment fund from the transactions carried out by the fund through the fund manager. The eligible investment fund is required to furnish a statement in the prescribed form to the prescribed income-tax authority containing information relating to the fulfilment of the conditions. The provisions of this section shall be applied in accordance with guidelines and in such manner as prescribed by the Board. The section also includes definitions of certain terms used in the section, such as "associate", "connected person", "corpus", and "entity".
Summary:
The Income Tax Act of India includes provisions for tax exemptions on certain types of income. This summary will focus on the sections related to income derived by foreign companies, non-residents, and the Indian government.

Section 10(6) covers income derived by foreign companies from royalty or fees for technical services received from the Indian government or an Indian concern. The tax on such income is payable by the Indian government or the Indian concern to the Central Government under the terms of the agreement.

Section 10(6A) covers income derived by non-residents or foreign companies from sources other than salary, royalty, or fees for technical services, received from the Indian government or an Indian concern. The tax on such income is payable by the Indian government or the Indian concern to the Central Government under the terms of the agreement or any other related agreement approved by the Central Government.

Section 10(6BB) covers income derived by the Government of a foreign State or a foreign enterprise from an Indian company engaged in the business of operating aircraft. The tax on such income is payable by the Indian company under the terms of the agreement to the Central Government.

Section 10(6C) covers income derived by a foreign company specified by the Central Government by way of royalty or fees for technical services received in pursuance of an agreement entered into with that Government for providing services in or outside India in projects connected with security of India.

Section 10(6D) covers income derived by a non-resident or a foreign company by way of royalty from, or fees for technical services rendered in or outside India to, the National Technical Research Organisation.

Section 10(7) covers allowances or perquisites paid or allowed as such outside India by the Government to a citizen of India for rendering service outside India.

Section 10(8) covers the remuneration received by an individual assigned to duties in India in connection with any cooperative technical assistance programmes and projects in accordance with an agreement entered into by the Central Government and the Government of a foreign State. It also covers any other income of such individual which accrues or arises outside India and is not deemed to accrue or arise in India, in respect of which such individual is required to pay any income or social security tax to the Government of that foreign State.

Section 10(8A) covers the remuneration or fee received by a consultant or any other income which accrues or arises to him or it outside India and is not deemed to accrue or arise in India, in respect of which such consultant is required to pay any income or social security tax to the Government of the country of his or its origin.

Section 10(8B) covers the remuneration received by an individual assigned to duties in India in connection with any technical assistance programme and project in accordance with an agreement entered into by the Central Government and the agency. It also covers any other income of such individual which accrues or arises outside India and is not deemed to accrue or arise in India, in respect of which such individual is required to pay any income or social security tax to the country of his origin.

Section 10(9) covers the income of any member of the family of any such individual as is referred to in clause (8) or clause (8A) or, as the case may be, clause (8B) accompanying him to India, which accrues or arises outside India, and is not deemed to accrue or arise in India, in respect of which such member is required to pay any income or social security tax to the Government of that foreign State or, as the case may be, country of origin of such member.

Section 10(10) covers death-cum-retirement gratuity received under the revised Pension Rules of the Central Government or, as the case may be, the Central Civil Services (Pension) Rules, 1972, or under any similar scheme applicable to the members of the civil services of the Union or holders of posts connected with defence or of civil posts under the Union or to the members of the all-India services or to the members of the civil services of a State or holders of civil posts under a State or to the employees of a local authority or any payment of retiring gratuity received under the Pension Code or Regulations applicable to the members of the defence services. It also covers any gratuity received under the Payment of Gratuity Act, 1972 (39 of 1972), to the extent it does not exceed an amount calculated in accordance with the provisions of sub-sections (2) and (3) of section 4 of that Act, and any other gratuity received by an employee on his retirement or on his becoming incapacitated prior to such retirement or on termination of his employment, or any gratuity received by his widow, children or dependants on his death, to the extent it does not, in either case, exceed one-half month’s salary for each year of completed service. The limit for exemption in this clause is specified by the Central Government.

Section 10(10A) covers any payment in commutation of pension received under the Civil Pensions (Commutation) Rules of the Central Government or under any similar scheme applicable to the members of the civil services of the Union or holders of posts connected with defence or of civil posts under the Union.

In this summary, the term “Central Government” refers to the government of India, and “State Government” refers to the government of a state in India. The term “foreign company” has the same meaning as in section 80B, and “royalty” and “fees for technical services” have the same meanings as in Explanation 2 to clause (vi) and clause (vii), respectively, of sub-section (1) of section 9. The term “non-resident” refers to a person who is not a resident in India. The term “international organisation” has the same meaning as in the International Organisations (Privileges and Immunities) Act, 1961. The term “agency” means an international organisation. The term “consultant” means any individual who is either not a citizen of India or, being a citizen of India, is not ordinarily resident in India, or any other person, being a non-resident, engaged by the agency for rendering technical services in India in connection with any technical assistance programme or project. Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India includes several provisions for tax exemptions for certain types of trusts, funds, and institutions. These exemptions are granted based on the nature and purpose of the organization, as well as how it uses its income and assets.

One type of exempt organization is a university or other educational institution that is substantially financed by the government. This means that if the government grant to such an institution exceeds a certain percentage of its total receipts (including any voluntary contributions), it will be considered as being substantially financed by the government and eligible for tax exemption.

Another type of exempt organization is a university or other educational institution that exists solely for educational purposes and not for profit, and has aggregate annual receipts that do not exceed a certain amount. Similarly, a hospital or other institution for the reception and treatment of persons suffering from illness or mental defectiveness, existing solely for philanthropic purposes and not for profit, and with aggregate annual receipts that do not exceed a certain amount, is also eligible for tax exemption.

In addition to these types of organizations, the Act also allows for tax exemptions for any other fund or institution established for charitable purposes, as well as for trusts or institutions wholly for public religious purposes or wholly for public religious and charitable purposes. These organizations must be approved by the prescribed authority, and must make an application in the prescribed form and manner for the purpose of grant of the exemption. The prescribed authority may also call for documents, information, or make inquiries as it deems necessary to satisfy itself about the genuineness of the activities of the organization.

Exempt organizations are required to apply their income wholly and exclusively to the objects for which they are established, and are generally prohibited from investing or depositing their funds in any assets other than those specifically allowed by the Act. Exemptions may also be granted for profits and gains of business, provided that the business is incidental to the attainment of the organization's objectives and separate books of account are maintained.

The Act also allows for the granting of tax exemptions for voluntary contributions to certain organizations, subject to certain conditions. These contributions must be held by the trust or institution in one or more of the forms or modes specified in the Act, and must not continue to be held after a certain period of time. Exemptions may also be denied in relation to any income of the fund or trust or institution, being profits and gains of business, unless the business is incidental to the attainment of its objectives and separate books of account are maintained in respect of such business.

The Taxation Laws (Amendment) Bill, 2006, has made certain changes to the provisions related to tax exemptions for trusts, funds, and institutions. For example, it has specified that every notification under sub-clause (iv) or sub-clause (v) shall be issued or approval under sub-clause (vi) or sub-clause (via) shall be granted or an order rejecting the application shall be passed within the period of twelve months from the end of the month in which such application was received. It also states that where the total income of the fund or trust or institution or any university or other educational institution or any hospital or other medical institution referred to in sub-clause (iv) or sub-clause (v) or sub-clause (vi) or sub-clause (via), without giving effect to the provisions of the said sub-clauses, exceeds the maximum amount which is not chargeable to tax in any previous year, such trust or institution or any university or other educational institution or any hospital or other medical institution shall get its accounts audited in respect of that year by an accountant as defined in the Explanation.

Any specified income of such Core Settlement Guarantee Fund is exempt from income tax, provided that any amount standing to the credit of the Fund and not charged to income-tax during any previous year is not shared with the specified person. If it is shared, the whole of the amount so shared shall be deemed to be the income of the previous year in which such amount is so shared and shall be chargeable to income-tax.

A venture capital company is defined as a company that has been granted a certificate of registration as a Venture Capital Fund under the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Venture Capital Funds) Regulations, 1996 or as a sub-category of Category I Alternative Investment Fund under the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Alternative Investment Funds) Regulations, 2012. A venture capital fund is defined as a fund operating under a trust deed registered under the Registration Act, 1908, which has been granted a certificate of registration as a Venture Capital Fund under the Venture Capital Funds Regulations or as a sub-category of Category I Alternative Investment Fund under the Alternative Investment Funds Regulations. The fund must invest at least two-thirds of its investible funds in unlisted equity shares or equity-linked instruments of venture capital undertakings, and must not invest in any venture capital undertaking in which its trustee or the settler holds more than 15% of the paid-up equity share capital. The units of the fund must not be listed in any recognized stock exchange.

A venture capital undertaking is defined as a venture capital undertaking as defined in the Venture Capital Funds Regulations or as defined in the Alternative Investment Funds Regulations.

Summary:

Section 10AA of the Income Tax Act of India, 1961, provides special provisions for deductions for newly established units in Special Economic Zones (SEZs). The deduction is available for a period of ten consecutive assessment years, starting from the year in which the unit begins to manufacture or produce articles or things or provide services. The unit must be located in a SEZ and must not have been formed by splitting up or reconstructing an existing business. Additionally, the unit must not have transferred its machinery or plant to a new business before the commencement of the SEZ Act, 2005.

The deduction is allowed for profits derived from the export of articles or things or services, including computer software. The export turnover is calculated as the consideration received in or brought into India for the export of articles or things or services, excluding freight, telecommunication charges, insurance, or expenses incurred in foreign exchange for rendering services outside India. The profits and gains derived from on-site development of computer software outside India are also considered as profits derived from the export of computer software.

If the unit is transferred before the expiry of the ten-year period to another unit in a scheme of amalgamation or demerger, no deduction is allowed for the amalgamating or demerged unit for the previous year in which the amalgamation or demerger takes place. The provisions of sections 10A(5) and (6) apply to the articles or things or services referred to in section 10AA as if the amalgamation or demerger had not taken place.

The loss referred to in section 72 or section 74, insofar as such loss relates to the business of the unit, is allowed to be carried forward or set off. The profits derived from the export of articles or things or services are calculated as the amount that bears to the profits of the business of the unit the same proportion as the export turnover in respect of such articles or things or services bears to the total turnover of the business carried on by the unit. The provisions of this section apply to any undertaking, being the unit, which fulfills the specified conditions.

No deduction is allowed for the assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2012, and subsequent years. The assessee may furnish a declaration in writing to the Assessing Officer that the provisions of this section may not be made applicable to him, in which case the provisions of this section shall not apply to him for any of the relevant assessment year.

Computer software means any computer programme recorded on any disc, tape, perforated media or other information storage device or any customized electronic data or any product or service of similar nature as may be notified by the Board, which is transmitted or exported from India to any place outside India by any means. Convertible foreign exchange means foreign exchange which is for the time being treated by the Reserve Bank of India as convertible foreign exchange for the purposes of the Foreign Exchange Management Act, 1999, and any rules made thereunder or any other corresponding law for the time being in force. Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India includes provisions for the tax-exempt status of certain income derived from property held for charitable or religious purposes. According to Section 11, income derived from property held under trust wholly for charitable or religious purposes is exempt from tax to the extent that it is applied to such purposes in India, and to the extent that any income is accumulated or set apart for application to such purposes in India, it is not to exceed 15% of the income from such property. Income derived from property held under trust in part for such purposes, created before the commencement of the Act, is also exempt to the extent that it is applied to such purposes in India, and to the extent that any income is finally set apart for application to such purposes in India, it is not to exceed 15% of the income from such property. Income derived from property held under trust created on or after April 1, 1952, for a charitable purpose which tends to promote international welfare in which India is interested, is exempt to the extent that it is applied to such purposes outside India. Income derived from property held under trust for charitable or religious purposes, created before April 1, 1952, is exempt to the extent that it is applied to such purposes outside India, provided that the Board has directed that it shall not be included in the total income of the person in receipt of such income. Income in the form of voluntary contributions made with a specific direction that they shall form part of the corpus of the trust or institution is also exempt. The amount of income that may be accumulated or set apart for application to charitable or religious purposes is subject to certain limitations and conditions. Capital gains arising from the transfer of a capital asset held for charitable or religious purposes may be deemed to have been applied to such purposes if the net consideration is utilized for acquiring another capital asset to be so held. Income that is not applied to charitable or religious purposes in India during the previous year but is accumulated or set apart for application to such purposes in India may be exempt from tax, provided certain conditions are met.

Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India has provisions related to the taxation of trusts and institutions that are established for charitable or religious purposes. Section 12 of the Act deals with the income of these trusts or institutions from contributions. Any voluntary contributions received by such a trust or institution, excluding those made with a specific direction to form part of the corpus, are deemed to be income derived from property held under trust for charitable or religious purposes. The provisions of section 11 and section 13 of the Act apply to this income.

Section 12(2) states that the value of any medical or educational services made available by a charitable or religious trust running a hospital or medical institution or an educational institution to certain specified persons shall be deemed to be income of the trust or institution. This income is chargeable to income tax for the previous year in which such services are provided, regardless of the provisions of section 11(1).

Section 12A lays down the conditions for the applicability of sections 11 and 12. The provisions of these sections do not apply to the income of a trust or institution unless certain conditions are met. These conditions include making an application for registration of the trust or institution in the prescribed form and manner to the Principal Commissioner or Commissioner before the 1st day of July, 1973, or before the expiry of a period of one year from the date of the creation of the trust or the establishment of the institution, whichever is later. The trust or institution must also be registered under section 12AA.

Section 12AA deals with the procedure for registration of a trust or institution. The Principal Commissioner or Commissioner, on receipt of an application for registration, may call for such documents or information from the trust or institution as he thinks necessary and may also make such inquiries as he may deem necessary. After satisfying himself about the objects of the trust or institution and the genuineness of its activities, he may pass an order in writing registering the trust or institution. If he is not so satisfied, he may pass an order in writing refusing to register the trust or institution. No order under sub-clause (ii) shall be passed unless the applicant has been given a reasonable opportunity of being heard.

Section 12AA(1A) states that all applications, pending before the Principal Chief Commissioner or Chief Commissioner on which no order has been passed before the 1st day of June, 1999, shall stand transferred on that day to the Principal Commissioner or Commissioner. The Principal Commissioner or Commissioner may proceed with such applications under that sub-section from the stage at which they were on that day.

Section 12AA(2) states that every order granting or refusing registration under clause (b) of sub-section (1) shall be passed before the expiry of six months from the end of the month in which the application was received.

Section 12AA(3) states that where a trust or an institution has been granted registration under clause (b) of sub-section (1) or has obtained registration at any time under section 12A, and subsequently the Principal Commissioner or Commissioner is satisfied that the activities of such trust or institution are not genuine or are not being carried out in accordance with the objects of the trust or institution, he may pass an order in writing cancelling the registration of such trust or institution. Provided that no order under this sub-section shall be passed unless such trust or institution has been given a reasonable opportunity of being heard.
Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India has several provisions related to the taxation of trusts and institutions. Section 11 of the Act outlines the conditions under which the income of a trust or institution is exempt from tax. However, Section 12 of the Act states that this exemption shall not apply if the activities of the trust or institution are being carried out in a manner that the provisions of sections 11 and 12 do not apply to exclude either whole or any part of the income due to operation of sub-section (1) of section 13.

Section 13 lists certain cases where the provisions of sections 11 and 12 shall not operate to exclude any part of the income from the total income of the person in receipt thereof. These cases include income from property held under a trust for private religious purposes which does not enure for the benefit of the public, income of a trust for charitable purposes or a charitable institution created or established after the commencement of the Act for the benefit of any particular religious community or caste, and income of a trust or institution if any part of such income or any property of the trust or the institution is during the previous year used or applied, directly or indirectly, for the benefit of any person referred to in sub-section (3).

Sub-section (3) of Section 13 lists the persons who are referred to in clause (c) of sub-section (1) and sub-section (2), including the author of the trust or the founder of the institution, any person who has made a substantial contribution to the trust or institution, a member of the family of the author or founder, any trustee or manager of the trust or institution, any relative of any such author, founder, person, member, trustee or manager, and any concern in which any of the persons referred to above has a substantial interest.

Furthermore, Section 12AA provides for the registration of trusts and institutions for the purpose of availing the tax exemption under Section 11. However, if it is noticed that the activities of the trust or institution are being carried out in a manner that the provisions of sections 11 and 12 do not apply, then the registration of such trust or institution may be cancelled under Section 12AA(3). However, the registration shall not be cancelled if the trust or institution proves that there was a reasonable cause for the activities to be carried out in the said manner.

In summary, the Income Tax Act of India provides for the tax exemption of the income of trusts and institutions, subject to certain conditions and limitations. The Act also provides for the registration and cancellation of registration of trusts and institutions for the purpose of availing the tax exemption. The Act lists certain cases where the tax exemption shall not apply, including cases where the income or property of the trust or institution is used or applied for the benefit of certain persons, including the author, founder, contributor, trustee, manager, relative, or any concern in which any of these persons has a substantial interest.

Summary:
The Indian Income Tax Act defines the term 'salary' to include various forms of remuneration such as wages, annuity, pension, gratuity, fees, commissions, perquisites, or profits in lieu of or in addition to any salary or wages. Salary also includes any advance of salary, payment received in respect of any period of leave not availed of by the employee, and the annual accretion to the balance at the credit of an employee participating in a recognized provident fund. The contribution made by the Central Government or any other employer to the account of an employee under a pension scheme is also considered salary. A 'perquisite' is defined to include the value of rent-free accommodation provided by the employer, any concession in the matter of rent respecting any accommodation provided by the employer, the value of any benefit or amenity granted or provided free of cost or at concessional rate, any sum paid by the employer in respect of any obligation which, but for such payment, would have been payable by the employee, and any sum payable by the employer, whether directly or through a fund, other than a recognized provident fund or an approved superannuation fund. The Income Tax Act also specifies deductions that can be made from the income chargeable under the head 'Salaries', including a deduction of fifty thousand rupees or the amount of the salary, whichever is less, and a deduction in respect of any allowance in the nature of an entertainment allowance specifically granted by an employer to the assessee who is in receipt of a salary from the Government.

Summary:

The Income Tax Act, 1961, is a comprehensive law that governs the taxation of income in India. This document provides a summary of certain sections of the Act related to the taxation of income from house property, profits and gains of business or profession, and the definition of certain legal terms.

Section 22 to 26 of the Act deal with the taxation of income from house property. The annual value of a property is determined based on the rent received or receivable, and certain deductions are allowed, such as a standard deduction of 30% and interest paid on borrowed capital. However, the amount of deduction is limited to Rs. 30,000 in respect of properties referred to in sub-section (2) of section 23. Where the property has been acquired or constructed with borrowed capital, the interest payable on such capital for the period prior to the previous year in which the property has been acquired or constructed is deducted in equal instalments for the previous year and for each of the four immediately succeeding previous years.

Section 25 provides that any interest chargeable under the Act, which is payable outside India, on which tax has not been paid or deducted under Chapter XVII-B, and in respect of which there is no person in India who may be treated as an agent under section 163, shall not be deducted in computing the income chargeable under the head “Income from house property”.

Section 25A deals with the taxation of arrears of rent and unrealised rent received subsequently. The amount of arrears of rent received from a tenant or the unrealised rent realised subsequently from a tenant is deemed to be the income from house property in respect of the financial year in which such rent is received or realised. A sum equal to 30% of the arrears of rent or the unrealised rent is allowed as deduction.

Section 26 provides that where property consisting of buildings or buildings and lands appurtenant thereto is owned by two or more persons and their respective shares are definite and ascertainable, such persons shall not in respect of such property be assessed as an association of persons, but the share of each such person in the income from the property as computed in accordance with sections 22 to 25 shall be included in his total income.

Section 27 defines certain legal terms used in sections 22 to 26. An individual who transfers otherwise than for adequate consideration any house property to his or her spouse, not being a transfer in connection with an agreement to live apart, or to a minor child not being a married daughter, is deemed to be the owner of the house property so transferred. The holder of an impartible estate is deemed to be the individual owner of all the properties comprised in the estate. A member of a co-operative society, company, or other association of persons to whom a building or part thereof is allotted or leased under a house building scheme of the society, company, or association is deemed to be the owner of that building or part thereof.

Section 28 deals with the taxation of profits and gains of business or profession. The profits and gains of any business or profession which was carried on by the assessee at any time during the previous year are chargeable to income-tax under the head “Profits and gains of business or profession”. Any compensation or other payment due to or received by a person, at or in connection with the termination of his management or the modification of the terms and conditions relating thereto, is also chargeable to income-tax under this head.

Section 44AD provides for a presumptive taxation scheme for small taxpayers engaged in a business. The section allows the assessee to declare a profit of 8% or 6% of the total turnover or gross receipts, as the case may be, subject to certain conditions. The section is applicable to resident individuals, Hindu undivided families (HUFs), and partnership firms (other than limited liability partnership firms) whose total turnover or gross receipts do not exceed Rs. 2 crores in a financial year.

Section 44ADA provides for a presumptive taxation scheme for professionals. The section allows the assessee to declare a profit of 50% of the total gross receipts, subject to certain conditions. The section is applicable to resident individuals, Hindu undivided families (HUFs), and partnership firms (other than limited liability partnership firms) who are engaged in a profession and whose total gross receipts do not exceed Rs. 50 lakhs in a financial year.

Section 44AE provides for a presumptive taxation scheme for taxpayers engaged in the business of plying, hiring, or leasing goods carriages. The section allows the assessee to declare a profit of 8% of the total turnover or gross receipts, subject to certain conditions. The section is applicable to resident individuals, Hindu undivided families (HUFs), and partnership firms (other than limited liability partnership firms) whose total turnover or gross receipts do not exceed Rs. 40 lakhs in a financial year.

Section 44BB provides for a presumptive taxation scheme for non-resident taxpayers engaged in the business of providing services or facilities in connection with the exploration or production of mineral oils. The section allows the assessee to declare a profit of 10% of the total turnover or gross receipts, subject to certain conditions.

Section 44BBB provides for a presumptive taxation scheme for non-resident taxpayers engaged in the business of operation of ships or aircraft. The section allows the assessee to declare a profit of 7.5% of the total turnover or gross receipts, subject to certain conditions.

Section 44B provides for a special provision for computing the profits and gains of business or profession in respect of certain specified services. The section provides that where any part of the income is earned by the assessee in India and the assessee is not required to keep and maintain accounts in India, the income earned in India shall be deemed to be the profits and gains of business or profession.

Section 44DB provides for a special provision for computing the profits and gains of business or profession in respect of a specified income. The section provides that where any part of the income is earned by the assessee in India and the assessee is not required to keep and maintain accounts in India, the income earned in India shall be deemed to be the profits and gains of business or profession.

Section 45 provides for the taxability of income from the transfer of certain capital assets. The section provides that where any capital asset is transferred, the income arising from the transfer shall be chargeable to income-tax under the head “Capital gains”. However, certain exemptions are provided, such as the transfer of a residential property for the purchase of another residential property.

Section 48 provides for the determination of the cost of acquisition and the cost of improvement of a capital asset. The section provides that the cost of acquisition shall be the price actually paid or payable for the acquisition of the asset. The cost of improvement shall be the amount actually paid or payable for any improvement of the asset.

Section 50C provides for the determination of the cost of acquisition of a capital asset in certain cases. The section provides that where the consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer of a capital asset is less than the fair market value of the asset, the cost of acquisition shall be deemed to be the fair market value of the asset.

Section 54 provides for the exemption of capital gains arising from the transfer of a residential property, subject to certain conditions. The section provides that where a residential property is transferred and the assessee purchases another residential property within a specified period, the capital gains arising from the transfer of the first property shall be exempt to the extent of the cost of the new property.

Section 54EC provides for the exemption of capital gains arising from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, subject to certain conditions. The section provides that where a long-term capital asset is transferred and the assessee invests the capital gains in certain specified bonds within a specified period, the capital gains shall be exempt to the extent of the amount invested.

Section 54F provides for the exemption of capital gains arising from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, subject to certain conditions. The section provides that where a long-term capital asset is transferred and the assessee purchases another long-term capital asset within a specified period, the capital gains arising from the transfer of the first property shall be exempt to the extent of the cost of the new property.

Section 54GB provides for the exemption of capital gains arising from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, subject to certain conditions. The section provides that where a long-term capital asset is transferred and the assessee invests the capital gains in equity shares of an eligible start-up within a specified period, the capital gains shall be exempt to the extent of the amount invested.

Section 56 provides for the taxability of income from other sources. The section provides that where any income is not chargeable to income-tax under any other head of income, it shall be chargeable to income-tax under the head “Income from other sources”.

Section 57 provides for the computation of income from other sources. The section provides that where any income is chargeable to income-tax under the head “Income from other sources”, the income shall be computed in accordance with the provisions of this section.

Section 58 provides for the deduction of certain sums from income from other sources. The section provides that where any income is chargeable to income-tax under the head “Income from other sources”, the assessee shall be entitled to deduct certain sums, as specified in this section.

Section 59 provides for the exclusion of certain sums from the total income. The section provides that where any income is chargeable to income-tax under the head “Income from other sources”, the assessee shall be entitled to exclude certain sums, as specified in this section, from the total income.

Section 60 provides for the inclusion of certain sums in the total income. The section provides that where any income is chargeable to income-tax under the head “Income from other sources”, the assessee shall be liable to include certain sums, as specified in this section, in the total income.

Section 61 provides for the computation of total income. The section provides that the total income of an assessee shall be computed in accordance with the provisions of this Act.

Section 64 provides for the charge of income-tax in the case of persons other than individuals, Hindu undivided families, and firms. The section provides that where the total income of any person, other than an individual, a Hindu undivided family, or a firm, is computed under this Act, the income-tax shall be charged at the rates applicable to the status of the assessee.

Section 68 provides for the burden of proof in the case of income not disclosed in the return. The section provides that where the income-tax authority has reason to believe that any income has escaped assessment, the onus of proving the correctness of the income returned or the correctness of the claim for any relief made in the return shall be on the assessee.

Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India outlines how to compute the income from profits and gains of business or profession. Section 28 defines the income, and Section 29 states that the income must be computed according to Sections 30 to 43D. This summary will focus on Sections 30 to 32.

Section 30 outlines the deductions allowed for rent, rates, taxes, repairs, and insurance for buildings used for business or professional purposes. If the premises are occupied by the assessee as a tenant, the rent paid and any amount paid for repairs can be deducted. If the premises are owned by the assessee, the amount paid for current repairs and insurance premiums can be deducted.

Section 31 outlines the deductions allowed for repairs and insurance of machinery, plant, or furniture used for business or professional purposes. The amount paid on account of current repairs and insurance premiums can be deducted.

Section 32 outlines the deductions allowed for depreciation of buildings, machinery, plant, or furniture, as well as intangible assets such as know-how, patents, copyrights, trade marks, licenses, franchises, or any other business or commercial rights of similar nature. The deductions allowed are based on the actual cost of the asset or the written down value, as prescribed. Certain provisions apply to specific assets, such as motor cars and machinery or plant acquired under an agreement with the Central Government. A deduction is not allowed for any motor car manufactured outside India acquired after February 28, 1975, but before April 1, 2001, unless it is used in a business of running it on hire for tourists or outside India in his business or profession in another country. Additionally, a deduction is not allowed for any machinery or plant if the actual cost thereof is allowed as a deduction in one or more years under an agreement entered into by the Central Government under Section 42.
Summary:

The document summarizes the provisions related to depreciation under the Income Tax Act of India. The Act allows for the deduction of depreciation on tangible and intangible assets used for business or profession. The depreciation rate varies depending on the type of asset and the year of acquisition. The Act also provides for investment allowance on certain specified assets. The deduction for depreciation is added to the income of the assessee if it cannot be fully utilized in the previous year due to lack of profits or gains. The Act also specifies the meaning of certain terms used in the section, such as "commercial vehicle," "know-how," and "assets." The Act applies whether or not the assessee has claimed the deduction in respect of depreciation in computing the income. The Act also provides for a higher deduction for assets acquired and installed in certain backward areas. The Act also specifies the assets that are not eligible for deduction, such as machinery or plant used by any other person before installation, machinery or plant installed in office premises or residential accommodation, office appliances or road transport vehicles, and machinery or plant whose actual cost is allowed as a deduction in computing the income chargeable under the head "Profits and gains of business or profession" of any one previous year. The Act also specifies the meaning of "moneys payable" and "sold" for the purposes of the section.
Summary:

The Investment Allowance Act of India provides for a deduction of 25% (or 20% for certain specified items) of the actual cost of a new ship, aircraft, machinery, or plant acquired or installed. This deduction is available for the assessment year relevant to the previous year in which the ship or aircraft is acquired or the machinery or plant is installed, or the immediately succeeding previous year. The section specifies certain conditions and exclusions, such as the exclusion of office appliances, road transport vehicles, and machinery or plant for which the full actual cost is allowed as a deduction in any one previous year. The Act also specifies that the ship or aircraft or machinery or plant must be new and must be used mainly for the purpose of business. Additionally, the section includes provisions for a higher deduction of 35% for certain specified industries and activities, such as the use of technology or other know-how developed in a laboratory owned or financed by the Government, a laboratory owned by a public sector company or a University, or an institution recognized in this behalf by the prescribed authority. The section also includes provisions for a higher deduction of 35% for machinery or plant installed for the purpose of controlling pollution or protecting the environment. The section also includes provisions for carrying forward and setting off any unabsorbed investment allowance.
Summary:

This document pertains to the Investment Allowance Act of India. The Act outlines the conditions and procedures for claiming investment allowance on the acquisition or installation of ships, aircraft, machinery, or plant. The investment allowance can be claimed as a deduction from the total income of the assessee for the assessment year in which the asset is acquired or installed. If the investment allowance is not sufficient to reduce the total income to nil, it can be carried forward to the following assessment year and so on, for a maximum of eight years. The Act also specifies the conditions that must be fulfilled to claim the deduction, including the creation of an Investment Allowance Reserve Account and the utilization of the amount credited to the reserve account for acquiring new assets. The document also provides provisions for the deemed wrongful making of the allowance in certain cases and the application of the provisions of sub-section (4A) of section 155. The Act also includes provisions for the sale or transfer of the ship, aircraft, machinery, or plant and the treatment of the balance of investment allowance in such cases.
Summary:
The Income Tax Act of India, 1961, includes various sections related to the taxation of profits and gains of business or profession. Section 32AB, titled "Investment deposit account," outlines the conditions under which an assessee can claim a deduction in the computation of their total income. The deduction is allowed if the assessee has deposited a certain amount in an account with the Development Bank or utilized the amount for the purchase of new ships, aircraft, machinery, or plant, in accordance with a scheme framed by the Central Government. The deduction is equal to the amount deposited or utilized, or 20% of the profits of the business or profession, whichever is less. However, the deduction is not allowed in relation to the assessment year commencing on the 1st day of April, 1991, or any subsequent assessment year. Additionally, the profits of the business or profession are calculated after deducting an amount equal to the depreciation computed in accordance with the provisions of sub-section (1) of section 32. Certain restrictions apply, such as the prohibition of deduction for the purchase of machinery or plant to be installed in office premises or residential accommodation, road transport vehicles, or any new machinery or plant to be installed in an industrial undertaking for the purpose of business of construction, manufacture or production of any article or thing specified in the list in the Eleventh Schedule. The deduction is also subject to the condition that the accounts of the business or profession of the assessee for the previous year relevant to the assessment year for which the deduction is claimed have been audited by an accountant as defined in the Explanation below sub-section (2) of section 288 and the assessee furnishes, along with his return of income, the report of such audit in the prescribed form duly signed and verified by such accountant. Any amount standing to the credit of the assessee in the deposit account shall not be allowed to be withdrawn before the expiry of a period of five years from the date of deposit except for specific purposes.

The deduction is available for a period of five years from the date of installation of the new plant or machinery. The deduction is equal to fifteen percent of the actual cost of the new plant or machinery. However, if the new plant or machinery is sold or otherwise transferred within a period of five years from the date of its installation, the amount of deduction allowed in respect of such new plant or machinery shall be deemed to be the income of the assessee chargeable under the head “Profits and gains of business or profession” of the previous year in which such new plant or machinery is sold or otherwise transferred.

The document summarizes the content of two sections of the Income Tax Act, 1961, specifically sections 32 and 33.

Section 32 outlines the provisions for investment allowance in certain backward areas. According to this section, if an assessee (a person or a company) invests in a new asset for the purpose of a business or enterprise located in a specified backward area in the states of Andhra Pradesh, Bihar, Telangana, or West Bengal, then they are entitled to a deduction of 15% of the actual cost of the new asset. This deduction is allowed for the assessment year relevant to the previous year in which the new asset is installed. However, if the new asset is sold or otherwise transferred within five years of its installation, the amount of deduction allowed under sub-section (1) in respect of such new asset will be deemed to be the income of the assessee for the previous year in which such new asset is sold or otherwise transferred, in addition to the taxability of gains arising on account of transfer of such new asset.

Section 33 deals with the provisions for development rebate. According to this section, if an assessee owns a new ship or new machinery or plant (other than office appliances or road transport vehicles) and uses it wholly for the purposes of their business, they are entitled to a deduction, known as development rebate. The amount of deduction varies depending on the type of asset and the year of installation. For instance, in the case of a ship, the deduction is 40% of the actual cost of the ship, while in the case of machinery or plant, the deduction ranges from 20% to 35% of the actual cost, depending on the type of machinery or plant and the year of installation. However, if the total income of the assessee assessable for the assessment year relevant to the previous year in which the ship was acquired or the machinery or plant installed or the immediately succeeding previous year, as the case may be, is less than the full amount of the development rebate, then the amount of the development rebate that cannot be allowed for that assessment year shall be carried forward to the following assessment year, and so on, for a maximum of eight assessment years immediately succeeding the assessment year relevant to the previous year in which the ship was acquired or the machinery or plant installed or the immediately succeeding previous year, as the case may be.
Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India contains provisions for deductions related to the planting of tea bushes on land owned by an assessee who carries on a business of growing and manufacturing tea in India. However, no deduction is allowed under clause (i) unless the planting has commenced after March 31, 1965, and been completed before April 1, 1990, and no deduction is allowed under clause (ii) unless the planting has commenced after March 31, 1965, and been completed before April 1, 1970. The total income of the assessee assessable for the assessment year relevant to the previous year in respect of which the deduction is required to be allowed under sub-section (1) is computed after deduction of the allowance under sub-section (1) or sub-section (1A) or clause (ii) of sub-section (2) of section 33, but without making any deduction under sub-section (1) of this section or any deduction under Chapter VI-A. If the total income is nil or less than the full amount of the development allowance, the sum to be allowed by way of development allowance for that assessment year under sub-section (1) shall be only such amount as is sufficient to reduce the said total income to nil, and the balance of the development allowance, to the extent to which it has not been allowed as aforesaid, shall be carried forward to the following assessment year. The deduction under sub-section (1) shall be allowed only if certain conditions are fulfilled, including the furnishing of prescribed particulars, the crediting of 75% of the development allowance to a reserve account to be utilized during a period of eight years next following for the purposes of the business of the undertaking, and such other conditions as may be prescribed.

The deduction is allowed if the business of any industrial undertaking carried on in India is discontinued in any previous year by reason of extensive damage to, or destruction of, any building, machinery, plant or furniture owned by the assessee and used for the purposes of such business as a direct result of certain specified causes.

It provides that depreciation allowance is allowed in respect of any asset owned by the assessee and used for the purposes of the business or profession. Development rebate is allowed in respect of any new machinery or plant installed and used for the purposes of the business or profession. The deduction is allowed at the prescribed rates and subject to the prescribed conditions.

Summary:

The Income Tax Act of 1961, specifically Section 35(1)(ii), allows for a deduction of capital expenditure incurred on scientific research. This deduction is allowed in equal installments over five years, starting from the year in which the expenditure is incurred. However, if the asset representing the expenditure ceases to be used for scientific research related to the business, a deduction is allowed for the deficiency in the previous year, and no deduction is allowed for subsequent previous years. If the asset is sold without being used for other purposes, the sale price is taken to be the value of the asset at the time of cessation. Additionally, if an asset is used in the business after it ceases to be used for scientific research related to that business, depreciation is admissible under Section 32(1)(ii).

Furthermore, under Section 35(2A), a deduction is allowed for a sum equal to one and one-third times the sum paid to a scientific research association, university, college, or other institution, or to a public sector company, to be used for scientific research undertaken under a programme approved by the prescribed authority. This deduction is not allowed under any other provision of the Income Tax Act.

Under Section 35(2AA), a deduction is allowed for a sum equal to one and one-half times the sum paid to a National Laboratory, university, Indian Institute of Technology, or specified person, with a specific direction that the said sum shall be used for scientific research undertaken under a programme approved by the prescribed authority. This deduction is not allowed under any other provision of the Income Tax Act.

Under Section 35(2AB), a company engaged in the business of bio-technology or in the business of manufacture or production of any article or thing, not being an article or thing specified in the list of the Eleventh Schedule, is allowed a deduction of a sum equal to one and one-half times the expenditure incurred on scientific research (not being expenditure in the nature of cost of any land or building) on in-house research and development facility as approved by the prescribed authority. This deduction is not allowed under any other provision of the Income Tax Act.

It is important to note that the prescribed authority must satisfy itself about the feasibility of carrying out the scientific research and submit its report to the Principal Chief Commissioner or Chief Commissioner or Principal Director General or Director General in such form as may be prescribed. Additionally, the deduction is not denied merely on the ground that the approval granted to the laboratory, or specified person has been withdrawn, or the programme undertaken by the National Laboratory, University, Indian Institute of Technology or specified person, has been withdrawn.
