Summary:
The Income Tax Act of India allows for the deduction of certain business expenses, including interest paid on capital borrowed for the purposes of the business or profession. However, there are certain restrictions on these deductions. For example, interest paid on capital borrowed for the acquisition of an asset cannot be deducted until the asset is first put to use. Additionally, recurring subscriptions paid periodically by shareholders or subscribers in Mutual Benefit Societies can be considered capital borrowed for the purpose of this clause. The pro rata amount of discount on a zero coupon bond is also considered capital borrowed, with the period of life of the bond calculated in a prescribed manner. Contributions made by an employer to a recognized provident fund or an approved superannuation fund, subject to certain limits and conditions, can also be deducted. Contributions made to a pension scheme on account of an employee, up to 10% of the employee's salary, can also be deducted. Bad debts or part thereof that are written off as irrecoverable in the accounts of the assessee can also be deducted, with certain limitations and conditions. A provision for bad and doubtful debts made by a scheduled bank, non-scheduled bank, public financial institution, State financial corporation, State industrial investment corporation, or non-banking financial company can also be deducted, up to a certain percentage of the total income. The Income Tax Act also includes definitions for various terms used in the act, such as "discount," "period of life of the bond," "salary," and "rural branch."

The document outlines various definitions related to Indian laws, specifically focusing on financial institutions and corporations. Here are some key definitions:

* A "subsidiary bank" is defined as a bank that is a subsidiary of the State Bank of India, a corresponding new bank constituted under certain sections of the Banking Companies (Acquisition and Transfer of Undertakings) Acts of 1970 and 1980, or any other bank included in the Second Schedule to the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934.
* A "public financial institution" is defined as a financial institution as defined in section 4A of the Companies Act, 1956.
* A "State financial corporation" is defined as a financial corporation established under section 3 or section 3A or an institution notified under section 46 of the State Financial Corporations Act, 1951.
* A "State industrial investment corporation" is defined as a Government company engaged in the business of providing long-term finance for industrial projects and eligible for deduction under clause (viii) of this sub-section.
* A "co-operative bank", "primary agricultural credit society", and "primary co-operative agricultural and rural development bank" have the meanings assigned to them in the Explanation to sub-section (4) of section 80P.
* A "non-banking financial company" has the meaning assigned to it in clause (f) of section 45-I of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934.
* A "specified entity" means a financial corporation, a financial corporation which is a public sector company, a banking company, a co-operative bank other than a primary agricultural credit society or a primary co-operative agricultural and rural development bank, a housing finance company, and any other financial corporation including a public company.
* "Eligible business" means the business of providing long-term finance for industrial or agricultural development, development of infrastructure facility in India, or development of housing in India.
* A "banking company" means a company to which the Banking Regulation Act, 1949 applies and includes any bank or banking institution referred to in section 51 of that Act.
* A "housing finance company" means a public company formed or registered in India with the main object of carrying on the business of providing long-term finance for the construction or purchase of houses in India for residential purposes.
* "Public company" has the meaning assigned to it in section 3 of the Companies Act, 1956.
* "Infrastructure facility" means an infrastructure facility as defined in the Explanation to clause (i) of sub-section (4) of section 80-IA, or any other public facility of a similar nature as may be notified by the Board in this behalf.

These definitions are important for understanding various provisions of Indian tax laws related to financial institutions and corporations.

Section 31 outlines the deductions allowed for depreciation of assets used for business purposes. It specifies the methods for calculating depreciation and the rates at which different types of assets can be depreciated.

Section 32 outlines the deductions allowed for capital expenditure incurred for the purpose of business or profession. It specifies the conditions under which these deductions can be claimed and the methods for calculating the amount of deduction.

Section 33 outlines the deductions allowed for scientific research expenditure. It specifies the conditions under which these deductions can be claimed and the methods for calculating the amount of deduction.

Section 34 outlines the conditions under which the amount of depreciation allowed as a deduction in any previous year can be carried forward and set off against the income of subsequent years.

Section 35 outlines the deductions allowed for expenditure on specified scientific research, rural development, and social welfare schemes. It specifies the conditions under which these deductions can be claimed and the methods for calculating the amount of deduction.

Section 36 outlines the deductions allowed for certain payments made to employees and other specified persons. It specifies the conditions under which these deductions can be claimed and the methods for calculating the amount of deduction.

Section 37 outlines the general rules for deduction of expenses incurred for the purpose of business or profession. It specifies the conditions under which these deductions can be claimed and the methods for calculating the amount of deduction.

Section 38 outlines the rules for deduction of expenses incurred on building, machinery, plant, or furniture which are not exclusively used for the purpose of business or profession. It specifies the conditions under which these deductions can be claimed and the methods for calculating the amount of deduction.

Section 40 outlines the amounts that shall not be deducted in computing the income chargeable under the head “Profits and gains of business or profession”. It specifies the conditions under which these deductions cannot be claimed and the methods for calculating the amount of deduction.

In particular, Section 40(a)(i) states that any interest, royalty, fees for technical services, or other sum chargeable under this Act, which is payable outside India or in India to a non-resident not being a company or to a foreign company, on which tax is deductible at source under Chapter XVII-B and such tax has not been deducted or, after deduction, has not been paid on or before the due date specified in sub-section (1) of section 139, shall not be deducted. However, if tax has been deducted in any subsequent year or has been deducted during the previous year but paid after the due date specified in sub-section (1) of section 139, such sum shall be allowed as a deduction in computing the income of the previous year in which such tax has been paid.

Similarly, Section 40(a)(ia) states that 30% of any sum payable to a resident, on which tax is deductible at source under Chapter XVII-B and such tax has not been deducted or, after deduction, has not been paid on or before the due date specified in sub-section (1) of section 139, shall not be deducted. However, if tax has been deducted in any subsequent year or has been deducted during the previous year but paid after the due date specified in sub-section (1) of section 139, 30% of such sum shall be allowed as a deduction in computing the income of the previous year in which such tax has been paid.

Section 40(a)(ib) states that any consideration paid or payable to a non-resident, not being a company or to a foreign company, in respect of any specified service, on which tax is deductible at source under Chapter XVII-B and such tax has not been deducted or, after deduction, has not been paid on or before the due date specified in sub-section (1) of section 139, shall not be deducted. However, if tax has been deducted in any subsequent year or has been deducted during the previous year but paid after the due date specified in sub-section (1) of section 139, such sum shall be allowed as a deduction in computing the income of the previous year in which such tax has been paid.

Section 40(a)(ii) states that any sum payable to a resident, on which tax is deductible at source under Chapter XVII-B and such tax has not been deducted or, after deduction, has not been paid on or before the due date specified in sub-section (1) of section 139, shall not be deducted. The document outlines various clauses related to the Income Tax Act of India, specifically focusing on the deductions allowed for certain payments. These deductions include:

* Any sum paid on account of equalisation levy under Chapter VIII of the Finance Act, 2016, provided that such levy has been deducted and paid on or before the due date.
* Any sum paid on account of fringe benefit tax under Chapter XIIH.
* Any sum paid on account of any rate or tax levied on the profits or gains of any business or profession, including any sum eligible for relief of tax under section 90 or 91.
* Any sum paid on account of wealth-tax, which includes any tax of a similar character chargeable under any law in force in any country outside India.
* Any amount paid by way of royalty, licence fee, service fee, privilege fee, service charge, or any other fee or charge, which is levied exclusively on, or appropriated directly or indirectly from, a State Government undertaking by the State Government.
* Any payment which is chargeable under the head “Salaries”, if it is payable outside India or to a non-resident and the tax has not been paid or deducted.
* In the case of any firm assessable as such, any payment of remuneration, bonus, commission, or remuneration to any partner who is not a working partner, or any payment of remuneration to any partner who is a working partner or of interest to any partner, which is not authorised by, or is not in accordance with, the terms of the partnership deed.
* In the case of an association of persons or body of individuals, any payment of interest, salary, bonus, commission, or remuneration made by such association or body to a member of such association or body.

The document also includes various explanations and provisos related to these clauses, which provide further clarification and detail on the deductions allowed.
Summary:

Section 37 of the Income Tax Act, 1961, provides for the deduction of any expenditure (not being in the nature of capital expenditure) incurred wholly and exclusively for the purpose of business or profession. However, certain exceptions apply, such as in the case of personal expenses, expenditure incurred for purposes not connected with the business or profession, or expenditure that is specifically disallowed under other provisions of the Act.

Section 40A of the Act lists certain expenses or payments that are not deductible in certain circumstances. These include expenditure that is excessive or unreasonable having regard to the fair market value of the goods, services, or facilities for which the payment is made, or the legitimate needs of the business or profession of the assessee or the benefit derived by or accruing to him therefrom. The section also disallows deductions for certain payments made to relatives or persons with a substantial interest in the business or profession of the assessee, as well as payments made in cash exceeding ten thousand rupees.

The document also includes provisions related to the deduction of provisions made for the payment of gratuity to employees, as well as deductions for sums paid towards the setting up or formation of, or as contributions to, certain funds, trusts, companies, associations, or institutions. However, such deductions are subject to specific conditions and limitations.

Overall, the document outlines various provisions related to the deduction of business or professional expenses under the Income Tax Act, 1961, and includes specific rules and exceptions that apply to different types of expenditure.
Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India, 1961, includes Section 41, which deals with profits chargeable to tax. This section outlines the rules for calculating profits or gains in cases where an allowance or deduction has been made in a previous year for a loss, expenditure, or trading liability. If the loss or expenditure is subsequently remitted or ceases, the amount obtained or the value of the benefit accrued is deemed to be profits and gains of business or profession and is chargeable to income tax as the income of that previous year. The same rule applies if the successor in business obtains any amount or benefit in respect of the loss or expenditure. The section also includes rules for calculating profits or gains when building, machinery, plant, or furniture is sold, discarded, demolished, or destroyed and the moneys payable, together with the scrap value, exceed the written down value. In such cases, the excess up to the difference between the actual cost and the written down value is chargeable to income tax as the income of the business of the previous year. Additionally, Section 41 includes rules for calculating profits or gains when a bad debt is recovered and when a special reserve created and maintained under clause (viii) of sub-section (1) of section 36 is withdrawn. Finally, the section includes a special provision for deductions in the case of business for prospecting, etc., for mineral oils.
Summary:

Section 43 of the Income Tax Act, 1961, provides definitions of certain terms relevant to income from profits and gains of business or profession. The section defines "actual cost" as the actual cost of the assets to the assessee, reduced by that portion of the cost thereof, if any, as has been met directly or indirectly by any other person or authority. However, there are certain provisions that modify the actual cost, such as:

* If the actual cost of a motor car exceeds twenty-five thousand rupees, the excess of the actual cost over such amount shall be ignored, and the actual cost thereof shall be taken to be twenty-five thousand rupees.
* If the assessee incurs any expenditure for acquisition of any asset or part thereof in respect of which a payment or aggregate of payments made to a person in a day, otherwise than by an account payee cheque drawn on a bank or an account payee bank draft or use of electronic clearing system through a bank account, exceeds ten thousand rupees, such expenditure shall be ignored for the purposes of determination of actual cost.

The section also includes provisions for the actual cost of an asset that is used for scientific research related to the business, is acquired by way of gift or inheritance, or is re-acquired by the assessee.

Section 44BB of the Income Tax Act, 1961, provides for the computation of profits and gains of business in certain cases. The section states that in the case of an assessee, being a non-resident, who is engaged in the business of exploration, etc., of mineral oils in India, the profits and gains of such business shall be computed in accordance with the provisions of this section. The section provides for the allowances that are admissible under this Act, such as expenditure by way of infructuous or abortive exploration expenses in respect of any area surrendered prior to the beginning of commercial production, expenditure incurred by the assessee in respect of drilling or exploration activities or services or in respect of physical assets used in that connection, and the depletion of mineral oil in the mining area in respect of the assessment year relevant to the previous year in which commercial production is begun and for such succeeding year or years as may be specified in the agreement.

Section 44BBB of the Income Tax Act, 1961, provides for the computation of profits and gains of business of a contractor in a block of land or a building in a block of land. The section states that in the case of an assessee, being a non-resident, who is engaged in the business of providing services or facilities in, or in relation to, a block of land or a building in a block of land, the profits and gains of such business shall be computed in accordance with the provisions of this section. The section provides for the allowances that are admissible under this Act, such as the actual cost of the assets to the assessee, reduced by that portion of the cost thereof, if any, as has been met directly or indirectly by any other person or authority. However, there are certain provisions that modify the actual cost, such as:

* If the actual cost of an asset, being a motor car which is acquired by the assessee after the 31st day of March, 1967, but before the 1st day of March, 1975, and is used otherwise than in a business of running it on hire for tourists, exceeds twenty-five thousand rupees, the excess of the actual cost over such amount shall be ignored, and the actual cost thereof shall be taken to be twenty-five thousand rupees.
* If the assessee incurs any expenditure for acquisition of any asset or part thereof in respect of which a payment or aggregate of payments made to a person in a day, otherwise than by an account payee cheque drawn on a bank or an account payee bank draft or use of electronic clearing system through a bank account, exceeds ten thousand rupees, such expenditure shall be ignored for the purposes of determination of actual cost.

The section also includes provisions for the actual cost of an asset that is used for scientific research related to the business, is acquired by way of gift or inheritance, or is re-acquired by the assessee.

In Section 44BBBA of the Income Tax Act, 1961, the Central Government has entered into an agreement with any person for the association or participation of the Central Government or any person authorized by it in such business. The section states that in the case of an assessee, being a non-resident, who has entered into an agreement with the Central Government for the association or participation of the Central Government or any person authorized by it in such business, the profits and gains of such business shall be computed in accordance with the provisions of this section. The section provides for the allowances that are admissible under this Act, such as expenditure by way of infructuous or abortive exploration expenses in respect of any area surrendered prior to the beginning of commercial production, expenditure incurred by the assessee in respect of drilling or exploration activities or services or in respect of physical assets used in that connection, and the depletion of mineral oil in the mining area in respect of the assessment year relevant to the previous year in which commercial production is begun and for such succeeding year or years as may be specified in the agreement. The section also states that such allowances shall be computed and made in the manner specified in the agreement, the other provisions of this Act being deemed for this purpose to have been modified to the extent necessary to give effect to the terms of the agreement.

In Section 44DA of the Income Tax Act, 1961, the business of the assessee consisting of the prospecting for or extraction or production of petroleum and natural gas is transferred wholly or partly or any interest in such business is transferred in accordance with the agreement referred to in sub-section (1), subject to the provisions of the said agreement and where the proceeds of the transfer (so far as they consist of capital sums) are less than the expenditure incurred remaining unallowed, a deduction equal to such expenditure remaining unallowed, as reduced by the proceeds of transfer, shall be allowed in respect of the previous year in which such business or interest, as the case may be, is transferred. If the proceeds of the transfer exceed the amount of the expenditure incurred remaining unallowed, so much of the excess as does not exceed the difference between the expenditure incurred in connection with the business or to obtain interest therein and the amount of such expenditure remaining unallowed, shall be chargeable to income-tax as profits and gains of the business in the previous year in which the business or interest therein, whether wholly or partly, had been transferred.

In Section 44DB of the Income Tax Act, 1961, where the business or interest in such business is transferred in a previous year in which such business carried on by the assessee is no longer in existence, the provisions of Section 44DA shall apply as if the business is in existence in that previous year. If the proceeds of the transfer are not less than the amount of the expenditure incurred remaining unallowed, no deduction for such expenditure shall be allowed in respect of the previous year in which the business or interest in such business is transferred or in respect of any subsequent year or years.

In Section 44DC of the Income Tax Act, 1961, where any asset which had once belonged to the assessee and had been used by him for the purposes of his business or profession and thereafter ceased to be his property by reason of transfer or otherwise, is re-acquired by him, the actual cost to the assessee shall be the actual cost to him when he first acquired the asset as reduced by the amount of any deduction allowed under clause (iv) of sub-section (1) of section 35 or under any corresponding provision of the Indian Income-tax Act, 1922 (11 of 1922). If the assessee re-acquires the asset by way of gift or inheritance, the actual cost of the asset to the assessee shall be the actual cost to the previous owner, as reduced by the amount of any deduction allowed under clause (iv) of sub-section (1) of section 35 or under any corresponding provision of the Indian Income-tax Act, 1922 (11 of 1922), in respect of any previous year relevant to the assessment year commencing before the 1st day of April, 1988 and the amount of depreciation that would have been allowable to the assessee for any assessment year commencing on or after the 1st day of April, 1988, as if the asset was the only asset in the relevant block of assets.

In Section 44DD of the Income Tax Act, 1961, where, before the date of acquisition by the assessee, the assets were at any time used by any other person for the purposes of his business or profession and the Assessing Officer is satisfied that the main purpose of the transfer of such assets, directly or indirectly to the assessee, was the reduction of a liability to income-tax (by claiming depreciation with reference to an enhanced cost), the actual cost to the assessee shall be such an amount as the Assessing Officer may, with the previous approval of the Joint Commissioner, determine having regard to all the circumstances of the case.

In Section 44DE of the Income Tax Act, 1961, where any asset which had once belonged to the assessee and had been used by him for the purposes of his business or profession and thereafter ceased to be his property by reason of transfer or otherwise, is re-acquired by him, the actual cost to the assessee shall be the actual cost to him when he first acquired the asset as reduced by the amount of any deduction allowed under clause (iv) of sub-section (1) of section 35 or under any corresponding provision of the Indian Income-tax Act, 1922 (11 of 1922). If the assessee re-acquires the asset by way of gift or inheritance, the actual cost of the asset to the assessee shall be the actual cost to the previous owner, as reduced by the amount of any deduction allowed under clause (iv) of sub-section (1) of section 35 or under any corresponding provision of the Indian Income-tax Act, 1922 (11 of 1922), in respect of any previous year relevant to the assessment year commencing before the 1st day of April, 1988 and the amount of depreciation that would have been allowable to the assessee for any assessment year commencing on or after the 1st day of April, 1988, as if the asset was the only asset in the relevant block of assets.

The document provides definitions for various terms related to Indian tax laws, specifically in relation to the Income Tax Act, 1961.

1. "Actual cost" refers to the actual cost of an asset to the assessee, as defined in various sections of the Income Tax Act, 1961. For example, if the asset is brought into use for the purpose of business or profession after February 28, 1946, the actual cost to the assessee is the actual cost of the building to the assessee, as reduced by an amount equal to the depreciation that would have been allowable had the building been used for the aforesaid purposes since the date of its acquisition by the assessee.
2. "Capital asset" refers to any kind of property held by an assessee, whether or not connected with his business or profession.
3. "Depreciation" refers to the wear and tear of an asset, and is allowable as a deduction under the Income Tax Act, 1961.
4. "Explanation 6" defines the actual cost of a capital asset transferred by a holding company to its subsidiary company or by a subsidiary company to its holding company.
5. "Explanation 7" defines the actual cost of a capital asset transferred by an amalgamating company to an amalgamated company.
6. "Explanation 7A" defines the actual cost of a capital asset transferred by a demerged company to the resulting company.
7. "Explanation 8" clarifies that interest paid in connection with the acquisition of an asset, relatable to any period after the asset is first put to use, shall not be included in the actual cost of the asset.
8. "Explanation 9" states that the actual cost of an asset acquired by an assessee on or after March 1, 1994, shall be reduced by the amount of duty of excise or the additional duty leviable under the Customs Tariff Act, 1975 in respect of which a claim of credit has been made and allowed.
9. "Explanation 10" defines the actual cost of an asset where a portion of the cost has been met by the Central Government or a State Government or any authority established under any law or by any other person in the form of a subsidy or grant or reimbursement.
10. "Explanation 11" defines the actual cost of an asset acquired by a non-resident assessee outside India and brought to India for use in his business or profession.
11. "Explanation 12" defines the actual cost of a capital asset acquired by an assessee under a scheme for corporatisation of a recognised stock exchange in India.
12. "Explanation 13" defines the actual cost of a capital asset on which deduction has been allowed or is allowable to the assessee under section 35AD.
13. "Paid" means actually paid or incurred according to the method of accounting upon which the profits or gains are computed under the head "Profits and gains of business or profession".
14. "Plant" includes ships, vehicles, books, scientific apparatus and surgical equipment used for the purposes of the business or profession, but does not include tea bushes or livestock or buildings or furniture and fittings.
15. "Scientific research" means any activities for the extension of knowledge in the fields of natural or applied science including agriculture, animal husbandry or fisheries.
16. "Speculative transaction" means a transaction in which a contract for the purchase or sale of any commodity, including stocks and shares, is periodically or ultimately settled otherwise than by the actual delivery or transfer of the commodity or scrips.
17. "Eligible transaction" means any transaction carried out electronically on screen-based systems through a stock broker or sub-broker or such other intermediary registered under section 12 of the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992 in accordance with the provisions of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 or the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992 or the Depositories Act, 1996 and the rules, regulations or bye-laws made or directions issued under those Acts or by banks or mutual funds on a recognised stock exchange, and supported by a time stamped contract note. This definition is relevant for the purpose of Explanation 1(d).
Summary:

The given text provides definitions and explanations related to various legal terms and concepts in the context of Indian laws. Here's a summary of the content:

1. Clause (e) defines "eligible transaction" as a transaction carried out electronically on screen-based systems for trading in commodity derivatives, supported by a time-stamped contract note with specific details, including a unique client identity number, unique trade number, and permanent account number.
2. "Recognised association" refers to a recognized association under the Forward Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1952, which fulfills certain conditions and is notified by the Central Government.
3. "Written down value" is defined in three parts: (a) for assets acquired in the previous year, it is the actual cost to the assessee; (b) for assets acquired before the previous year, it is the actual cost to the assessee less all depreciation allowed; and (c) for any block of assets, it is the aggregate of the written down values of all the assets in that block, adjusted for acquisitions, sales, discards, demolitions, or depreciation.
4. Explanation 1 states that in the case of succession in business or profession, the written down value of any asset or block of assets for the successor will be the amount that would have been taken as its written down value if the assessment had been made directly on the person succeeded to.
5. Explanation 2A and 2B deal with the written down value of blocks of assets in cases of demerger, where assets are transferred from a demerged company to the resulting company.
6. Explanation 2C explains the actual cost of the block of assets in the case of a limited liability partnership that has been converted from a private company or unlisted public company.
7. Explanation 3 deems any allowance in respect of depreciation carried forward under sub-section (2) of section 32 as depreciation "actually allowed."
8. Explanation 4 defines the terms "moneys payable" and "sold" for the purpose of this clause.
9. Explanation 5 deals with the written down value of the block of assets in the case of a company that has acquired assets from a recognized stock exchange in India under a scheme for corporatisation approved by the Securities and Exchange Board of India.
10. Explanation 6 provides rules for computing the written down value of assets when the income of an assessee is derived in part from agriculture and in part from business chargeable to income-tax under the head "Profits and gains of business or profession."

These definitions and explanations are crucial for understanding various provisions and calculations related to taxes, commodity trading, and asset depreciation under Indian laws.
The Income Tax Act of India, 1961, contains provisions related to the taxation of foreign exchange fluctuation. According to Section 43AA, any gain or loss arising on account of any change in foreign exchange rates shall be treated as income or loss, as the case may be, and such gain or loss shall be computed in accordance with the income computation and disclosure standards notified under sub-section (2) of section 145. This section applies to all foreign currency transactions, including monetary items, non-monetary items, translation of financial statements of foreign operations, forward exchange contracts, and foreign currency translation reserves.

Section 43B of the Income Tax Act of India, 1961, states that certain deductions are only allowed on actual payment. These deductions include taxes, duties, cess, or fees, contributions to provident funds or superannuation funds or gratuity funds or any other fund for the welfare of employees, interest on loans or borrowings from public financial institutions or State financial corporations or State industrial investment corporations, interest on loans or advances from scheduled banks or co-operative banks, sums payable by the assessee as an employer in lieu of any leave at the credit of his employees, and sums payable by the assessee to the Indian Railways for the use of railway assets. These deductions are allowed only in computing the income referred to in section 28 of the previous year in which such sum is actually paid by the assessee. However, if the assessee pays the sum on or before the due date applicable for furnishing the return of income, the deduction is allowed in computing the income of the previous year in which the liability to pay such sum was incurred.

Section 43A of the Income Tax Act of India, 1961, contains special provisions consequential to changes in the rate of exchange of currency. If an assessee has acquired any asset in a previous year from a country outside India for the purposes of his business or profession and there is an increase or reduction in the liability of the assessee as expressed in Indian currency (as compared to the liability existing at the time of acquisition of the asset) at the time of making payment towards the whole or a part of the cost of the asset or towards repayment of the whole or a part of the moneys borrowed by him from any person, directly or indirectly, in any foreign currency specifically for the purpose of acquiring the asset along with interest, if any, the amount by which the liability is so increased or reduced during such previous year and which is taken into account at the time of making the payment, irrespective of the method of accounting adopted by the assessee, shall be added to or deducted from the actual cost of the asset or the amount of expenditure of a capital nature or the cost of acquisition of the capital asset, as the case may be. The amount arrived at after such addition or deduction shall be taken to be the actual cost of the asset or the amount of expenditure of a capital nature or the cost of acquisition of the capital asset. However, if an addition to or deduction from the actual cost or expenditure or cost of acquisition has been made under this section, as it stood immediately before its substitution by the Finance Act, 2002, on account of an increase or reduction in the liability, the amount to be added to or deducted from the actual cost or expenditure or cost of acquisition at the time of making the payment shall be so adjusted that the total amount added to or deducted from the actual cost or expenditure or cost of acquisition is equal to the increase or reduction in the liability taken into account at the time of making payment.

The Income Tax Act of India, 1961, also contains provisions related to depreciation. According to Section 32, the allowance for depreciation is computed with reference to the actual cost of the asset as reduced by the amount of any scrap value and is allowed in accordance with and subject to the provisions of this section. The block of assets is a group of assets falling within a class of assets, being a class of depreciable assets, owned, controlled or used by the assessee and used for the purposes of the business or profession. The written down value of a block of assets shall be computed as the actual cost of the assets in the block after deducting therefrom the amount of depreciation allowable under this section in respect of each of the previous years. The written down value of a block of assets shall, in respect of any previous year, be the aggregate of the written down values of the assets in the block as computed in the manner provided in this sub-section. The written down value of a block of assets shall not exceed the actual cost of the block of assets.

Section 32(1)(ii) of the Income Tax Act of India, 1961, states that in respect of any block of assets, the allowance for depreciation under this section shall be at the rate of 15% of the actual cost of the assets in the block for the previous year or, at the option of the assessee, at the rate of 15% of the written down value of the assets in the block at the beginning of the previous year, whichever is higher. However, in the case of a new plant or machinery installed after the 31st day of March, 1998, the allowance for depreciation shall be at the rate of 25% of the actual cost of the new plant or machinery for the previous year or, at the option of the assessee, at the rate of 25% of the written down value of the new plant or machinery at the beginning of the previous year, whichever is higher. The allowance for depreciation in respect of any block of assets shall be computed with reference to the actual cost of the assets in the block as reduced by the amount of any scrap value. The written down value of a block of assets shall be computed as the actual cost of the assets in the block after deducting therefrom the amount of depreciation allowable under this section in respect of each of the previous years. The written down value of a block of assets shall, in respect of any previous year, be the aggregate of the written down values of the assets in the block as computed in the manner provided in this sub-section. The written down value of a block of assets shall not exceed the actual cost of the block of assets.

In summary, the Income Tax Act of India, 1961, contains provisions related to the taxation of foreign exchange fluctuation, deductions allowed only on actual payment, and depreciation. Section 43AA states that any gain or loss arising on account of any change in foreign exchange rates shall be treated as income or loss, as the case may be, and such gain or loss shall be computed in accordance with the income computation and disclosure standards notified under sub-section (2) of section 145. Section 43B states that certain deductions are only allowed on actual payment, while Section 32 contains provisions related to depreciation. The allowance for depreciation is computed with reference to the actual cost of the asset as reduced by the amount of any scrap value and is allowed in accordance with and subject to the provisions of this section. The block of assets is a group of assets falling within a class of assets, being a class of depreciable assets, owned, controlled or used by the assessee and used for the purposes of the business or profession. The written down value of a block of assets shall be computed as the actual cost of the assets in the block after deducting therefrom the amount of depreciation allowable under this section in respect of each of the previous years. The written down value of a block of assets shall not exceed the actual cost of the block of assets.
Summary:

This summary explains several Indian laws related to taxation and financial transactions.

Section 43C deals with the computation of cost of acquisition of certain assets. If an asset becomes the property of an amalgamated company under a scheme of amalgamation and is sold as stock-in-trade, the cost of acquisition of the said asset to the amalgamated company is the cost of acquisition of the said asset to the amalgamating company, as increased by the cost of any improvement made and the expenditure incurred in connection with such transfer. Similarly, if an asset becomes the property of the assessee on the total or partial partition of a Hindu undivided family or under a gift or will or an irrevocable trust, the cost of acquisition of the said asset to the assessee is the cost of acquisition of the said asset to the transferor or the donor, as the case may be, as increased by the cost of any improvement made and the expenditure incurred in connection with such transfer.

Section 43CA states that where the consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer by an assessee of an asset (other than a capital asset), being land or building or both, is less than the value adopted or assessed or assessable by any authority of a State Government for the purpose of payment of stamp duty in respect of such transfer, the value so adopted or assessed or assessable shall be deemed to be the full value of the consideration received or accruing as a result of such transfer.

Section 43CB deals with the computation of income from construction and service contracts. The profits and gains arising from a construction contract or a contract for providing services shall be determined on the basis of percentage of completion method in accordance with the income computation and disclosure standards notified under sub-section (2) of section 145.

Section 43D deals with the taxation of income of public financial institutions, public companies, etc. The income by way of interest in relation to certain categories of bad or doubtful debts is chargeable to tax in the previous year in which it is credited by the public financial institution or the scheduled bank or a co-operative bank or the State financial corporation or the State industrial investment corporation or the public company to its profit and loss account for that year or, as the case may be, in which it is actually received by that institution or bank or corporation or company, whichever is earlier.

Section 44 of the Income Tax Act, 1961 deals with the computation of profits and gains of insurance business. The profits and gains of any business of insurance, including any such business carried on by a mutual insurance company or by a co-operative society, shall be computed in accordance with the rules contained in the First Schedule.

Section 44A provides for a special provision for deduction in the case of trade, professional or similar association. Where the amount received during a previous year by any trade, professional or similar association from its members falls short of the expenditure incurred solely for the purposes of protection or advancement of the common interests of its members, the amount so fallen short shall be allowed as a deduction in computing the income of the association assessable for the relevant assessment year under the head "Profits and gains of business or profession" and if there is no income assessable under that head or the deficiency allowable exceeds such income, the whole or the balance of the deficiency, as the case may be, shall be allowed as a deduction in computing the income of the association assessable for the relevant assessment year under any other head.

Section 44AA deals with the maintenance of accounts by certain persons carrying on profession or business. Every person carrying on legal, medical, engineering or architectural profession or the profession of accountancy or technical consultancy or interior decoration or any other profession as is notified by the Board in the Official Gazette shall keep and maintain such books of account and other documents as may enable the Assessing Officer to compute his total income in accordance with the provisions of this Act. Every person carrying on business or profession not being a profession referred to in sub-section (1) shall, if his income from business or profession exceeds one lakh twenty thousand rupees or his total sales, turnover or gross receipts, as the case may be, in business or profession exceed or exceeds ten lakh rupees in any one of the three years immediately preceding the previous year or where the business or profession is newly set up in any previous year, if his income from business or profession is likely to exceed one lakh twenty thousand rupees or his total sales, turnover or gross receipts, as the case may be, in business or profession are or is likely to exceed ten lakh rupees during such previous year or where the profits and gains from the business are deemed to be the profits and gains of the assessee under section 44AE or section 44BB or section 44BBB, as the case may be, and the assessee has claimed his income to be lower than the profits or gains so deemed to be the profits and gains of his business, as the case may be, during such previous year or where the provisions of sub-section (4) of section 44AD are applicable in his case and his income exceeds the maximum amount which is not chargeable to income-tax in any previous year, keep and maintain such books of account and other documents as may enable the Assessing Officer to compute his total income in accordance with the provisions of this Act.

Section 44AB provides for the audit of accounts of certain persons carrying on business or profession. Every person carrying on business shall, if his total sales, turnover or gross receipts, as the case may be, in business exceed or exceeds one crore rupees in any previous year or carrying on profession shall, if his gross receipts in profession exceed fifty lakh rupees in any previous year or carrying on the business shall, if the profits and gains from the business are deemed to be the profits and gains of such person under section 44AE or section 44BB or section 44BBB, as the case may be, and he has claimed his income to be lower than the profits or gains so deemed to be the profits and gains of his business, as the case may be, in any previous year or carrying on the profession shall, if the profits and gains from the profession are deemed to be the profits and gains of such person under section 44ADA and he has claimed such income to be lower than the profits and gains so deemed to be the profits and gains of his profession and his income exceeds the maximum amount which is not chargeable to income-tax in any previous year or carrying on the business shall, if the provisions of sub-section (4) of section 44AD are applicable in his case and his income exceeds the maximum amount which is not chargeable to income-tax in any previous year, get his accounts of such previous year audited by an accountant before the specified date and furnish by that date the report of such audit in the prescribed form duly signed and verified by such accountant and setting forth such particulars as may be prescribed.

Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India contains several sections related to the computation of profits and gains from business or profession. This summary will focus on Sections 44AC, 44AD, 44ADA, and 44AE.

Section 44AC, which has been omitted, used to provide a special provision for computing profits and gains from the business of trading in certain goods.

Section 44AD lays down special provisions for computing profits and gains of business on a presumptive basis. If an eligible assessee engaged in an eligible business claims that the profits and gains from the business are equal to eight percent of the total turnover or gross receipts, then such profits and gains shall be deemed to be the profits and gains of such business chargeable to tax. However, if the eligible assessee receives any amount by an account payee cheque or an account payee bank draft or use of electronic clearing system through a bank account, then the rate of eight percent is reduced to six percent. Any deduction allowable under sections 30 to 38 shall be deemed to have been already given full effect to, and no further deduction under those sections shall be allowed.

Section 44ADA provides for the computation of profits and gains of profession on a presumptive basis. If an assessee, being a resident in India, is engaged in a profession referred to in sub-section (1) of section 44AA and whose total gross receipts do not exceed fifty lakh rupees in a previous year, then a sum equal to fifty per cent. of the total gross receipts of the assessee in the previous year on account of such profession shall be deemed to be the profits and gains of such profession chargeable to tax. Any deduction allowable under sections 30 to 38 shall be deemed to have been already given full effect to and no further deduction under those sections shall be allowed.

Section 44AE provides for the computation of profits and gains of business of plying, hiring or leasing goods carriages. If an assessee owns not more than ten goods carriages at any time during the previous year and is engaged in the business of plying, hiring or leasing such goods carriages, then the income of such business chargeable to tax shall be deemed to be the aggregate of the profits and gains from all the goods carriages owned by him in the previous year, computed in accordance with the provisions of sub-section (2). Any deduction allowable under sections 30 to 38 shall be deemed to have been already given full effect to and no further deduction under those sections shall be allowed. The written down value of any asset used for the purpose of the business shall be deemed to have been calculated as if the assessee had claimed and had been actually allowed the deduction in respect of the depreciation for each of the relevant assessment years. The provisions of sections 44AA and 44AB shall not apply in so far as they relate to the business referred to in sub-section (1).

In summary, these sections of the Income Tax Act of India provide for the computation of profits and gains from business or profession on a presumptive basis, with certain conditions and limitations. These provisions aim to simplify the tax compliance process for small businesses and professionals.
Summary:

Section 44AF of the Income Tax Act, 1961, deals with special provisions for computing profits and gains of retail business. According to this section, a sum equal to 5% of the total turnover in the previous year on account of such business or a sum higher than the aforesaid sum as declared by the assessee in his return of income shall be deemed to be the profits and gains of such business chargeable to tax under the head "Profits and gains of business or profession". However, this provision does not apply if the total turnover exceeds an amount of forty lakh rupees in the previous year. Any deduction allowable under sections 30 to 38 shall be deemed to have been already given full effect to and no further deduction under those sections shall be allowed. The written down value of any asset used for the purpose of the business referred to in sub-section (1) shall be deemed to have been calculated as if the assessee had claimed and had been actually allowed the deduction in respect of the depreciation for each of the relevant assessment years. The provisions of sections 44AA and 44AB shall not apply in so far as they relate to the business referred to in sub-section (1) and in computing the monetary limits under those sections, the total turnover or, as the case may be, the income from the said business shall be excluded. An assessee may claim lower profits and gains than the profits and gains specified in sub-section (1) if he keeps and maintains such books of account and other documents as required under sub-section (2) of section 44AA and gets his accounts audited and furnishes a report of such audit as required under section 44AB. This section shall not apply to any assessment year beginning on or after the 1st day of April, 2011.

Section 44B of the Income Tax Act, 1961, deals with special provision for computing profits and gains of shipping business in the case of non-residents. According to this section, a sum equal to 7.5% of the aggregate of the amounts specified in sub-section (2) shall be deemed to be the profits and gains of such business chargeable to tax under the head “Profits and gains of business or profession”. The amounts referred to in sub-section (1) shall be the following, namely: (i) the amount paid or payable (whether in or out of India) to the assessee or to any person on his behalf on account of the carriage of passengers, livestock, mail or goods shipped at any port in India; and (ii) the amount received or deemed to be received in India by or on behalf of the assessee on account of the carriage of passengers, livestock, mail or goods shipped at any port outside India. The explanation to this section provides that the amount referred to in clause (i) or clause (ii) shall include the amount paid or payable or received or deemed to be received, as the case may be, by way of demurrage charges or handling charges or any other amount of similar nature.

Section 44BB of the Income Tax Act, 1961, deals with special provision for computing profits and gains in connection with the business of exploration, etc., of mineral oils. According to this section, a sum equal to 10% of the aggregate of the amounts specified in sub-section (2) shall be deemed to be the profits and gains of such business chargeable to tax under the head “Profits and gains of business or profession”. The amounts referred to in sub-section (1) shall be the following, namely: (a) the amount paid or payable (whether in or out of India) to the assessee or to any person on his behalf on account of the provision of services and facilities in connection with, or supply of plant and machinery on hire used, or to be used, in the prospecting for, or extraction or production of, mineral oils in India; and (b) the amount received or deemed to be received in India by or on behalf of the assessee on account of the provision of services and facilities in connection with, or supply of plant and machinery on hire used, or to be used, in the prospecting for, or extraction or production of, mineral oils outside India. The explanation to this section provides that the amount referred to in clause (i) or clause (ii) shall include the amount paid or payable or received or deemed to be received, as the case may be, by way of demurrage charges or handling charges or any other amount of similar nature. An assessee may claim lower profits and gains than the profits and gains specified in that sub-section, if he keeps and maintains such books of account and other documents as required under sub-section (2) of section 44AA and gets his accounts audited and furnishes a report of such audit as required under section 44AB.

Section 44BBA of the Income Tax Act, 1961, deals with special provision for computing profits and gains of the business of operation of aircraft in the case of non-residents. According to this section, a sum equal to 5% of the aggregate of the amounts specified in sub-section (2) shall be deemed to be the profits and gains of such business chargeable to tax under the head “Profits and gains of business or profession”. The amounts referred to in sub-section (1) shall be the following, namely: (a) the amount paid or payable (whether in or out of India) to the assessee or to any person on his behalf on account of the carriage of passengers, livestock, mail or goods from any place in India; and (b) the amount received or deemed to be received in India by or on behalf of the assessee on account of the carriage of passengers, livestock, mail or goods from any place outside India.

Section 44BBB of the Income Tax Act, 1961, deals with special provision for computing profits and gains of foreign companies engaged in the business of civil construction, etc., in certain turnkey power projects. According to this section, a sum equal to 10% of the amount paid or payable (whether in or out of India) to the said assessee or to any person on his behalf on account of such civil construction, erection, testing or commissioning shall be deemed to be the profits and gains of such business chargeable to tax under the head “Profits and gains of business or profession”. An assessee may claim lower profits and gains than the profits and gains specified in that sub-section, if he keeps and maintains such books of account and other documents as required under sub-section (2) of section 44AA and gets his accounts audited and furnishes a report of such audit as required under section 44AB.
Summary:

Section 44AB of the Income Tax Act in India requires a person carrying on a business to get their accounts audited if their total sales, turnover, or gross receipts exceed a certain threshold. If such an audit is conducted and a report is submitted, the Assessing Officer will proceed to make an assessment of the total income or loss of the assessee.

Section 44C of the Act deals with the deduction of head office expenditure in the case of non-residents. It specifies that no allowance shall be made in respect of head office expenditure in excess of a certain percentage of the adjusted total income or the amount attributable to the business or profession of the assessee in India, whichever is least.

Section 44D of the Act contains special provisions for computing income by way of royalties, etc., in the case of foreign companies. It specifies that the deductions admissible under certain sections in computing the income by way of royalty or fees for technical services received from Government or an Indian concern in pursuance of an agreement made by the foreign company with Government or with the Indian concern before the 1st day of April, 1976, shall not exceed in the aggregate twenty per cent of the gross amount of such royalty or fees. No deduction in respect of any expenditure or allowance shall be allowed under any of the said sections in computing the income by way of royalty or fees for technical services received from Government or an Indian concern after the 31st day of March, 1976 but before the 1st day of April, 2003.

Section 44DA of the Act contains provisions for computing income by way of royalty or fees for technical services received from Government or an Indian concern in pursuance of an agreement made by a non-resident (not being a company) or a foreign company with Government or the Indian concern after the 31st day of March, 2003. The income shall be computed under the head “Profits and gains of business or profession” in accordance with the provisions of this Act. Every non-resident (not being a company) or a foreign company shall keep and maintain books of account and other documents in accordance with the provisions contained in section 44AA and get his accounts audited by an accountant as defined in the Explanation below sub-section (2) of section 288 and furnish along with the return of income, the report of such audit in the prescribed form duly signed and verified by such accountant.

Section 44DB of the Act contains special provisions for computing deductions in the case of business reorganisation of co-operative banks. The deduction under section 32, section 35D, section 35DD or section 35DDA shall be allowed in accordance with the provisions of this section if business reorganisation of a co-operative bank has taken place during the financial year. The amount of deduction allowable to the predecessor co-operative bank and the successor co-operative bank shall be determined in accordance with the formula specified in the section.

This document discusses Indian laws related to the taxation of capital gains. It explains the concept of capital gains and the situations in which they are chargeable to income tax. According to the document, any profits or gains arising from the transfer of a capital asset in a previous year are chargeable to income tax under the head "Capital gains" (Section 45(1)). However, there are certain exceptions to this rule, such as the provisions of sections 3, 54, 54B, 54D, 54E, 54EA, 54EB, 54F, 54G, and 54H.

The document also discusses the treatment of capital gains in the case of business reorganization, specifically the amalgamation or demerger of a co-operative bank. In such cases, the provisions of section 35D, section 35DD or section 35DDA apply to the successor co-operative bank in the financial years subsequent to the year of business reorganisation as they would have applied to the predecessor co-operative bank, as if the business reorganisation had not taken place (Section 35BB).

The document also defines various legal terms related to business reorganization, such as "amalgamated co-operative bank," "amalgamating co-operative bank," "amalgamation," "business reorganisation," "co-operative bank," "demerger," "demerged co-operative bank," "predecessor co-operative bank," "successor co-operative bank," and "resulting co-operative bank."

In addition, the document explains the tax treatment of capital gains in the case of insurance receipts for damage to or destruction of a capital asset due to natural disasters, riots, civil disturbances, accidents, or enemy action. In such cases, the profits or gains arising from the receipt of the insurance money or other assets are chargeable to income tax as the income of the previous year in which the money or other assets were received (Section 45(1A)). The fair market value of the asset on the date of such receipt is deemed to be the full value of the consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer of the capital asset.

The document also discusses the tax treatment of capital gains in the case of conversion or treatment of a capital asset into stock-in-trade of a business carried on by the owner. In such cases, the profits or gains arising from the transfer are chargeable to income tax as the income of the previous year in which the stock-in-trade is sold or otherwise transferred by the owner (Section 45(2)). The fair market value of the asset on the date of such conversion or treatment is deemed to be the full value of the consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer of the capital asset.

Finally, the document explains the tax treatment of capital gains in the case of transfer of beneficial interest in securities by a depository or participant. In such cases, the profits or gains arising from the transfer are chargeable to income tax as the income of the beneficial owner of the previous year in which the transfer took place (Section 45(2A)). The cost of acquisition and the period of holding of any securities are determined on the basis of the first-in-first-out method. The expressions "beneficial owner," "depository," and "security" have the meanings assigned to them in the Depositories Act, 1996.
Summary:

This document summarizes various sections of the Income Tax Act related to capital gains in India.

Section 45 states that capital gains on the distribution of assets by companies in liquidation are not considered a transfer by the company. However, shareholders receiving money or other assets from the company are chargeable to income tax under the head "Capital gains" in the year of distribution.

Section 46A states that if a shareholder or holder of other specified securities receives any consideration from a company for purchasing its own shares or other specified securities, the difference between the cost of acquisition and the value of consideration received is deemed to be the capital gains arising to the shareholder or holder of other specified securities.

Section 47 lists transactions not regarded as transfer, including any distribution of capital assets on the total or partial partition of a Hindu undivided family, any transfer of a capital asset under a gift or will or an irrevocable trust, and any transfer of a capital asset by a company to its subsidiary company if the subsidiary company is an Indian company.

Section 47 also includes a proviso that nothing contained in clauses (iv) or (v) shall apply to the transfer of a capital asset made after February 29, 1988, as stock-in-trade.

Additionally, there are provisions related to the transfer of a capital asset in a scheme of amalgamation and transfer of a capital asset being a share or shares held in an Indian company, by the amalgamating foreign company to the amalgamated foreign company.

It is important to note that capital gains tax laws in India can be complex, and it is recommended that individuals consult with a tax professional for specific advice related to their individual circumstances.
Summary:

This document pertains to the Income Tax Act of India and outlines various provisions for the taxation of capital gains. Capital gains refer to the profit earned from the sale of a capital asset, which can include property, shares, bonds, or other investments. The document specifies various scenarios in which the tax liability on capital gains may be exempted or reduced. These scenarios include:

1. Transfer of a capital asset by a banking company to a banking institution under the Banking Regulation Act, 1949.
2. Transfer of a capital asset in a scheme of amalgamation or demerger, subject to certain conditions.
3. Transfer of a capital asset in a business reorganisation by a co-operative bank to another co-operative bank.
4. Transfer of a capital asset by a shareholder in a business reorganisation in consideration of allotment of shares in the successor co-operative bank.
5. Transfer of a capital asset in a demerger by a foreign company to another foreign company, subject to certain conditions.
6. Transfer of shares by the resulting company in a scheme of demerger to the shareholders of the demerged company.
7. Transfer of a capital asset by a shareholder in a scheme of amalgamation, subject to certain conditions.
8. Transfer of a capital asset, such as bonds or Global Depository Receipts, made outside India by a non-resident to another non-resident.
9. Transfer of a capital asset, such as rupee-denominated bonds of an Indian company, made outside India by a non-resident to another non-resident.
10. Transfer of a capital asset, such as bonds or Global Depository Receipts or rupee-denominated bonds of an Indian company, made on a recognised stock exchange located in an International Financial Services Centre by a non-resident to another non-resident, where the consideration for such transaction is paid or payable in foreign currency.
11. Transfer of a capital asset, such as Government Securities, made outside India through an intermediary dealing in settlement of securities, by a non-resident to another non-resident.
12. Transfer of Sovereign Gold Bonds issued by the Reserve Bank of India under the Sovereign Gold Bond Scheme, 2015, by way of redemption, by an assessee being an individual.

Summary:

Section 48 of the Income Tax Act, 1961, outlines the mode of computation for income charged under the head “Capital gains.” The income is computed by deducting certain amounts from the full value of the consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer of the capital asset. These amounts include expenditure incurred wholly and exclusively in connection with such transfer and the cost of acquisition of the asset and any improvement thereto. In the case of a non-resident assessee, capital gains arising from the transfer of shares or debentures of an Indian company are computed by converting the cost of acquisition, expenditure, and full value of the consideration into the same foreign currency as was initially utilised in the purchase of the shares or debentures and then reconverting it back into Indian currency. The provision also applies to every reinvestment thereafter in, and sale of, shares in, or debentures of, an Indian company. The provisions of clause (ii) are modified for a non-resident assessee, where the cost of acquisition is replaced with the indexed cost of acquisition. Certain exceptions to this rule are provided for capital gains arising from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, being a bond or debenture other than capital indexed bonds issued by the Government or Sovereign Gold Bond issued by the Reserve Bank of India under the Sovereign Gold Bond Scheme, 2015. In the case of an assessee being a non-resident, any gains arising on account of appreciation of rupee against a foreign currency at the time of redemption of rupee denominated bond of an Indian company held by him are ignored for the purposes of computation of full value of consideration under this section. Where shares, debentures or warrants referred to in the proviso to clause (iii) of section 47 are transferred under a gift or an irrevocable trust, the market value on the date of such transfer shall be deemed to be the full value of consideration received or accruing as a result of transfer for the purposes of this section. No deduction is allowed in computing the income chargeable under the head “Capital gains” in respect of any sum paid on account of securities transaction tax under Chapter VII of the Finance (No. 2) Act, 2004 (23 of 2004). The Explanation to this section provides the meaning of various terms used in this section.

Section 49 of the Income Tax Act, 1961, deals with the cost with reference to certain modes of acquisition. The cost of acquisition of a capital asset is deemed to be the cost for which the previous owner of the property acquired it, as increased by the cost of any improvement of the assets incurred or borne by the previous owner or the assessee, as the case may be. This provision applies where the capital asset became the property of the assessee on any distribution of assets on the total or partial partition of a Hindu undivided family, under a gift or will, by succession, inheritance or devolution, on any distribution of assets on the dissolution of a firm, body of individuals, or other association of persons, where such dissolution had taken place at any time before the 1st day of April, 1987, on any distribution of assets on the liquidation of a company, under a transfer to a revocable or an irrevocable trust, or under any such transfer as is referred to in clause (iv) or clause (v) or clause (vi) or clause (via) or clause (viaa) or clause (viab) or clause (vib) or clause (vic) or clause (vica) or clause (vicb) or clause (vicc) or clause (xiii) or clause (xiiib) or clause (xiv) of section 47. The cost of acquisition of the asset shall be deemed to be the cost for which the previous owner of the property acquired it, as increased by the cost of any improvement of the assets incurred or borne by the previous owner or the assessee, as the case may be. Explanation to this section provides the meaning of the expression “previous owner of the property” in relation to any capital asset owned by an assessee.

In conclusion, Sections 48 and 49 of the Income Tax Act, 1961, provide for the mode of computation of income charged under the head “Capital gains” and the cost with reference to certain modes of acquisition, respectively. The provisions of these sections are applicable to both residents and non-residents, and the cost of acquisition is determined based on the mode of acquisition of the capital asset. The provisions of these sections should be carefully studied and understood to ensure proper compliance with the Income Tax Act, 1961.
Summary:

Section 49 of the Income Tax Act, 1961, provides for the cost of acquisition of a capital asset in certain cases. This section modifies the provisions of sections 48 and 49 of the Act for depreciable assets. If the full value of consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer of the asset together with the full value of such consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer of any other capital asset falling within the block of the assets during the previous year, exceeds the aggregate of the following amounts, namely: (i) expenditure incurred wholly and exclusively in connection with such transfer or transfers; (ii) the written down value of the block of assets at the beginning of the previous year; and (iii) the actual cost of any asset falling within the block of assets acquired during the previous year, such excess shall be deemed to be the capital gains arising from the transfer of short-term capital assets. Where any block of assets ceases to exist as such, the cost of acquisition of the block of assets shall be the written down value of the block of assets at the beginning of the previous year, as increased by the actual cost of any asset falling within that block of assets, acquired by the assessee during the previous year and the income received or accruing as a result of such transfer or transfers shall be deemed to be the capital gains arising from the transfer of short-term capital assets.

Section 50A of the Income Tax Act, 1961, provides for the cost of acquisition of a capital asset in case of depreciable assets. If the capital asset is an asset in respect of which a deduction on account of depreciation under clause (i) of sub-section (1) of section 32 has been obtained by the assessee in any previous year, the provisions of section 48 and 49 shall apply subject to the modification that the written down value, as defined in clause (6) of section 43, of the asset, as adjusted, shall be taken as the cost of acquisition of the asset.

Section 50B of the Income Tax Act, 1961, provides for the cost of acquisition of a capital asset in case of slump sale. Any profits or gains arising from the slump sale effected in the previous year shall be chargeable to income-tax as capital gains arising from the transfer of long-term capital assets and shall be deemed to be the income of the previous year in which the transfer took place. If the capital asset is an undertaking or division transferred by way of such sale, the net worth of the undertaking or the division, as the case may be, shall be deemed to be the cost of acquisition and the cost of improvement for the purposes of section 48 and 49 and no regard shall be given to the provisions contained in the second proviso to section 48. Every assessee, in the case of slump sale, shall furnish in the prescribed form along with the return of income, a report of an accountant as defined in the Explanation below sub-section (2) of section 288, indicating the computation of the net worth of the undertaking or division, as the case may be.
Summary:

This document summarizes various sections of the Income Tax Act, 1961, specifically focusing on those related to Indian laws for the computation of capital gains.

Section 48 deals with the mode of computation of capital gains. It states that the net consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer of a capital asset is the full value of the consideration, and the cost of acquisition, improvement, and any expenditure incurred wholly and exclusively in connection with such transfer shall be deducted from the full value of the consideration.

Section 49 provides for the cost of acquisition in certain cases. It states that where the capital asset was acquired by the assessee before the 1st day of April 1981, the cost of acquisition shall be deemed to be the cost of acquisition to the previous owner, as increased by the cost of any improvement incurred by the assessee or the previous owner, as the case may be.

Section 50C deals with the special provision for full value of consideration in certain cases. It states that where the consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer of a capital asset, being land or building or both, is less than the value adopted or assessed or assessable by any authority of a State Government for the purpose of payment of stamp duty, the value so adopted or assessed or assessable shall be deemed to be the full value of the consideration received or accruing as a result of such transfer.

Section 50CA deals with the special provision for full value of consideration for transfer of share other than quoted share. It states that where the consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer of a capital asset, being share of a company other than a quoted share, is less than the fair market value of such share, the value so determined shall be deemed to be the full value of consideration received or accruing as a result of such transfer.

Section 50D deals with the fair market value deemed to be full value of consideration in certain cases. It states that where the consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer of a capital asset is not ascertainable or cannot be determined, then, for the purpose of computing income chargeable to tax as capital gains, the fair market value of the said asset on the date of transfer shall be deemed to be the full value of the consideration received or accruing as a result of such transfer.

Section 51 deals with advance money received. It states that any advance or other money received and retained by the assessee in respect of negotiations for the transfer of a capital asset shall be deducted from the cost for which the asset was acquired or the written down value or the fair market value, as the case may be, in computing the cost of acquisition.

Section 52 has been omitted.

Section 53 has also been omitted. It earlier provided for an exemption of capital gains from a residential house.

Section 54 deals with profit on sale of property used for residence. It states that where the capital gain arises from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, being a residential house, and the assessee has within a period of one year before or two years after the date on which the transfer took place purchased, or has within a period of three years after that date constructed, one residential house in India, then, instead of the capital gain being charged to income-tax as income of the previous year in which the transfer took place, it shall be dealt with in accordance with the provisions of this section.

Section 54B deals with capital gains on the transfer of land used for agricultural purposes. If the capital gain arises from the transfer of a capital asset being land that was being used for agricultural purposes by the assessee (an individual or their parent or a Hindu undivided family) in the two years immediately preceding the transfer, and the assessee purchases any other land for agricultural purposes within two years after the date of the transfer, then the capital gain shall be dealt with in accordance with the provisions of this section. If the amount of the capital gain is greater than the cost of the new land, the difference between the amount of the capital gain and the cost of the new land shall be charged under section 45 as the income of the previous year. If the amount of the capital gain is equal to or less than the cost of the new land, the capital gain shall not be charged under section 45.

If the amount of the capital gain is not utilised by the assessee for the purchase of the new land before the date of furnishing the return of income under section 139, it shall be deposited by him before furnishing such return in an account in any such bank or institution as may be specified in, and utilised in accordance with, any scheme which the Central Government may frame. If the amount deposited under this sub-section is not utilised wholly or partly for the purchase of the new land within the period specified in sub-section (1), then the amount not so utilised shall be charged under section 45 as the income of the previous year in which the period of two years from the date of the transfer of the original asset expires.
