If the capital gain arises from the transfer by way of compulsory acquisition under any law of a capital asset, being land or building or any right in land or building, forming part of an industrial undertaking belonging to the assessee which, in the two years immediately preceding the date on which the transfer took place, was being used by the assessee for the purposes of the business of the said undertaking, and the assessee has within a period of three years after that date purchased any other land or building or any right in any other land or building or constructed any other building for the purposes of shifting or re-establishing the said undertaking or setting up another industrial undertaking, then the capital gain shall be dealt with in accordance with the provisions of this section. If the amount of the capital gain is greater than the cost of the new land, building, or right, the difference between the amount of the capital gain and the cost of the new asset shall be charged under section 45 as the income of the previous year. If the amount of the capital gain is equal to or less than the cost of the new asset, the capital gain shall not be charged under section 45.

If the amount of the capital gain is not utilised by the assessee for the purchase or construction of the new asset before the date of furnishing the return of income under section 139, it shall be deposited by him before furnishing such return in an account in any such bank or institution as may be specified in, and utilised in accordance with, any scheme which the Central Government may frame. If the amount deposited under this sub-section is not utilised wholly or partly for the purchase or construction of the new asset within the period specified in sub-section (1), then the amount not so utilised shall be charged under section 45 as the income of the previous year in which the period of three years from the date of the transfer of the original asset expires.
The document is a section from the Income Tax Act, 1961, which deals with capital gains and their taxation. Specifically, this section discusses the provisions for the non-charging of capital gains in certain cases.

Under section 54E, if a long-term capital asset is transferred before April 1, 1992, and the assessee invests or deposits the whole or any part of the net consideration in a new asset within six months of the transfer, the capital gain will be dealt with in accordance with the following provisions:

* If the cost of the new asset is not less than the net consideration in respect of the original asset, the whole of the capital gain shall not be charged under section 45.
* If the cost of the new asset is less than the net consideration in respect of the original asset, so much of the capital gain as bears to the whole of the capital gain the same proportion as the cost of acquisition of the new asset bears to the net consideration shall not be charged under section 45.

There are some provisions and explanations that provide further clarification:

* Provision 1 states that in a case where the original asset is transferred after February 28, 1983, the assessee must invest or deposit the whole or any part of the net consideration in the new asset by initially subscribing to such new asset.
* Provision 2 states that in a case where the transfer of the original asset is by way of compulsory acquisition under any law and the full amount of compensation awarded for such acquisition is not received by the assessee on the date of such transfer, the period of six months referred to in this sub-section shall be reckoned from the date immediately following the date on which such compensation is received by the assessee or the 31st day of March, 1992, whichever is earlier.

Explanation 1 defines the term "specified asset" as any of the following assets:

* In a case where the original asset is transferred before March 1, 1979, any of the following assets:
	+ Securities of the Central Government or a State Government.
	+ Savings certificates as defined in clause (c) of section 2 of the Government Savings Certificates Act, 1959.
	+ Units in the Unit Trust of India established under the Unit Trust of India Act, 1963.
	+ Debentures specified by the Central Government for the purposes of clause (ii) of sub-section (1) of section 80L.
	+ Shares in any Indian company which are issued to the public or are listed in a recognized stock exchange in India in accordance with the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, and any rules made thereunder, where the investment in such shares is made before March 1, 1978.
	+ Deposits for a period of not less than three years with certain banks or co-operative societies.
* In a case where the original asset is transferred after February 28, 1979 but before March 1, 1983, National Rural Development Bonds as specified by the Central Government.
* In a case where the original asset is transferred after February 28, 1983 but before April 1, 1986, any of the following assets:
	+ Securities of the Central Government which the Central Government may specify in this behalf.
	+ Special series of units of the Unit Trust of India established under the Unit Trust of India Act, 1963, which the Central Government may specify in this behalf.
	+ National Rural Development Bonds as specified in Explanation 1 or as may be specified in this behalf under this clause by the Central Government.
	+ Debentures issued by the Housing and Urban Development Corporation Limited as the Central Government may specify in this behalf.
* In a case where the original asset is transferred after March 31, 1986, any of the assets specified in clause (c) and bonds issued by any public sector company as the Central Government may specify in this behalf.
* In a case where the original asset is transferred after March 31, 1989, any of the assets specified in clauses (c) and (d) and such debentures or bonds issued by the National Housing Bank established under the National Housing Bank Act, 1987, as the Central Government may specify in this behalf.

Explanation 2 defines the term "eligible issue of capital" as having the meaning assigned to it in sub-section (3) of section 80CC.

Explanation 3 states that an assessee shall not be deemed to have invested the whole or any part of the net consideration in any equity shares referred to in sub-clause (va) of clause (a) of Explanation 1, unless the assessee has subscribed to or purchased the shares in the manner specified in sub-section (4) of section 80CC.

Explanation 4 defines the term "cost" in relation to any new asset as the amount of such deposit.

Explanation 5 defines the term "net consideration" in relation to the transfer of a capital asset as the full value of the consideration received or accruing as a result of the transfer of the capital asset as reduced by any expenditure incurred wholly and exclusively in connection with such transfer.

Sub-section (1A) states that if the assessee deposits the whole or any part of the net consideration in respect of the original asset in any new asset, being a deposit referred to in sub-clause (vi) of clause (a) of Explanation 1 below sub-section (1), the cost of such new asset shall not be taken into account for the purposes of that sub-section unless the following conditions are fulfilled:

* The assessee furnishes, along with the deposit, a declaration in writing to the bank or the co-operative society referred to in the said sub-clause (vi) with which such deposit is made, to the effect that the assessee will not take any loan or advance on the security of such deposit during a period of three years from the date on which the deposit is made.
* The assessee furnishes, along with the return of income for the assessment year relevant to the previous year in which the transfer of the original asset was effected or within such further time as may be allowed by the Assessing Officer, a copy of the declaration referred to in clause (a) duly attested by an officer not below the rank of sub-agent, agent or manager of such bank or an officer of corresponding rank of such co-operative society.

Sub-section (1B) states that where on the fulfilment of the conditions specified in sub-section (1A), the cost of the new asset referred to in that sub-section is taken into account for the purposes of sub-section (1), the assessee shall, within a period of ninety days from the expiry of the period of three years reckoned from the date of such deposit, furnish to the Assessing Officer a certificate from the officer referred to in clause (b) of sub-section (1A) to the effect that the assessee has not taken any loan or advance on the security of such deposit during the said period of three years.

Sub-section (1C) states that where the capital gain arises from the transfer of the original asset, made after March 31, 1992, in respect of which the assessee had received any amount by way of advance on or before February 29, 1992 and had invested or deposited the whole or any part of such amount in the new asset on or before the later date, then, the provisions of clauses (a) and (b) of sub-section (1) shall apply in the case of such investment or deposit as they apply in the case of investment or deposit under that sub-section.

Sub-section (2) states that where the new asset is transferred, or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money, within a period of three years from the date of its acquisition, the amount of capital gain arising from the transfer of the original asset not charged under section 45 shall be deemed to be income chargeable under the head "Capital gains" of the previous year in which the new asset is transferred or converted into money.

The cost of the new asset and the net consideration in respect of the original asset are important factors in determining whether and to what extent the capital gain is not charged under section 45. Summary:

This document summarizes various sections of the Income Tax Act, 1961 related to Indian laws on capital gains.

Section 54B deals with capital gains arising from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, where the assessee invests the whole or any part of the net consideration in a new asset. The new asset can be a residential house, or a plot of land for the construction of a residential house. If the cost of the new asset is not less than the net consideration, the whole of the capital gain is not charged under section 45. If the cost of the new asset is less than the net consideration, so much of the capital gain as bears to the whole of the capital gain the same proportion as the cost of the new asset bears to the net consideration, is not charged under section 45. However, if the new asset is transferred or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money within a period of three years from the date of its acquisition, the amount of capital gain arising from the transfer of the original asset not charged under section 45 on the basis of the cost of such new asset is deemed to be the income chargeable under the head “Capital gains” relating to long-term capital assets of the previous year in which the new asset is transferred or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money.

Section 54D deals with capital gains arising from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, where the assessee invests the whole or any part of the net consideration in a new asset being a deposit in a public sector company engaged in the business of providing long-term finance for construction or purchase of houses in India. If the cost of the new asset is not less than the net consideration, the whole of the capital gain is not charged under section 45. If the cost of the new asset is less than the net consideration, so much of the capital gain as bears to the whole of the capital gain the same proportion as the cost of the new asset bears to the net consideration, is not charged under section 45. However, if the new asset is transferred or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money within a period of three years from the date of its acquisition, the amount of capital gain arising from the transfer of the original asset not charged under section 45 on the basis of the cost of such new asset is deemed to be the income chargeable under the head “Capital gains” relating to long-term capital assets of the previous year in which the new asset is transferred or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money.

Section 54EA deals with capital gains arising from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, where the assessee invests the whole or any part of the net consideration in specified securities. The specified securities can be bonds, debentures, shares of a public company or units of any mutual fund referred to in clause (23D) of section 10. If the cost of the specified securities is not less than the net consideration, the whole of the capital gain is not charged under section 45. If the cost of the specified securities is less than the net consideration, so much of the capital gain as bears to the whole of the capital gain the same proportion as the cost of the specified securities bears to the net consideration, is not charged under section 45. However, if the specified securities are transferred or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money at any time within a period of three years from the date of their acquisition, the amount of capital gain arising from the transfer of the original asset not charged under section 45 on the basis of the cost of such specified securities as provided in clause (a) or clause (b) of sub-section (1) shall be deemed to be the income chargeable under the head “Capital gains” relating to long-term capital assets of the previous year in which the specified securities are transferred or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money.

Section 54EB deals with capital gains arising from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, where the assessee invests the whole or any part of the capital gains in any of the assets specified by the Board in this behalf by notification in the Official Gazette. If the cost of the long-term specified asset is not less than the capital gain arising from the transfer of the original asset, the whole of such capital gain shall not be charged under section 45. If the cost of the long-term specified asset is less than the capital gain arising from the transfer of the original asset, so much of the capital gain as bears to the whole of the capital gain the same proportion as the cost of acquisition of the long-term specified asset bears to the whole of the capital gain, shall not be charged under section 45. However, if the long-term specified asset is transferred or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money at any time within a period of seven years from the date of its acquisition, the amount of capital gains arising from the transfer of the original asset not charged under section 45 on the basis of the cost of such long-term specified asset as provided in clause (a), or as the case may be, clause (b) of sub-section (1) shall be deemed to be the income chargeable under the head “Capital gains” relating to long-term capital assets of the previous year in which the long-term specified asset is transferred or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money.

Section 54EC deals with capital gains arising from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, where the assessee invests the whole or any part of the capital gains in the long-term specified asset. The long-term specified asset can be bonds issued by the National Highway Authority of India or by the Rural Electrification Corporation Limited. If the cost of the long-term specified asset is not less than the capital gain arising from the transfer of the original asset, the whole of such capital gain shall not be charged under section 45. If the cost of the long-term specified asset is less than the capital gain arising from the transfer of the original asset, so much of the capital gain as bears to the whole of the capital gain the same proportion as the cost of acquisition of the long-term specified asset bears to the whole of the capital gain, shall not be charged under section 45. However, if the long-term specified asset is transferred or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money within a period of three years from the date of its acquisition, the amount of capital gain arising from the transfer of the original asset not charged under section 45 on the basis of the cost of such long-term specified asset as provided in clause (a) or clause (b) of sub-section (1) shall be deemed to be the income chargeable under the head “Capital gains” relating to long-term capital assets of the previous year in which the long-term specified asset is transferred or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money.

Overall, these sections of the Income Tax Act, 1961 provide for exemptions from capital gains tax in certain situations where the assessee invests the whole or any part of the net consideration or capital gains in specified assets. The exemptions are subject to certain conditions and limitations, such as the time period within which the new asset or long-term specified asset is acquired, the cost of the new asset or long-term specified asset, and the period within which the new asset or long-term specified asset is transferred or converted (otherwise than by transfer) into money.
Summary:

Section 54EC of the Income Tax Act, 1961, provides exemption from capital gains tax if the gains are invested in long-term specified assets within a certain period. The section defines long-term specified assets as certain types of bonds issued by specified authorities or companies, with a lock-in period of three years (or five years in certain cases). The cost of the long-term specified asset is taken into account for the purposes of this section, and certain deductions are not allowed under other sections of the Act. The section also specifies the conditions for the eligible issue of capital and listed securities or units. If the specified equity shares are sold or transferred within one year of acquisition, the capital gains exemption is reversed and charged as income in the previous year of sale or transfer. The cost of the specified equity shares cannot be deducted under certain sections of the Act. Section 54EE provides exemption from capital gains tax if the gains are invested in units of a specified fund within a certain period. The section specifies the investment limit and the lock-in period for the long-term specified asset. If the long-term specified asset is transferred within three years of acquisition, the capital gains exemption is reversed and charged as income in the previous year of transfer.
Summary:

Section 54F of the Income Tax Act allows for the exemption of capital gains tax on the transfer of certain capital assets if the gain is invested in the purchase or construction of a residential house. The section applies to individuals and Hindu undivided families who transfer a long-term capital asset, not being a residential house, and purchase or construct a residential house within a specified period. The cost of the new asset must be equal to or greater than the net consideration in respect of the original asset in order for the exemption to apply. If the cost of the new asset is less than the net consideration, a portion of the capital gain will still be charged under section 45. Additionally, if the assessee owns more than one residential house on the date of transfer or purchases or constructs another residential house within the specified period, the exemption will not apply.

Section 54G of the Income Tax Act provides for the exemption of capital gains tax on the transfer of assets used for the purposes of an industrial undertaking in an urban area if the assets are shifted to a non-urban area and certain conditions are met. The section applies to the transfer of machinery, plant, buildings, land, or rights in building or land used for the purposes of the business of an industrial undertaking. The assessee must purchase new machinery or plant, acquire building or land, shift the original asset and transfer the establishment of the undertaking, and incur expenses on specified purposes within a certain period. The capital gain will not be charged under section 45 if the amount of the capital gain is equal to or less than the cost of the new asset. If the amount of the capital gain is greater than the cost of the new asset, the difference between the two will be charged as income under section 45. The cost of the new asset will be nil for the purpose of computing any capital gain arising from its transfer within three years of its being purchased, acquired, constructed, or transferred.

In both sections, if the amount of the capital gain is not appropriated towards the cost of the new asset within a certain period, it must be deposited in a specified bank or institution and can be withdrawn in accordance with a scheme framed by the Central Government. If the unutilized amount is not withdrawn within the specified period, it will be charged as income under section 45.
The document is a section of the Income Tax Act of India, specifically Sections 54GB, 54GA, and 54GB. These sections provide exemptions and rules for capital gains arising from the transfer of certain assets.

Section 54GB applies to capital gains arising from the transfer of a long-term capital asset, which is a residential property, by an eligible assessee. If the assessee utilizes the net consideration for subscription in the equity shares of an eligible company before the due date of furnishing the return of income, and the company utilizes this amount for the purchase of a new asset within one year, then the capital gain will not be charged to income-tax. If the net consideration is greater than the cost of the new asset, then a portion of the capital gain will not be charged based on the proportion of the cost of the new asset to the net consideration. If the amount of the net consideration is equal to or less than the cost of the new asset, the capital gain will not be charged.

If the amount received by the company for the issue of shares to the assessee is not utilized for the purchase of the new asset before the due date of furnishing the return of income, it must be deposited in a bank or institution as specified in a scheme framed by the Central Government. The return furnished by the assessee must be accompanied by proof of such deposit. The amount already utilized by the company for the purchase of the new asset together with the amount deposited will be deemed to be the cost of the new asset. If the amount so deposited is not utilized for the purchase of the new asset within the specified period, the portion of the capital gain that would not have been charged had the amount actually utilized for the purchase of the new asset been the cost of the new asset will be charged as income of the assessee for the previous year.

Section 54GA applies to capital gains arising from the transfer of a capital asset, being machinery or plant or building or land or any rights in building or land used for the purposes of the business of an industrial undertaking situate in an urban area, effected in the course of, or in consequence of the shifting of such industrial undertaking to any Special Economic Zone. If the assessee has within a period of one year before or three years after the date on which the transfer took place, purchased machinery or plant for the purposes of business of the industrial undertaking in the Special Economic Zone, acquired building or land or constructed building for the purposes of his business in the Special Economic Zone, shifted the original asset and transferred the establishment of such undertaking to the Special Economic Zone, and incurred expenses on such other purposes as may be specified in a scheme framed by the Central Government, then the capital gain will not be charged to income-tax. If the amount of the capital gain is greater than the cost and expenses incurred in relation to the new asset, the difference between the amount of the capital gain and the cost of the new asset shall be charged under section 45 as the income of the previous year. If the amount of the capital gain is equal to, or less than, the cost of the new asset, the capital gain shall not be charged.

If the amount of capital gain is not appropriated by the assessee towards the cost and expenses incurred in relation to the new asset within one year before the date on which the transfer of the original asset took place, or which is not utilized by him for all or any of the purposes mentioned in clauses (a) to (d) of sub-section (1) before the date of furnishing the return of income, it shall be deposited in an account in any such bank or institution as may be specified in, and utilised in accordance with, any scheme which the Central Government may, by notification in the Official Gazette, frame in this behalf and such return shall be accompanied by proof of such deposit. The amount, if any, already utilized by the assessee for all or any of the aforesaid purposes together with the amount so deposited shall be deemed to be the cost of the new asset. If the amount deposited is not utilized wholly or partly for all or any of the purposes mentioned in clauses (a) to (d) of sub-section (1) within the period specified in that sub-section, then, the amount not so utilized shall be charged under section 45 as the income of the previous year in which the period of three years from the date of the transfer of the original asset expires, and the assessee shall be entitled to withdraw such amount in accordance with the scheme aforesaid.
Summary:

This document discusses various sections of Indian tax laws related to capital gains. Section 54F deals with the exemption of long-term capital gains arising from the transfer of a residential property if the assessee invests the amount of capital gain in purchasing or constructing a new residential property. The exemption is available only if the new property is purchased or constructed within a specified period and is not sold or transferred within five years from the date of its acquisition. If the new property is sold or transferred within five years, the capital gain arising from the transfer of the residential property will be deemed to be the income of the assessee chargeable under the head “Capital gains” of the previous year in which such property is sold or transferred.

Section 54H provides an extension of time for acquiring a new asset or depositing or investing the amount of capital gain in certain cases of compulsory acquisition of the original asset. The period for acquiring the new asset or depositing or investing the amount of capital gain is reckoned from the date of receipt of compensation in such cases.

Section 55 defines the terms “adjusted”, “cost of improvement”, and “cost of acquisition” for the purposes of sections 48 and 49. The cost of improvement in relation to any capital asset, except goodwill, tenancy rights, stage carriage permits or loom hours, means all expenditure of a capital nature incurred in making any additions or alterations to the capital asset after it became the property of the assessee or the previous owner. The cost of acquisition in relation to a capital asset means the amount of the purchase price or the cost of improvement, as the case may be.

It is important to note that these sections use legal terms and concepts that have specific meanings under Indian tax laws. Therefore, it is recommended to consult a tax professional or the relevant tax authorities for a better understanding of these provisions and their implications.

This document outlines various provisions related to Indian laws, specifically focusing on the Income Tax Act, 1961. The summary of the relevant sections is as follows:

Section 56(2)(x): This section deals with the taxation of income from other sources. It specifies that any compensation or other payment received by a person in connection with the termination of his employment or the modification of the terms and conditions relating thereto is taxable as income.

Section 57: This section outlines the deductions that can be made from the income chargeable under the head “Income from other sources”. It specifies that all income arising to any person by virtue of a transfer, whether revocable or not and whether effected before or after the commencement of this Act, shall be chargeable to income-tax as the income of the transferor and shall be included in his total income, where there is no transfer of the assets from which the income arises.

Section 61: This section deals with revocable transfer of assets. It specifies that all income arising to any person by virtue of a revocable transfer of assets shall be chargeable to income-tax as the income of the transferor and shall be included in his total income.

Section 62: This section deals with transfer irrevocable for a specified period. It specifies that the provisions of section 61 shall not apply to any transfer irrevocable for a specified period.

These provisions aim to ensure that income earned by individuals and entities is properly taxed and that deductions and exemptions are allowed in a fair and transparent manner. Section 60 defines "transfer" and "revocable transfer" for the purposes of sections 60, 61, and 62. A transfer is deemed to be revocable if it contains any provision for the re-transfer of income or assets to the transferor, or if it gives the transferor the right to re-assume power over the income or assets.

Section 63 includes a list of assets and income that are considered to be transferred for the purposes of sections 60, 61, and 62. This includes assets transferred by way of trust, assets transferred before April 1, 1961 that are not revocable for a period exceeding six years, and assets transferred to a person or association of persons for the immediate or deferred benefit of the transferor's spouse.

Section 64 includes various provisions for including the income of certain family members in the tax liability of an individual. This includes the income of the individual's spouse, minor child, and son's wife from assets transferred by the individual. The income of the spouse is included in the tax liability of the individual if it is earned from a concern in which the individual has a substantial interest, or if it arises from assets transferred by the individual to the spouse otherwise than for adequate consideration. The income of the minor child is included in the tax liability of the individual if it is earned from manual work or an activity involving the application of the child's skill, talent, or specialized knowledge and experience.

Section 65 defines the liability of a person in respect of income included in the income of another person. This section states that if the income of one person is included in the income of another person, the first person is liable to pay tax on that income.

Overall, these sections of the Indian tax laws provide rules for determining the tax liability of individuals and families, and specify which assets and income are considered to be transferred for tax purposes.

The document summarizes various sections of the Income Tax Act, 1961 related to the set-off and carry-forward of losses. Section 70 deals with the set-off of losses from one head against income from another. It explains that a loss under any head, other than capital gains, can be set off against income under any other head in the same assessment year. If there is income under the head of capital gains, the loss can be set off against that income as well. However, there are certain exceptions to this rule.

Section 71A deals with the transitional provisions for set-off of loss under the head "Income from house property". It states that where the net result of the computation under this head is a loss in the assessment years commencing on the 1st day of April, 1993 or the 1st day of April, 1994, such loss can be carried forward and set off in the assessment year commencing on the 1st day of April, 1995, and the balance, if any, in the assessment year commencing on the 1st day of April, 1996, against the income under any head.

Section 71B deals with the carry forward and set off of loss from house property. It states that where the net result of the computation under this head is a loss, such loss can be carried forward to the following assessment year and set off against the income from house property assessable for that assessment year. The loss, if any, which has not been set off wholly, can be carried forward to the following assessment year, not being more than eight assessment years immediately succeeding the assessment year for which the loss was first computed.

Section 72 deals with the carry forward and set off of business losses. It states that where the net result of the computation under the head "Profits and gains of business or profession" is a loss, such loss can be carried forward to the following assessment year and set off against the profits and gains of any business or profession carried on by the assessee and assessable for that assessment year. If the loss cannot be wholly set off, the amount of loss not so set off can be carried forward to the following assessment year and so on for eight assessment years immediately succeeding the assessment year for which the loss was first computed.

Section 72A deals with the provisions relating to carry forward and set-off of accumulated loss and unabsorbed depreciation allowance in amalgamation or demerger, etc. It states that where there has been an amalgamation of certain companies, the accumulated loss and unabsorbed depreciation of the amalgamating company shall be deemed to be the loss or allowance for unabsorbed depreciation of the amalgamated company for the previous year in which the amalgamation was effected. Other provisions of the Act relating to set-off and carry forward of loss and allowance for depreciation shall apply accordingly. However, there are certain conditions that need to be fulfilled for the loss to be set off or carried forward and the unabsorbed depreciation to be allowed in the assessment of the amalgamated company.
In India, the Income Tax Act, 1961 (the Act) governs the taxation of income. This document summarizes provisions related to the carry forward and set off of accumulated losses and unabsorbed depreciation allowances in certain cases of business reorganization.

Section 72A of the Act deals with the set off and carry forward of accumulated losses and unabsorbed depreciation allowances in cases of amalgamation or demerger. In a case of amalgamation, the accumulated loss and unabsorbed depreciation of the amalgamating company shall be deemed to be the loss or allowance for depreciation of the amalgamated company for the previous year in which the scheme of amalgamation is brought into force. The provisions of this section apply to amalgamations that are sanctioned and brought into force by the Central Government under section 45(7) of the Banking Regulation Act, 1949. For the purposes of this section, "accumulated loss" means the loss of the amalgamating company that it would have been entitled to carry forward and set off under the provisions of section 72 if the amalgamation had not taken place. "Unabsorbed depreciation" means the allowance for depreciation of the amalgamating company that remains to be allowed and would have been allowed to such company if the amalgamation had not taken place.

Section 72AA of the Act deals with the provisions relating to carry forward and set off of accumulated loss and unabsorbed depreciation allowance in schemes of amalgamation of banking companies in certain cases. This section applies where there has been an amalgamation of a banking company with any other banking institution under a scheme sanctioned and brought into force by the Central Government under section 45(7) of the Banking Regulation Act, 1949. In such cases, the accumulated loss and the unabsorbed depreciation of the banking company shall be deemed to be the loss or allowance for depreciation of the banking institution for the previous year in which the scheme of amalgamation is brought into force. The other provisions of the Act relating to set off and carry forward of loss and allowance for depreciation shall apply accordingly.

Section 72AB of the Act deals with the provisions relating to carry forward and set off of accumulated loss and unabsorbed depreciation allowance in business reorganization of co-operative banks. In a case where the amalgamation has taken place during the previous year, the successor co-operative bank shall be allowed to set off the accumulated loss and the unabsorbed depreciation, if any, of the predecessor co-operative bank as if the amalgamation had not taken place. The provisions of this section shall apply if the predecessor co-operative bank has been engaged in the business of banking for three or more years and has held at least three-fourths of the book value of fixed assets as on the date of the business reorganization, continuously for two years prior to the date of business reorganization. The successor co-operative bank must hold at least three-fourths of the book value of fixed assets of the predecessor co-operative bank acquired through business reorganization, continuously for a minimum period of five years immediately succeeding the date of business reorganization, continue the business of the predecessor co-operative bank for a minimum period of five years from the date of business reorganization, and fulfill such other conditions as may be prescribed.

In summary, the Income Tax Act, 1961 provides for the set off and carry forward of accumulated losses and unabsorbed depreciation allowances in cases of amalgamation, demerger, or business reorganization of co-operative banks. The provisions vary depending on the type of business reorganization and the nature of the entities involved.
Summary:

Section 72A of the Income Tax Act, 1961, deals with the set-off and carry forward of losses in the case of business reorganization of co-operative banks. According to this section, the accumulated loss and unabsorbed depreciation of the demerged co-operative bank can be set off against the income of the resulting co-operative bank, subject to certain conditions. These conditions include the transfer of the whole or a part of the undertaking of the demerged co-operative bank to the resulting co-operative bank, and the continuity of the business of the demerged co-operative bank. The Central Government may also specify other conditions to ensure that the business reorganization is for genuine business purposes. The period of set off and carry forward of loss and allowance for depreciation is deemed to be two different previous years for the purposes of this section. If the conditions specified in sub-section (2) or notified under sub-section (4) are not complied with, the set off of accumulated loss or unabsorbed depreciation allowed in any previous year to the successor co-operative bank shall be deemed to be the income of the successor co-operative bank chargeable to tax for the year in which the conditions are not complied with.

Section 73 of the Income Tax Act, 1961, deals with losses in speculation business. According to this section, any loss, computed in respect of a speculation business carried on by the assessee, shall not be set off except against profits and gains, if any, of another speculation business. Where for any assessment year any loss computed in respect of a speculation business has not been wholly set off under sub-section (1), so much of the loss as is not so set off or the whole loss where the assessee had no income from any other speculation business, shall, subject to the other provisions of this Chapter, be carried forward to the following assessment year. No loss shall be carried forward under this section for more than four assessment years immediately succeeding the assessment year for which the loss was first computed.

Section 73A of the Income Tax Act, 1961, deals with the carry forward and set off of losses by specified business. According to this section, any loss, computed in respect of any specified business referred to in section 35AD, shall not be set off except against profits and gains, if any, of any other specified business. Where for any assessment year any loss computed in respect of the specified business referred to in sub-section (1) has not been wholly set off under sub-section (1), so much of the loss as is not so set off or the whole loss where the assessee has no income from any other specified business, shall, subject to the other provisions of this Chapter, be carried forward to the following assessment year.

Section 74 of the Income Tax Act, 1961, deals with losses under the head “Capital gains”. According to this section, where in respect of any assessment year, the net result of the computation under the head “Capital gains” is a loss to the assessee, the whole loss shall, subject to the other provisions of this Chapter, be carried forward to the following assessment year. No loss shall be carried forward under this section for more than eight assessment years immediately succeeding the assessment year for which the loss was first computed.

Section 74A of the Income Tax Act, 1961, deals with losses from certain specified sources falling under the head “Income from other sources”. According to this section, the amount of loss incurred by the assessee in the activity of owning and maintaining race horses in any assessment year shall not be set off against income, if any, from any source other than the activity of owning and maintaining race horses in that year and shall, subject to the other provisions of this Chapter, be carried forward to the following assessment year. The loss can be set off against the income from the activity of owning and maintaining race horses assessable for that assessment year, provided that the activity of owning and maintaining race horses is carried on by him in the previous year relevant for that assessment year. No portion of the loss shall be carried forward for more than four assessment years immediately succeeding the assessment year for which the loss was first computed.

The Income Tax Act, 1961, contains various provisions related to deductions that can be claimed by taxpayers. This summary will focus on Sections 80AB, 80AC, 80B, and Section 80C, which deal with deductions in respect of certain incomes, deduction not to be allowed unless return furnished, definitions, and deduction in respect of life insurance premia, deferred annuity, contributions to provident fund, subscription to certain equity shares or debentures, etc.

Section 80AB:
This section deals with deductions to be made with reference to the income included in the gross total income. Where any deduction is required to be made or allowed under any section 4 included in this Chapter under the heading 'C.—Deductions in respect of certain incomes' in respect of any income of the nature specified in that section which is included in the gross total income of the assessee, then, notwithstanding anything contained in that section, for the purpose of computing the deduction under that section, the amount of income of that nature as computed in accordance with the provisions of this Act (before making any deduction under this Chapter) shall alone be deemed to be the amount of income of that nature which is derived or received by the assessee and which is included in his gross total income.

Section 80AC:
This section deals with deduction not to be allowed unless return furnished. Where in computing the total income of an assessee of any previous year relevant to the assessment year commencing on or after -

(i) the 1st day of April, 2006 but before the 1st day of April, 2018, any deduction is admissible under section 80-IA or section 80-IAB or section 80-IB or section 80-IC or section 80-ID or section 80-IE;

(ii) the 1st day of April, 2018, any deduction is admissible under any provision of this Chapter under the heading “C.—Deductions in respect of certain incomes”,

no such deduction shall be allowed to him unless he furnishes a return of his income for such assessment year on or before the due date specified under sub-section (1) of section 139.

Section 80B:
This section deals with definitions. In this Chapter—

(5) “gross total income” means the total income computed in accordance with the provisions of this Act, before making any deduction under this Chapter;

Section 80C:
This section deals with deduction in respect of life insurance premia, deferred annuity, contributions to provident fund, subscription to certain equity shares or debentures, etc. In computing the total income of an assessee, being an individual or a Hindu undivided family, there shall be deducted, in accordance with and subject to the provisions of this section, the whole of the amount paid or deposited in the previous year, being the aggregate of the sums referred to in sub-section (2), as does not exceed 150,000 rupees.

The sums referred to in sub-section (1) shall be any sums paid or deposited in the previous year by the assessee -

(i) to effect or to keep in force an insurance on the life of persons specified in sub-section (4);

(ii) to effect or to keep in force a contract for a deferred annuity, not being an annuity plan referred to in clause (xii), on the life of persons specified in sub-section (4):

Provided that such contract does not contain a provision for the exercise by the insured of an option to receive a cash payment in lieu of the payment of the annuity;

(iii) by way of deduction from the salary payable by or on behalf of the Government to any individual being a sum deducted in accordance with the conditions of his service, for the purpose of securing to him a deferred annuity or making provision for his spouse or children, in so far as the sum so deducted does not exceed one-fifth of the salary;

(iv) as a contribution by an individual to any provident fund to which the Provident Funds Act, 1925 (19 of 1925) applies;

(v) as a contribution to any provident fund set up by the Central Government and notified by it in this behalf in the Official Gazette, where such contribution is to an account standing in the name of any person specified in sub-section (4);

(vi) as a contribution by an employee to a recognised provident fund;

(vii) as a contribution by an employee to an approved superannuation fund;

(viii) as subscription, in the name of any person specified in sub-section (4), to any such security of the Central Government or any such deposit scheme as that Government may, by notification in the Official Gazette, specify in this behalf;

(ix) as subscription to any such savings certificate as defined in clause (c) of section 2 of the Government Savings Certificates Act, 1959 (46 of 1959), as the Central Government may, by notification in the Official Gazette, specify in this behalf;

(x) as a contribution, in the name of any person specified in sub-section (4), for participation in the Unit-linked Insurance Plan, 1971 (hereafter in this section referred to as the Unit-linked Insurance Plan) specified in Schedule II of the Unit Trust of India (Transfer of Undertaking and Repeal) Act, 2002 (58 of 2002);

(xi) as a contribution in the name of any person specified in sub-section (4) for participation in any such unit-linked insurance plan of the LIC Mutual Fund referred to in clause (23D) of section 10, as the Central Government may, by notification in the Official Gazette, specify in this behalf;

(xii) to effect or to keep in force a contract for such annuity plan of the Life Insurance Corporation or any other insurer as the Central Government may, by notification in the Official Gazette, specify;

(xiii) as subscription to any units of any Mutual Fund referred to in clause (23D) of section 10 or from the Administrator or the specified company under any plan formulated in accordance with such scheme as the Central Government may, by notification in the Official Gazette, specify in this behalf;

(xiv) as a contribution by an individual to any pension fund set up by any Mutual Fund referred to in clause (23D) of section 10 or by the Administrator or the specified company, as the Central Government may, by notification in the Official Gazette, specify in this behalf;

(xv) as subscription to any such deposit scheme of, or as a contribution to any such pension fund set up by, the National Housing Bank established under section 3 of the National Housing Bank Act, 1987 (53 of 1987) (hereafter in this section referred to as the National Housing Bank), as the Central Government may, by notification in the Official Gazette, specify in this behalf;

(xvi) as subscription to any such deposit scheme of -

(a) a public sector company which is engaged in providing long-term finance for construction or purchase of houses in India for residential purposes; or

(b) any authority constituted in India by or under any law enacted either for the purpose of dealing with and satisfying the need for housing accommodation or for the purpose of planning, development or improvement of cities, towns and villages, or for both,

as the Central Government may, by notification in the Official Gazette, specify in this behalf;

(xvii) as tuition fees (excluding any payment towards any development fees or donation or payment of similar nature), whether at the time of admission or thereafter, -

(a) to any university, college, school or other educational institution situated within India;

(b) for the purpose of full-time education of any of the persons specified in sub-section (4);

(xviii) for the purposes of purchase or construction of a residential house property the income from which is chargeable to tax under the head “Income from house property” (or which would, if it had not been used for the assessee's own residence, have been chargeable to tax under that head), where such payments are made towards or by way of -

(a) any instalment or part payment of the amount due under any self-financing or other scheme of any development authority, housing board or other authority engaged in the construction and sale of house property on ownership basis; or

(b) any instalment or part payment of the amount due to any company or co-operative society of which the assessee is a shareholder or member towards the cost of the house property allotted to him; or

(c) repayment of the amount borrowed by the assessee from -

(1) the Central Government or any State Government, or

(2) any bank, including a co-operative bank, or

(3) the Life Insurance Corporation, or

(4) the National Housing Bank, or

(5) any public company formed and registered in India with the main object of carrying on the business of providing long-term finance for construction or purchase of houses in India for residential purposes which is eligible for deduction under clause (viii) of sub-section (1) of section 36, or

(6) any company in which the public are substantially interested or any co-operative society, where such company or co-operative society is engaged in the business of financing the construction of houses, or

(7) the assessee’s employer where such employer is an authority or a board or a corporation or any other body established or constituted under a Central or State Act, or

(8) the assessee’s employer where such employer is a public company or a public sector company or a university established by law or a college affiliated to such university or a local authority or a co-operative society; or

(d) stamp duty, registration fee and other expenses for the purpose of transfer of such house property to the assessee.

However, the deductions under Section 80C shall not be available in respect of any sum paid on account of the assessee himself being the beneficiary of such insurance policy or annuity plan or being the owner of the house property.
Summary:

The Income Tax Act of 1961, Section 80C, provides for deductions from taxable income for certain investments and expenses. The section allows for a deduction of up to 1.5 lakh rupees for investments in specified savings schemes, life insurance premiums, tuition fees, and equity shares or debentures in eligible companies, among other things. The investments and expenses must be made in the financial year for which the tax is being filed. The section also specifies the conditions under which the deductions will not be allowed, such as if the investments are sold or transferred within a certain period. The section aims to promote long-term savings and investment in specified areas.

In the context of Indian laws, the following terms are relevant:

* Income Tax Act, 1961: This is the primary law governing the taxation of income in India.
* Section 80C: This section of the Income Tax Act provides for deductions from taxable income for certain investments and expenses.
* Financial year: This refers to the period from April 1 to March 31 of the following year, during which the investments and expenses must be made to be eligible for deduction.
* Specified savings schemes: These are savings schemes specified in Section 80C, such as Public Provident Fund, National Savings Certificate, and Sukanya Samriddhi Account.
* Life insurance premiums: The premiums paid for life insurance policies are eligible for deduction under Section 80C.
* Tuition fees: The fees paid for the full-time education of up to two children are eligible for deduction under Section 80C.
* Equity shares or debentures: Investments in equity shares or debentures of eligible companies are eligible for deduction under Section 80C. The section specifies the conditions under which the deductions will not be allowed, such as if the investments are sold or transferred within a certain period.

In summary, Section 80C of the Income Tax Act provides for deductions from taxable income for certain investments and expenses. The section aims to promote long-term savings and investment in specified areas. The relevant Indian laws include the Income Tax Act, 1961, and the specified savings schemes, life insurance premiums, tuition fees, and equity shares or debentures that are eligible for deduction under Section 80C. The section also specifies the conditions under which the deductions will not be allowed.
Summary:

Section 80C of the Income Tax Act, 1961 allows a deduction of up to INR 1.5 lakh for investments made in certain specified savings schemes, life insurance premiums, and tuition fees for children. The section also includes deductions for contributions to certain pension funds, deferred annuity plans, and equity-linked savings schemes. The deduction is allowed only if the investments or payments are made out of the individual's taxable income.

Section 80CCA allows a deduction for deposits under the National Savings Scheme or payment to a deferred annuity plan. The maximum deduction allowed is INR 20,000, which is increased to INR 30,000 for the assessment years 1989-90 and 1990-91 and to INR 40,000 for subsequent assessment years. No deduction is allowed for amounts deposited or paid on or after April 1, 1992. If any amount withdrawn or received in respect of these deposits or annuity plans exceeds the amount invested, it is deemed to be the income of the assessee and is chargeable to tax.

Section 80CCB allows a deduction of up to INR 10,000 for investments made in units of any mutual fund specified under section 10 or of the Unit Trust of India under any plan formulated in accordance with the Equity Linked Savings Scheme. No deduction is allowed for amounts invested on or after April 1, 1992. If any amount invested in the units is returned to the assessee in whole or in part, it is deemed to be the income of the assessee and is chargeable to tax.

Section 80CCC allows a deduction for contributions to certain pension funds. The maximum deduction allowed is INR 1.5 lakh. If any amount withdrawn or received in respect of these contributions exceeds the amount invested, it is deemed to be the income of the assessee and is chargeable to tax.

These sections were amended by the Finance Act, 2020, to increase the maximum deduction allowed under Section 80C from INR 1.5 lakh to INR 2 lakh, and to allow a deduction for contributions to the National Pension System (NPS) under Section 80CCD. The deduction for contributions to the NPS is subject to certain conditions and limitations.
Summary:

Section 10(10D) of the Income Tax Act, 1961, provides for tax exemption on maturity proceeds of life insurance policies. However, there are certain conditions to be fulfilled for such exemption. This section explains the conditions and the amount that is exempt from tax.

According to Section 10(10D), any amount received under a life insurance policy, including the sum assured and bonus, is exempt from income tax if the policy satisfies the following conditions:

1. The policy must be issued on or before March 31, 2003, or
2. The policy must be issued on or after April 1, 2003, and the premium paid for any of the years during the policy term does not exceed 10% of the actual capital sum assured.

However, the exemption is not available in the following cases:

1. If the policy is surrendered before the end of the term, and
2. If the policy is assigned or transferred to any person before the end of the term.

In addition, Section 80C allows a deduction of up to Rs. 1.5 lakh for the amount paid towards the premium of a life insurance policy. Section 80CCD allows an additional deduction of up to Rs. 50,000 for contributions made to the National Pension System (NPS) by an individual.

Therefore, if an individual pays the premium for a life insurance policy and also contributes to the NPS, they can claim a deduction of up to Rs. 2 lakh under Section 80C and 80CCD. The maturity proceeds of the life insurance policy will be exempt from tax under Section 10(10D) if the policy satisfies the conditions mentioned above.

It is important to note that the exemption under Section 10(10D) is not available for policies issued on or after April 1, 2012, if the premium paid for any of the years during the policy term exceeds 10% of the actual capital sum assured. However, this limit is relaxed to 15% for persons with a disability or severe disability, or for persons suffering from specified diseases.

In conclusion, Section 10(10D) of the Income Tax Act, 1961, provides for tax exemption on maturity proceeds of life insurance policies, subject to certain conditions. An individual can claim a deduction of up to Rs. 2 lakh under Section 80C and 80CCD for the amount paid towards the premium of a life insurance policy and contributions made to the NPS. The maturity proceeds of the life insurance policy will be exempt from tax under Section 10(10D) if the policy satisfies the conditions mentioned above.

This means that the assessee can claim a deduction for donations made to specified funds or institutions, subject to the conditions outlined in sub-section (2).

* Prime Minister's National Relief Fund
* National Foundation for Communal Harmony

* The donation must be made in cash or cheque
* The donation must be made to a fund or institution specified in sub-section (2)
* The assessee must have a receipt for the donation
* The donation must not be made to a trust or institution that is not approved by the Income Tax Department

For example, donations made to the Prime Minister's National Relief Fund or the National Defence Fund are eligible for a 100% deduction.

The section also includes provisions for carry forward of unabsorbed deductions. If the amount of deduction exceeds the total income of the assessee, the unabsorbed deduction can be carried forward for a period of eight assessment years.

This document discusses the conditions for an institution or fund to be considered a charitable institution or fund under the Income Tax Act of India, 1961. The institution or fund must meet the following criteria:

1. The institution or fund must be established for charitable purposes, which do not include any purpose that is of a religious nature.
2. The institution or fund must not have any provision for the transfer or application of its income or assets for any purpose other than a charitable purpose.
3. The institution or fund must not be expressed to be for the benefit of any particular religious community or caste.
4. The institution or fund must maintain regular accounts of its receipts and expenditure.

7. If the institution or fund had been approved under clause (vi) for the previous year beginning on April 1, 2007 and ending on March 31, 2008, it shall be deemed to have been established for charitable purposes for the previous year beginning on April 1, 2008 and ending on March 31, 2009 and approved under the said clause (vi) for the previous year beginning on April 1, 2008 and ending on March 31, 2009.
8. No deduction shall be allowed under this section in respect of donation of any sum exceeding INR 2,000 unless such sum is paid by any mode other than cash.

Section 80GGA of the Income Tax Act of India allows for deductions in respect of certain incomes. Specifically, it applies to sums paid by an assessee to an association or institution for carrying out a programme of rural development or training of persons for implementing programmes of rural development. The deduction is not denied merely on the grounds that the approval granted to the programme or association/institution has been withdrawn after the payment of the sum by the assessee. Additionally, sums paid to a public sector company, local authority, or association or institution for carrying out an eligible project or scheme under section 35AC are also eligible for deductions. The deduction is not denied if the approval granted to the association or institution or the notification notifying the eligible project or scheme is withdrawn after the payment of the sum by the assessee. Sums paid by the assessee to an association or institution for conservation of natural resources or afforestation, to a rural development fund, or to the National Urban Poverty Eradication Fund are also eligible for deductions. No deduction is allowed for sums exceeding ten thousand rupees unless such sum is paid by any mode other than cash. Deductions are not allowed in the case of an assessee whose gross total income includes income which is chargeable under the head “Profits and gains of business or profession”. Deductions are also not allowed under any other provision of the Act for the same or any other assessment year. Section 80GGB allows for deductions in respect of contributions given by companies to political parties. Contributions made by way of cash are not eligible for deductions. The word “contribute” has the meaning assigned to it under section 293A of the Companies Act, 1956. Section 80GGC allows for deductions in respect of contributions given by any person to political parties, except local authorities and every artificial juridical person wholly or partly funded by the Government. Contributions made by way of cash are not eligible for deductions. The definition of “political party” is provided as a political party registered under section 29A of the Representation of the People Act, 1951.

The excerpt comes from the Income Tax Act of India and discusses the tax deductions allowed for certain types of income. Specifically, it outlines the deductions available for profits and gains derived from the execution of a foreign project or a housing project.

For profits and gains derived from the execution of a foreign project, there is a deduction allowed for an amount equal to a percentage of the profits and gains. The percentage varies depending on the assessment year, with the deduction being gradually reduced each year until it is completely phased out in the assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2005. The deduction is only allowed if the assessee maintains separate accounts for the profits and gains derived from the business of the execution of the foreign project, and those accounts have been audited by an accountant. The profits and gains must also be debited to a reserve account and brought into India in convertible foreign exchange within a specified time frame.

Similarly, for profits and gains derived from the execution of a housing project awarded on the basis of global tender and aided by the World Bank, there is a deduction allowed for an amount equal to a percentage of the profits and gains. Again, the percentage varies depending on the assessment year and is gradually reduced until it is completely phased out in the assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2005. The deduction is only allowed if the assessee maintains separate accounts for the profits and gains derived from the business of the execution of the housing project, and those accounts have been audited by an accountant. The profits and gains must also be debited to a reserve account and utilized for the purposes of the business of the assessee during a period of five years next following.

Overall, the excerpt highlights the importance of maintaining separate accounts and following specific procedures in order to qualify for tax deductions related to profits and gains derived from the execution of foreign or housing projects.
Summary:

Section 80HHC of the Income Tax Act, 1961, allows for a deduction in computing the total income of an assessee who is engaged in the business of exporting goods or merchandise. The deduction is applicable to profits derived from the export of such goods or merchandise, and the extent of the deduction is determined by sub-section (1B) of the section. The section applies to all goods or merchandise, except for mineral oil, minerals, and ores (other than processed minerals and ores specified in the Twelfth Schedule). The sale proceeds of the exported goods or merchandise must be received in, or brought into, India by the assessee within six months from the end of the previous year, or within such further period as allowed by the competent authority. The profits derived from the export are computed differently for goods or merchandise manufactured or processed by the assessee, trading goods, and a combination of both. The profits computed under this section are further increased by the amount which bears to ninety per cent of certain sums referred to in section 28, based on the proportion of export turnover to total turnover of the business carried on by the assessee. There are additional provisions for assessees with export turnover not exceeding rupees ten crores during the previous year, and for those with export turnover exceeding rupees ten crores during the previous year, subject to certain conditions.
Summary:

This document discusses the provisions of Section 80HHD of the Income Tax Act, 1961, which allows for a deduction in computing the total income of an Indian company or a person (other than a company) resident in India who is engaged in the business of a hotel or of a tour operator, approved by the prescribed authority, or of a travel agent. The deduction is applicable for assessment years beginning on the 1st day of April, 2001, and is equal to the aggregate of 40% of the profits derived by the assessee from services provided to foreign tourists and so much of the amount not exceeding 40% of the profits referred to in sub-clause (i) as is debited to the profit and loss account of the previous year in respect of which the deduction is to be allowed and credited to a reserve account to be utilised for the purposes of the business of the assessee in the manner laid down in sub-section (4). For assessment years beginning on the 1st day of April, 2002, the deduction is equal to the aggregate of 30% of the profits derived by the assessee from services provided to foreign tourists and so much of the amount not exceeding 30% of the profits referred to in sub-clause (i) as is debited to the profit and loss account of the previous year in respect of which the deduction is to be allowed and credited to a reserve account to be utilised for the purposes of the business of the assessee in the manner laid down in sub-section (4). The deduction is subject to certain conditions and limitations, including the requirement that the business of the assessee is approved by the prescribed authority. The document also explains various legal terms and concepts used in the section, such as "assessee," "profits," "foreign tourists," and "reserve account."

This document discusses the Indian Income Tax Act of 1961, specifically Section 80HHD, which provides deductions for profits derived from services provided to foreign tourists. The section applies only to services provided to foreign tourists the receipts of which are received in, or brought into, India by the assessee in convertible foreign exchange within a period of six months from the end of the previous year or within such further period as the competent authority may allow. The amount of deduction allowed varies depending on the assessment year, with 30% of profits allowed for the assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2002, 25% for the assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2003, and 15% for the assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2004. The deduction is calculated based on profits derived from services provided to foreign tourists, which is the amount that bears to the profits of the business the same proportion as the receipts specified in sub-section (2) bear to the total receipts of the business carried on by the assessee. The amount credited to the reserve account under clause (b) of sub-section (1) must be utilized for specific purposes related to the growth of Indian tourism, such as construction of new hotels, purchase of new cars and coaches, and provision of new facilities for the growth of Indian tourism, within a period of five years from the previous year in which the amount was credited. If the amount is utilized for any other purpose or not utilized within the specified period, it will be deemed to be the profits of the previous year in which the amount was so utilized or not utilized and will be charged to tax accordingly. The deduction is not admissible unless the assessee furnishes a report of an accountant certifying that the deduction has been correctly claimed based on the amount of convertible foreign exchange received for services provided by him to foreign tourists and the payments made by him to any assessee referred to in sub-section (2A) and the payments received by him in Indian currency as referred to in the Explanation 1 to sub-section (2). The deduction is also subject to certain conditions and limitations, including the requirement that the assessee must be engaged in the business of providing services to foreign tourists and that the services must be provided to foreign tourists.
The document to summarize is Section 80HHF of the Income Tax Act, 1961 of India. This section allows for a deduction in the total income of an assessee (an Indian company or a person resident in India) who is engaged in the business of exporting or transferring by any means out of India film software, television software, music software, television news software, including telecast rights. The deduction is allowed to the extent of profits derived from such business, as specified in sub-section (1A). The extent of deduction of profits is an amount equal to 80% of such profits for an assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2001, 70% thereof for an assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2002, 50% thereof for an assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2003, 30% thereof for an assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2004, and no deduction shall be allowed in respect of the assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2005 and any subsequent assessment year.

The deduction is allowed only if the consideration in respect of the software is received in, or brought into, India by the assessee in convertible foreign exchange, within a period of six months from the end of the previous year or within such further period as the competent authority may allow. The consideration is deemed to have been received in India where it is credited to a separate account maintained for the purpose by the assessee with any bank outside India with the approval of the Reserve Bank of India. The expression “competent authority” means the Reserve Bank of India or such other authority as is authorized under any law for the time being in force for regulating payments and dealings in foreign exchange.

For the purposes of sub-section (1), profits derived from the business shall be the amount which bears to the profits of the business, the same proportion as the export turnover bears to the total turnover of the business carried on by the assessee. The deduction is not admissible unless the assessee furnishes in the prescribed form, along with the return of income, the report of an accountant, as defined in the Explanation below sub-section (2) of section 288, certifying that the deduction has been correctly claimed in accordance with the provisions of this section.

The deduction under sub-section (1) shall not be admissible unless the assessee furnishes in the prescribed form, along with the return of income, the report of an accountant, as defined in the Explanation below sub-section (2) of section 288, certifying that the deduction has been correctly claimed in accordance with the provisions of this section.

Where a deduction under this section is claimed and allowed in respect of profits of the business for any assessment year, no deduction shall be allowed in relation to such profits under any other provision of this Act for the same or any other assessment year.

For the purposes of this section, “convertible foreign exchange” shall have the meaning assigned to it in clause (a) of the Explanation to section 80HHC, “film software” means any software or programme recorded on any disc, tape, perforated media or other information storage device for use in connection with films or video recordings, “television software” means any software or programme recorded on any disc, tape, perforated media or other information storage device for use in connection with television broadcasting, “music software” means any software or programme recorded on any disc, tape, perforated media or other information storage device for use in connection with music, “television news software” means any software or programme recorded on any disc, tape, perforated media or other information storage device for use in connection with television news broadcasting, including telecast rights, “export turnover” means the consideration in respect of software received in, or brought into, India by the assessee in convertible foreign exchange in accordance with sub-section (2), but does not include freight, telecommunication charges or insurance attributable to the delivery of the software outside India or expenses, if any, incurred in foreign exchange in providing the services outside India, “total turnover” shall not include any sum referred to in clauses (iiia), (iiib) and (iiic) of section 28, any freight, telecommunication charges or insurance attributable to the delivery of the software outside India, and expenses, if any, incurred in foreign exchange in providing the services outside India.

In conclusion, Section 80HHF of the Income Tax Act, 1961 of India provides for a deduction in the total income of an assessee who is engaged in the business of exporting or transferring by any means out of India film software, television software, music software, television news software, including telecast rights. The deduction is allowed to the extent of profits derived from such business and is subject to certain conditions and limitations. The section defines various terms used in the section and also provides that no deduction shall be allowed in respect of the assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2005 and any subsequent assessment year.

Section 80-I of the Income Tax Act of India allows for a deduction of 20% (or 25% for companies) of the profits and gains derived from an industrial undertaking or a ship or the business of a hotel, provided that the undertaking meets certain conditions. These conditions include not being formed by the splitting up or reconstruction of a business already in existence, not formed by the transfer of machinery or plant previously used for any purpose, manufacturing or producing articles or things or operating a cold storage plant in India, and employing a certain number of workers. The deduction is allowed for profits and gains derived from the undertaking from the 1st day of April, 1981 to the 31st day of March, 1991, with the possibility of an extension by the Central Government. Additionally, there is a provision for a higher deduction of 25% (or 30% for companies) for industrial undertakings, ships, or hotels that begin to operate on or after April 1, 1990 but before April 1, 1991. The section also includes an explanation for what constitutes previously used machinery or plant.
