Summary:

Section 80-IA of the Income Tax Act, 1961 allows for deductions in computing the total income of an assessee in respect of profits and gains derived by an undertaking from certain businesses. The deduction is equal to a certain percentage of the profits of the undertaking and applies to the assessment year relevant to the previous year in which the undertaking begins to manufacture or produce articles or things, operate its cold storage plant or plants, or the ship is first brought into use, or the business of the hotel starts functioning, or the company commences work by way of repairs to ocean-going vessels or other powered craft. The percentage of deduction varies depending on the type of undertaking, with a higher percentage for small-scale industrial undertakings. The deduction is allowed for the initial assessment year and each of the seven assessment years immediately succeeding the initial assessment year, although this period may be extended in certain cases. The profits and gains of the undertaking are computed as if it were the only source of income of the assessee for the purposes of determining the quantum of deduction.

Section 80HH allows for a deduction of 30% of the profits of an undertaking engaged in the business of processing, preservation and packaging of fruit juices, fruit pulps or fruit concentrates. The explanation to this section defines fruit juices, fruit pulps or fruit concentrates as products obtained from the processing, preservation or packaging of fruits in a manner that the essential bio-chemical and physical properties of the fruits are retained and which are suitable for human consumption.

Section 80-IH allows for a deduction of 30% of the profits of an undertaking engaged in the business of providing telecommunication services. The explanation to this section defines telecommunication services as services made available to users by means of any transmission or reception of signs, signals, writing, images and sounds or intelligence of any nature, by wire, radio, visual or other electro-magnetic means.

Section 80-II allows for a deduction of 30% of the profits of an undertaking engaged in the business of production of fertilisers. The explanation to this section defines fertilisers as any substance of any kind used or capable of being used for making the soil more fertile for the growth of plants and includes urea, ammonium sulphate, ammonium nitrate, single super phosphate, triple super phosphate, nitro phosphate, ammonium phosphate, calcium ammonium nitrate, calcite, dolomite, rock phosphate, gypsum, lime and potash.

In all of these sections, the deduction is subject to certain conditions and limitations, and the profits and gains of the undertaking are computed in a specific manner for the purposes of determining the quantum of deduction.
Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India allows for a deduction of 100% of profits and gains derived from certain businesses for a period of ten consecutive assessment years. This deduction applies to industrial undertakings or enterprises engaged in infrastructure development, including the development of infrastructure facilities, telecommunication services, industrial parks, or special economic zones. The deduction can be claimed for any ten consecutive assessment years out of fifteen years beginning from the year in which the undertaking or enterprise begins to operate. In the case of telecommunication services, the deduction is 100% for the first five assessment years and 30% for the next five assessment years. This section applies to undertakings that are not formed by splitting up or reconstructing an existing business and do not use previously used machinery or plant for any purpose, with certain exceptions. The deduction also applies to enterprises that develop or operate and maintain infrastructure facilities and have entered into an agreement with the government for the development or operation and maintenance of the facility. In the case of transfer of an infrastructure facility to another enterprise, the deduction is available to the transferee enterprise for the unexpired period during which the transferor enterprise would have been entitled to the deduction. The definition of "infrastructure facility" includes roads, highways, water supply projects, ports, airports, and industrial parks, among others. The deduction is also available to undertakings that generate or distribute power, lay new transmission or distribution lines, or undertake substantial renovation and modernisation of existing transmission or distribution lines.
Summary:

Section 80-IAB of the Income Tax Act allows a developer of a Special Economic Zone (SEZ) to claim a deduction of 100% of the profits and gains derived from the business of developing an SEZ for ten consecutive assessment years. This deduction can be claimed for any ten consecutive assessment years out of fifteen years beginning from the year in which the SEZ has been notified by the Central Government. However, this deduction is not applicable if the development of the SEZ begins on or after April 1, 2017. If the profits and gains of the undertaking have not been included in the total income due to the application of Section 80-IA(13), the undertaking is entitled to deduction for the unexpired period of ten consecutive assessment years. In case of transfer of operation and maintenance of the SEZ to another developer, the deduction is allowed to the transferee developer for the remaining period. This deduction is not applicable to an assessee who is not a developer. Section 80-IA(5) states that the profits and gains of an eligible business to which the provisions of sub-section (1) apply shall be computed as if such eligible business were the only source of income of the assessee during the previous year relevant to the initial assessment year and to every subsequent assessment year up to and including the assessment year for which the determination is to be made. Section 80-IA(6) states that where housing or other activities are an integral part of the highway project and the profits of which are computed on such basis and manner as may be prescribed, such profit shall not be liable to tax where the profit has been transferred to a special reserve account and the same is actually utilised for the highway project excluding housing and other activities before the expiry of three years following the year in which such amount was transferred to the reserve account. Section 80-IA(7) states that the deduction under sub-section (1) from profits and gains derived from an undertaking shall not be admissible unless the accounts of the undertaking for the previous year relevant to the assessment year for which the deduction is claimed have been audited by an accountant and the assessee furnishes, along with his return of income, the report of such audit in the prescribed form duly signed and verified by such accountant. Section 80-IA(8) states that where any goods or services held for the purposes of the eligible business are transferred to any other business carried on by the assessee, or where any goods or services held for the purposes of any other business carried on by the assessee are transferred to the eligible business and, in either case, the consideration, if any, for such transfer as recorded in the accounts of the eligible business does not correspond to the market value of such goods or services as on the date of the transfer, then, for the purposes of the deduction under this section, the profits and gains of such eligible business shall be computed as if the transfer, in either case, had been made at the market value of such goods or services as on that date. Section 80-IA(9) states that where any amount of profits and gains of an undertaking or of an enterprise in the case of an assessee is claimed and allowed under this section for any assessment year, deduction to the extent of such profits and gains shall not be allowed under any other provisions of this Chapter under the heading” C.—Deductions in respect of certain incomes”, and shall in no case exceed the profits and gains of such eligible business of undertaking or enterprise, as the case may be. Section 80-IA(10) states that where it appears to the Assessing Officer that, owing to the close connection between the assessee carrying on the eligible business to which this section applies and any other person, or for any other reason, the course of business between them is so arranged that the business transacted between them produces to the assessee more than the ordinary profits which might be expected to arise in such eligible business, the Assessing Officer shall, in computing the profits and gains of such eligible business for the purposes of the deduction under this section, take the amount of profits as may be reasonably deemed to have been derived therefrom. Section 80-IA(11) states that the Central Government may, after making such inquiry as it may think fit, direct, by notification in the Official Gazette, that the exemption conferred by this section shall not apply to any class of industrial undertaking or enterprise with effect from such date as it may specify in the notification. Section 80-IA(12) states that where any undertaking of an Indian company which is entitled to the deduction under this section is transferred, before the expiry of the period specified in this section, to another Indian company in a scheme of amalgamation or demerger, no deduction shall be admissible under this section to the amalgamating or the demerged company for the previous year in which the amalgamation or the demerger takes place and the provisions of this section shall, as far as may be, apply to the amalgamated or the resulting company as they would have applied to the amalgamating or the demerged company if the amalgamation or demerger had not taken place. Section 80-IA(13) states that nothing contained in this section shall apply in relation to a business which is in the nature of a works contract awarded by any person (including the Central or State Government) and executed by the undertaking or enterprise referred to in sub-section (1).

Summary:

Section 80-IA of the Income Tax Act allows for deductions on profits and gains from specified businesses in India. Sub-section (1) states that where the gross total income of an assessee includes any profits and gains derived from an eligible business, there shall be allowed a deduction of an amount equal to 100% of the profits and gains derived from such business for three consecutive assessment years. This deduction may be claimed by the assessee for any three consecutive assessment years out of seven beginning from the year in which the eligible business is incorporated.

An eligible business is defined in sub-section (4) as a business carried out by an eligible start-up engaged in innovation, development or improvement of products or processes or services or a scalable business model with a high potential of employment generation or wealth creation. An eligible start-up is defined as a company or a limited liability partnership engaged in eligible business which fulfills certain conditions, such as being incorporated on or after April 1, 2016 but before April 1, 2021, having a total turnover of not more than 25 crore rupees in the previous year relevant to the assessment year for which deduction is claimed, and holding a certificate of eligible business from the Inter-Ministerial Board of Certification.

Sub-section (5) and sub-sections (7) to (11) of section 80-IA apply to the start-ups for the purpose of allowing deductions under sub-section (1).

Section 80-IB allows for deductions on profits and gains from certain industrial undertakings other than infrastructure development undertakings. This section applies to any industrial undertaking which fulfills certain conditions, such as not being formed by splitting up or the reconstruction of a business already in existence, not formed by the transfer to a new business of machinery or plant previously used for any purpose, manufacturing or producing any article or thing not specified in the list in the Eleventh Schedule, and employing ten or more workers in a manufacturing process carried on with the aid of power or employing twenty or more workers in a manufacturing process carried on without the aid of power.

The amount of deduction in the case of an industrial undertaking is 25% (or 30% where the assessee is a company) of the profits and gains derived from such industrial undertaking for a period of ten consecutive assessment years (or twelve consecutive assessment years where the assessee is a co-operative society) beginning with the initial assessment year. This deduction is subject to the fulfillment of certain conditions, such as beginning to manufacture or produce articles or things or to operate a plant or plants at a specified time period.

In the case of an industrial undertaking in an industrially backward State specified in the Eighth Schedule, the amount of deduction is 100% of the profits and gains derived from such industrial undertaking for five assessment years beginning with the initial assessment year and thereafter 25% (or 30% where the assessee is a company) of the profits and gains derived from such industrial undertaking. The total period of deduction does not exceed ten consecutive assessment years (or twelve consecutive assessment years where the assessee is a co-operative society).

The amount of deduction and the period of deduction depend on the category of the district and the commencement of operations.

Summary:

This document summarizes various provisions related to tax deductions under Section 80-IA of the Income Tax Act, 1961. The section provides for deductions for profits and gains derived from specified businesses.

Sub-section (2) defines the term 'specified business', which includes businesses such as developing and building housing projects, scientific research and development, and cold chain facilities for agricultural produce. The deductions available for these businesses vary depending on the nature of the business and the time period for which the deduction is claimed.

For example, in the case of housing projects approved before March 31, 2008, an undertaking can claim a deduction of 100% of the profits derived from the housing project for the relevant assessment year, subject to certain conditions. These conditions include the size of the plot of land (minimum one acre), the maximum built-up area of the residential units (1000 square feet within the city of Delhi or Mumbai or within 25 kilometers from the municipal limits of these cities, and 1500 square feet at any other place), and the built-up area of the shops and other commercial establishments included in the housing project (not more than 3% or 5000 square feet, whichever is higher, of the aggregate built-up area of the housing project).

Similarly, in the case of scientific research and development, a company can claim a deduction of 100% of the profits and gains of such business for a period of five assessment years beginning from the initial assessment year if certain conditions are met, such as being registered in India, having the main object of scientific and industrial research and development, and being approved by the prescribed authority.

The document also includes provisions for deductions in the case of cold chain facilities for agricultural produce, processing, preservation and packaging of fruits or vegetables or meat and meat products or poultry or marine or dairy products or from the integrated business of handling, storage and transportation of foodgrains.

It is important to note that these deductions are subject to certain conditions and limitations, and the specific provisions of the Income Tax Act, 1961 should be consulted for more details.

Summary:

The provided document consists of five sections of the Income Tax Act, 1961, specifically sections 80-IA, 80-IB, 80-IC, 80-ID, and 80-IE. These sections offer deductions on profits and gains derived by certain undertakings or enterprises.

1. Section 80-IA: This section pertains to profits and gains from industrial undertakings or enterprises engaged in infrastructure development. The deduction ranges from 100% to 25% of profits for specified periods, depending on the nature of the undertaking or enterprise. It applies to undertakings or enterprises located in specified areas or those undertaking substantial expansion.

2. Section 80-IB: This section provides deductions for profits and gains from specified businesses in specified areas. The deduction ranges from 100% to 25% of profits for specified periods, depending on the nature of the undertaking or enterprise. It applies to undertakings or enterprises located in specified areas or those undertaking substantial expansion.

3. Section 80-IC: This section offers deductions for profits and gains from undertakings in specified backward areas. The deduction ranges from 100% to 30% of profits for specified periods, depending on the location and nature of the undertaking.

4. Section 80-ID: This section provides deductions for profits and gains from the business of hotels and convention centers in specified areas. The deduction is 100% of profits for five consecutive assessment years beginning from the initial assessment year. It applies to hotels and convention centers located in the National Capital Territory of Delhi and specific districts of Faridabad, Gurgaon, Gautam Budh Nagar, and Ghaziabad.

5. Section 80-IE: This section offers deductions for profits and gains from undertakings in North-Eastern States. The deduction is 100% of profits for ten consecutive assessment years commencing with the initial assessment year. It applies to undertakings that manufacture or produce eligible articles or things, undertake substantial expansion, or carry on eligible businesses in the North-Eastern States.
Summary:

Section 80JJA of the Income Tax Act allows a deduction for profits and gains derived from the business of collecting and processing or treating of bio-degradable waste for generating power or producing bio-fertilizers, bio-pesticides or other biological agents or for producing bio-gas or making pellets or briquettes for fuel or organic manure. The deduction is equal to the whole of such profits and gains for a period of five consecutive assessment years beginning with the assessment year relevant to the previous year in which such business commences.

Section 80JJAA allows a deduction of 30% of additional employee cost incurred in the course of a business for three assessment years including the assessment year relevant to the previous year in which such employment is provided. However, this deduction is not allowed if the business is formed by splitting up or reconstruction of an existing business, acquired by the assessee by way of transfer from any other person or as a result of any business reorganization, or unless the assessee furnishes along with the return of income the report of the accountant as defined in the Explanation to section 288 giving such particulars in the report as may be prescribed.

The term "additional employee cost" means total emoluments paid or payable to additional employees employed during the previous year. However, it does not include any contribution paid or payable by the employer to any pension fund or provident fund or any other fund for the benefit of the employee under any law for the time being in force, any lump-sum payment paid or payable to an employee at the time of termination of his service or superannuation or voluntary retirement, such as gratuity, severance pay, leave encashment, voluntary retrenchment benefits, commutation of pension and the like.

The term "additional employee" means an employee who has been employed during the previous year and whose employment has the effect of increasing the total number of employees employed by the employer as on the last day of the preceding year, but does not include an employee whose total emoluments are more than twenty-five thousand rupees per month, an employee for whom the entire contribution is paid by the Government under the Employees’ Pension Scheme notified in accordance with the provisions of the Employees’ Provident Funds and Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 1952, an employee employed for a period of less than two hundred and forty days during the previous year, or an employee who does not participate in the recognized provident fund.

The provisions of section 80JJAA, as they stood immediately prior to their amendment by the Finance Act, 2016, shall apply to an assessee eligible to claim any deduction for any assessment year commencing on or before the 1st day of April, 2016.

Section 80LA allows a deduction in respect of certain incomes of Offshore Banking Units and International Financial Services Centre. The deduction is allowed if the gross total income of an assessee includes any income referred to in sub-section (2) and the assessee is a scheduled bank or any bank incorporated by or under the laws of a country outside India and having an Offshore Banking Unit in a Special Economic Zone or a Unit of an International Financial Services Centre.
Summary:

Section 80LA of the Income Tax Act allows for a deduction of income for an Offshore Banking Unit (OBU) in a Special Economic Zone (SEZ), a business referred to in section 6 of the Banking Regulation Act, 1949 with an undertaking in a SEZ or any other undertaking that develops, operates, or maintains a SEZ, or any Unit of the International Financial Services Centre from its business for which it has been approved for setting up in such a Centre in a SEZ. The deduction is 100% for five consecutive assessment years beginning with the assessment year relevant to the previous year in which the permission was obtained, and 50% for the next five consecutive assessment years. The income referred to in sub-section (1) is the income from an OBU in a SEZ, from the business referred to in section 6 of the Banking Regulation Act, 1949 with an undertaking in a SEZ or any other undertaking that develops, operates, or maintains a SEZ, or any Unit of the International Financial Services Centre from its business for which it has been approved for setting up in such a Centre in a SEZ. No deduction under this section shall be allowed unless the assessee furnishes along with the return of income, the report of an accountant as defined in the Explanation below sub-section (2) of section 288, certifying that the deduction has been correctly claimed in accordance with the provisions of this section, and a copy of the permission obtained under clause (a) of sub-section (1) of section 23 of the Banking Regulation Act, 1949 (10 of 1949). For the purposes of this section, International Financial Services Centre, scheduled bank, Special Economic Zone, and Unit have the same meanings as assigned to them in the Special Economic Zones Act, 2005.

Section 80M, 80MM, and 80N have been omitted.

Section 80-O allows for a deduction in respect of royalties, etc., from certain foreign enterprises. Where the gross total income of an assessee, being an Indian company or a person (other than a company) who is resident in India, includes any income received by the assessee from the Government of a foreign State or foreign enterprise in consideration for the use outside India of any patent, invention, design or registered trade mark and such income is received in convertible foreign exchange in India, or having been received in convertible foreign exchange outside India, is brought into India, by or on behalf of the assessee in accordance with any law for the time being in force for regulating payments and dealings in foreign exchange, there shall be allowed, in accordance with and subject to the provisions of this section, a deduction of an amount equal to 40% for an assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2001; 30% for an assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2002; 20% for an assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2003; and 10% for an assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2004, in computing the total income of the assessee. No deduction shall be allowed in respect of the assessment year beginning on the 1st day of April, 2005 and any subsequent assessment year. The income must be received in India within a period of six months from the end of the previous year, or within such further period as the competent authority may allow in this behalf. The assessee must furnish a certificate, in the prescribed form, along with the return of income, certifying that the deduction has been correctly claimed in accordance with the provisions of this section. For the purposes of this section, convertible foreign exchange means foreign exchange which is for the time being treated by the Reserve Bank of India as convertible foreign exchange for the purposes of the law for the time being in force for regulating payments and dealings in foreign exchange; foreign enterprise means a person who is a non-resident; services rendered or agreed to be rendered outside India shall include services rendered from India but shall not include services rendered in India; and competent authority means the Reserve Bank of India or such other authority as is authorised under any law for the time being in force for regulating payments and dealings in foreign exchange.
Summary:

This document pertains to the Indian Income Tax Act, specifically addressing the deductions allowed for various types of income. 

Section 80P provides deductions for cooperative societies. The deduction varies depending on the type of society and its income sources. For instance, a consumers' cooperative society can claim a deduction on the whole of its income derived from letting godowns or warehouses for storage, processing, or marketing of commodities. A housing society, urban consumers' society, or a society engaged in manufacturing operations with power can claim a deduction on income by way of interest on securities or any income from house property, provided their gross total income does not exceed twenty thousand rupees.

Section 80PA offers a 100% deduction on profits and gains attributable to an eligible business for a Producer Company having a total turnover of less than one hundred crore rupees in any previous year. Eligible business includes the marketing of agricultural produce grown by the members, the purchase of agricultural implements, seeds, livestock, or other articles intended for agriculture for the purpose of supplying them to the members, or the processing of the agricultural produce of the members.

Section 80Q allows a 20% deduction on profits and gains derived from a business carried on in India of printing and publication of books or publication of books. This deduction is not applicable if the assessee is also entitled to deductions under sections 80HH, 80HHA, 80HHC, 80-I, 80-IA, 80J, or 80P.

Section 80QQ, which was omitted, used to allow a deduction in respect of profits and gains from the business of publication of books.

Section 80QQA provides a 25% deduction on income derived by an individual resident in India, being an author, in the exercise of his profession on account of any lump sum consideration for the assignment or grant of any of his interests in the copyright of any book, or of royalties or copyright fees (whether receivable in lump sum or otherwise) in respect of such book. This deduction is subject to certain conditions, such as the book being in the nature of a dictionary, thesaurus, or encyclopaedia, or being prescribed or recommended as a text book, or included in the curriculum, by any University for a degree or post-graduate course of that University, and being written in any language specified in the Eighth Schedule to the Constitution or in any such other language as the Central Government may specify.

Section 80QQB offers a deduction in respect of royalty income, etc., of authors of certain books other than text-books. The deduction is equal to the whole of such income or an amount of three lakh rupees, whichever is less. This deduction is also subject to certain conditions.
Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India includes several sections that provide deductions for certain types of income earned from foreign sources. Section 80QQB allows a deduction for income earned from the sale of books by authors, excluding certain types of publications. The deduction is a percentage of the total value of books sold during the previous year, and specific rules apply for income earned from sources outside India. Section 80R provides a deduction for remuneration received by Indian citizens from universities, educational institutions, associations, or bodies established outside India for services rendered during their stay outside India in their capacity as professors, teachers, or research workers. The deduction is a percentage of the remuneration and is subject to certain conditions, including the requirement that the income be brought into India in convertible foreign exchange within a specific time frame. Section 80RR allows a deduction for professional income derived from foreign sources in certain cases, including authors, playwrights, artists, musicians, actors, and sportspeople. The deduction is a percentage of the income and is subject to similar conditions as Section 80R. Section 80RRA provides a deduction for remuneration received in foreign currency for services rendered outside India by Indian citizens. The deduction is a percentage of the remuneration and is subject to certain conditions, including the requirement that the income be brought into India in convertible foreign exchange within a specific time frame. The term "competent authority" is used throughout these sections and refers to the Reserve Bank of India or another authority authorized under any law for the time being in force for regulating payments and dealings in foreign exchange.
Summary:
The Income Tax Act of India allows for various deductions in the computation of an individual's total income. This summary focuses on Section 80RRB, which allows for a deduction in respect of royalty on patents, and Section 80TTA, which allows for a deduction in respect of interest on deposits in savings account.

Section 80RRB:
This section allows for a deduction in respect of royalty on patents. It applies to an individual who is a resident of India, a patentee, and in receipt of any income by way of royalty in respect of a patent registered on or after April 1, 2003 under the Patents Act, 1970. The deduction is equal to the whole of such income or three lakh rupees, whichever is less. No deduction will be allowed if the income by way of royalty exceeds the amount of royalty under the terms and conditions of a licence settled by the Controller under the Patents Act, 1970. The deduction is also not allowed if the income earned from any source outside India is not brought into India by, or on behalf of, the assessee in convertible foreign exchange within a period of six months from the end of the previous year in which such income is earned. A certificate in the prescribed form, duly signed by the prescribed authority, along with the return of income, is required to claim the deduction.

Section 80TTA:
This section allows for a deduction in respect of interest on deposits in savings account. It applies to an individual or a Hindu undivided family, and the income by way of interest on deposits (not being time deposits) in a savings account with a banking company, a co-operative society engaged in carrying on the business of banking, or a Post Office. The deduction is equal to the whole of such amount or ten thousand rupees, whichever is less. No deduction will be allowed if the income is derived from any deposit in a savings account held by, or on behalf of, a firm, an association of persons or a body of individuals.

In addition to these sections, the Income Tax Act of India also allows for other deductions such as Section 80U which allows for a deduction in case of a person with disability.
Summary:

The Income Tax Act of India includes various sections that provide deductions and exemptions for individuals and businesses. Section 80U of the Act allows for a deduction of up to 75,000 rupees for individuals with a disability, as defined by the Persons with Disabilities (Equal Opportunities, Protection of Rights and Full Participation) Act, 1995. This includes conditions such as autism, cerebral palsy, and multiple disabilities. The deduction can be claimed by furnishing a certificate issued by a medical authority in the prescribed form along with the return of income. If the condition of disability requires reassessment, a new certificate must be obtained and furnished for any assessment year relating to a previous year beginning after the expiry of the previous year during which the certificate of disability had expired. The term "disability" has the meaning assigned to it in clause (i) of section 2 of the Persons with Disabilities (Equal Opportunities, Protection of Rights and Full Participation) Act, 1995, and includes "autism", "cerebral palsy" and "multiple disabilities" referred to in clauses (a), (c) and (h) of section 2 of the National Trust for Welfare of Persons with Autism, Cerebral Palsy, Mental Retardation and Multiple Disabilities Act, 1999. The term "medical authority" refers to the medical authority as defined in clause (p) of section 2 of the Persons with Disabilities (Equal Opportunities, Protection of Rights and Full Participation) Act, 1995, or such other medical authority as may be specified by the Central Government for certifying "autism", "cerebral palsy", "multiple disabilities", "person with disability" and "severe disability". The term "person with disability" refers to a person with a disability as defined in clause (t) of section 2 of the Persons with Disabilities (Equal Opportunities, Protection of Rights and Full Participation) Act, 1995, or clause (j) of section 2 of the National Trust for Welfare of Persons with Autism, Cerebral Palsy, Mental Retardation and Multiple Disabilities Act, 1999. The term "person with severe disability" refers to a person with eighty per cent or more of one or more disabilities, as referred to in sub-section (4) of section 56 of the Persons with Disabilities (Equal Opportunities, Protection of Rights and Full Participation) Act, 1995, or a person with severe disability referred to in clause (o) of section 2 of the National Trust for Welfare of Persons with Autism, Cerebral Palsy, Mental Retardation and Multiple Disabilities Act, 1999.

The Fourth Schedule deals with recognized provident funds and sets out the conditions such a fund must meet to receive and retain recognition. These conditions include all employees being employed in India or by an employer whose principal place of business is in India, contributions being a definite proportion of an employee's salary, and the fund being vested in trustees under a trust which is not revocable. Summary:

The document outlines the rules and regulations for a recognized provident fund in India. The fund is established by an employer for the benefit of its employees and is managed by trustees. Contributions to the fund can be made by both the employer and the employee, and the accumulated balance is payable to the employee when they cease to be employed. There are certain conditions and restrictions on the payment of the balance, such as a minimum period of continuous service or specific circumstances for termination of service. The fund may also consist of amounts transferred from individual accounts of employees in other recognized provident funds. The rules also provide for the relaxation of certain conditions under specific circumstances. The accumulated balance in the fund is exempt from income tax under certain conditions, such as continuous service for five years or more, termination of service due to ill health or closure of the employer's business. The tax on the accumulated balance is calculated based on the tax that would have been payable if the fund had not been a recognized provident fund. The trustees of the fund are responsible for deducting income tax at source on the accumulated balance payable to the employee. The rules also provide for the treatment of balances in newly recognized provident funds.
Summary:
The document discusses the rules and regulations for recognized provident funds in India. These funds must maintain accounts that are open to inspection by income-tax authorities and must provide abstracts of those accounts to the Assessing Officer. Employers who object to an order refusing or withdrawing recognition from a provident fund may appeal to the Board within 60 days. The document also covers the treatment of funds transferred by an employer to trustees in trust for employees, which is deemed to be capital expenditure. The Board has the power to make rules regarding statements and other information to be submitted with an application for recognition, limiting contributions by employees, regulating the investment or deposit of fund moneys, assessing consideration received for an assignment or charge of a beneficial interest in a recognized provident fund, and determining exemption from tax in respect of contributions and interest credited to individual accounts of employees in a provident fund from which recognition has been withdrawn. Part B of the document discusses approved superannuation funds, which must be established under an irrevocable trust in connection with a trade or undertaking carried on in India, with at least 90% of the employees employed in India. The fund must have for its sole purpose the provision of annuities for employees in the trade or undertaking on their retirement or incapacitation, or for the widows, children, or dependants of such employees. The employer in the trade or undertaking must be a contributor to the fund, and all annuities, pensions, and other benefits granted from the fund must be payable only in India. An application for approval of a superannuation fund or part of a superannuation fund must be made in writing by the trustees of the fund to the Assessing Officer by whom the employer is assessable, and must be accompanied by a copy of the instrument under which the fund is established and by two copies of the rules and accounts of the fund for the prior year or years. If any alteration is made in the rules, constitution, objects, or conditions of the fund after the date of the application for approval, the trustees of the fund must forthwith communicate such alteration to the Assessing Officer, and in default of such communication any approval given shall be deemed to have been withdrawn. Contributions by an employer, including interest thereon, that are repaid to the employer are deemed to be the income of the employer of the previous year in which they are so repaid. Tax on contributions made by an employer and paid to an employee during his lifetime must be deducted at the average rate of tax at which the employee was liable to tax during the preceding three years or during the period, if less than three years, when he was a member of the fund. The trustees of the fund must pay the tax to the credit of the Central Government within the prescribed time and in such manner as the Board may direct. Where an employer deducts from the emoluments paid to an employee or pays on his behalf any contributions of that employee to an approved superannuation fund, he must include all such deductions or payments in the return which he is required to furnish under section 206. An employer or trustee of a superannuation fund who objects to an order of the Principal Chief Commissioner or Chief Commissioner refusing or withdrawing approval from a superannuation fund may appeal to the Board within 60 days.

An approved superannuation fund is one that complies with certain conditions, including being established under an irrevocable trust, having the sole purpose of providing gratuity to employees, and having the employer as a contributor. The 1[Principal Chief Commissioner or Chief Commissioner] or 2[Principal Commissioner or Commissioner] has the power to grant or withdraw approval of a superannuation fund. If a fund ceases to be an approved superannuation fund, the trustees remain liable to tax on any sum paid on account of returned contributions. The Board has the power to make rules regarding the approval and withdrawal of approval, conditions for approval, and the assessment of penalties. Similar rules apply to approved gratuity funds, which are funds established for the purpose of providing gratuity to employees in a trade or undertaking carried on in India. Chapter XXB of the Act deals with the requirement as to the mode of acceptance, payment, or repayment in certain cases to counteract evasion of tax. Section 269UB deals with the appropriate authority, while Section 269UC deals with restrictions on the transfer of immovable property. Summary:

The document pertains to Indian laws related to the administration and taxation of provident funds and superannuation funds. A provident fund is an employee benefit fund established by an employer, which can be recognized by the Income Tax Department to grant tax exemptions on contributions and interest earned. The rules for recognition, withdrawal of recognition, and conditions for approval of a provident fund are detailed. The accounts of a recognized provident fund must be maintained in a specific form, be open for inspection by income tax authorities, and provide abstracts to the Assessing Officer. Employers can appeal the orders of tax authorities regarding recognition or withdrawal of recognition.

A transferred balance from an unrecognized provident fund to a recognized one is calculated, and the aggregate is deemed to be income received by the employee in the previous year in which recognition takes effect. The remainder of the transferred balance is disregarded, but no other exemption or relief shall be granted in respect of any sum comprised in such transferred balance.

Superannuation funds are also covered in the document. An employer can establish a superannuation fund for the benefit of their employees, and such a fund can receive approval from the Income Tax Department. The conditions for approval, application for approval, and consequences of alterations in the rules, constitution, objects, or conditions of the fund are discussed.

The taxation of contributions and payments from provident funds and superannuation funds is also addressed. Contributions by an employer, including interest, repaid to the employer are deemed to be the income of the employer of the previous year in which it is so repaid. Tax on contributions paid to an employee, including interest, is deducted at the average rate of tax at which the employee was liable to tax during the preceding three years or during the period, if less than three years, when he was a member of the fund. The trustees of the fund are responsible for deducting tax and paying it to the credit of the Central Government.

In summary, the document outlines the rules and regulations related to provident funds and superannuation funds in India, including the conditions for recognition, taxation of contributions and payments, and the responsibilities of employers and trustees.
Summary:

The Income Tax Act, 1961, includes provisions related to approved superannuation funds and approved gratuity funds. An approved superannuation fund is a fund established under an irrevocable trust in connection with a trade or undertaking carried on in India, and not less than ninety per cent of the employees shall be employed in India. The fund must have for its sole purpose the provision of a gratuity to employees in the trade or undertaking on their retirement at or after a specified age or on their becoming incapacitated prior to such retirement or on termination of their employment after a minimum period of service specified in the rules of the fund or to the widows, children or dependants of such employees on their death. The employer in the trade or undertaking must be a contributor to the fund, and all benefits granted by the fund must be payable only in India.

An application for approval of a superannuation fund must be made in writing by the trustees of the fund to the Assessing Officer, accompanied by a copy of the instrument under which the fund is established and by two copies of the rules. If any alteration in the rules, constitution, objects or conditions of the fund is made at any time after the date of the application for approval, the trustees of the fund must forthwith communicate such alterations to the Assessing Officer.

Where an employer deducts from the emoluments paid to an employee or pays on his behalf any contributions of that employee to an approved superannuation fund, he shall include all such deductions or payments in the return which he is required to furnish under section 206.

An employer objecting to an order of the Principal Chief Commissioner or Chief Commissioner refusing to accord approval to a superannuation fund or an order withdrawing such approval may appeal, within sixty days of such order, to the Board. The appeal shall be in such form and shall be verified in such manner and shall be subject to the payment of such fee as may be prescribed.

If a fund or a part of a fund for any reason ceases to be an approved superannuation fund, the trustees of the fund shall nevertheless remain liable to tax on any sum paid on account of returned contributions (including interest on contributions, if any), in so far as the sum so paid is in respect of contributions made before the fund or part of the fund ceased to be an approved superannuation fund under the provisions of this Part.

The trustees of an approved superannuation fund and any employer who contributes to an approved superannuation fund shall, when required by notice from the Assessing Officer, within such period, not being less than twenty-one days from the date of the notice, as may be specified in the notice, furnish such return, statement, particulars or information, as the Assessing Officer may require.

The Board may make rules prescribing the statements and other information to be submitted along with an application for approval, prescribing the returns, statements, particulars, or information which the Assessing Officer may require from the trustees of an approved superannuation fund or from the employer, limiting the ordinary annual contribution and any other contributions to an approved superannuation fund by an employer, regulating the investment or deposit of the moneys of an approved superannuation fund, providing for the assessment by way of penalty of any consideration received by an employee for an assignment of, or creation of a charge upon, his beneficial interest in an approved superannuation fund, determining the extent to, and the manner in, which exemption from payment of tax may be granted in respect of any payment made from a superannuation fund from which approval has been withdrawn, providing for the withdrawal of approval in the case of a fund which ceases to satisfy the requirements of this Part or of the rules made thereunder, and generally, to carry out the purposes of this Part and to secure such further control over the approval of the superannuation funds and the administration of approved superannuation funds as it may deem requisite.

An approved gratuity fund is a fund established under an irrevocable trust in connection with a trade or undertaking carried on in India, and not less than ninety per cent of the employees shall be employed in India. The fund must have for its sole purpose the provision of a gratuity to employees in the trade or undertaking on their retirement at or after a specified age or on their becoming incapacitated prior to such retirement or on termination of their employment after a minimum period of service specified in the rules of the fund or to the widows, children or dependants of such employees on their death. The employer in the trade or undertaking must be a contributor to the fund, and all benefits granted by the fund must be payable only in India.

An application for approval of a gratuity fund shall be made in writing by the trustees of the fund to the Assessing Officer, accompanied by a copy of the instrument under which the fund is established and by two copies of the rules. If any alteration in the rules, constitution, objects or conditions of the fund is made at any time after the date of the application for approval, the trustees of the fund must forthwith communicate such alterations to the Assessing Officer.

Where any gratuity is paid to an employee during his lifetime, the gratuity shall be treated as salary paid to the employee for the purposes of this Act.

If a gratuity fund for any reason ceases to be an approved gratuity fund, the trustees of the fund shall nevertheless remain liable to tax on any gratuity paid to any employee.

Where any contributions by an employer (including the interest thereon, if any) are repaid to the employer, the amount so repaid shall be deemed for the purposes of income-tax to be the income of the employer of the previous year in which they are so repaid.

An employer objecting to an order of the Principal Chief Commissioner or Chief Commissioner refusing to accord approval to a gratuity fund or an order withdrawing such approval may appeal, within sixty days of such order, to the Board. The appeal shall be in such form and shall be verified in such manner and shall be subject to the payment of such fee as may be prescribed.

The trustees of an approved gratuity fund and any employer who contributes to an approved gratuity fund shall, when required by notice from the Assessing Officer, furnish within such period, not being less than twenty-one days from the date of the notice, as may be specified in the notice, such return, statement, particulars or information, as the Assessing Officer may require.

The Board may make rules prescribing the statements and other information to be submitted along with an application for approval, limiting the ordinary annual and other contributions of an employer to the fund, regulating the investment or deposit of the moneys of an approved gratuity fund, providing for the assessment by way of penalty of any consideration received by an employee for an assignment of, or creation of a charge upon, his beneficial interest in an approved gratuity fund, and generally, to carry out the purposes of this Part and to secure such further control over the approval of the gratuity funds and the administration of approved gratuity funds as it may deem requisite.

* The definition of income has been amended to include income from the transfer of capital assets, such as property or shares, at any time during the previous year.
* Deductions under section 80C have been increased from Rs. 10,000 to Rs. 15,000.
* The rate of tax for individuals has been increased from 10% to 20% for income between Rs. 10,00,000 and Rs. 25,00,000.
* A new section 80CCD has been inserted to provide deductions for contributions made to the National Pension System (NPS).
* A new section 80TTA has been inserted to provide deductions for interest earned on savings accounts with banks and post offices.
* A new section 80CCG has been inserted to provide deductions for investment in equity savings schemes.
* The rate of tax for domestic companies has been increased from 30% to 33%.
* A new section 115BAA has been inserted to provide for a lower rate of tax for domestic companies.
* A new section 115BAB has been inserted to provide for a lower rate of tax for new manufacturing companies.
* The rate of tax for foreign companies has been increased from 40% to 43%.
* A new section 115JB has been inserted to provide for a minimum alternate tax for foreign companies.

It is important to note that these amendments are subject to the provisions of the Income Tax Act, 1961, and any other relevant laws. It is recommended that taxpayers consult with a tax professional to understand the implications of these amendments on their specific situation.

Summary:

The Income Tax Act of 1961, as amended by several subsequent acts, contains provisions related to the assessment, taxation, and appeals for income tax in India. The act includes various sections that outline the powers and duties of tax authorities, the procedures for assessing and challenging tax assessments, and the penalties for non-compliance.

Some notable sections of the act include:

* Section 133A, which grants tax authorities the power to summon any person for the purpose of examination and inspection in relation to an assessment.
* Section 143, which outlines the procedure for making an assessment of a taxpayer's income.
* Section 246A, which allows taxpayers to appeal decisions made by the Assessing Officer to the Commissioner of Income Tax.
* Section 252, which allows taxpayers to appeal decisions made by the Commissioner of Income Tax to the Income Tax Appellate Tribunal.
* Section 271C, which imposes a penalty for failure to furnish a return of income.
* Section 272A, which imposes a penalty for failure to comply with a notice issued under Section 133A.

The act also includes provisions for the establishment of various tax authorities, such as the Income Tax Appellate Tribunal, the Income Tax Settlement Commission, and the Dispute Resolution Panel. These authorities are responsible for hearing and deciding appeals and disputes related to income tax assessments.

The act has been amended several times since its enactment to address changes in the economy, tax policy, and technology. Some of the notable amendments include the Finance Act of 2000, which introduced the concept of e-filing of income tax returns, and the Finance Act of 2017, which introduced new provisions for the taxation of digital transactions.

It is important to note that this summary is not an exhaustive review of the Income Tax Act of 1961 and its amendments. It is meant to provide an overview of some of the key provisions and concepts in the act. It is recommended to consult the actual text of the act and seek professional advice for any specific tax-related matter.

The document summarizes various amendments made to the Income Tax Act, 1961, related to Indian laws. Here are the key takeaways:

* Section 80J was omitted by Act 33 of 1996, effective April 1, 1990.
* Section 88A was also omitted by Act 33 of 1996, effective April 1, 1994.
* A new section 88A was inserted by Act 11 of 1987, effective April 1, 1988.
* Item 8 was omitted by Act 16 of 1981, effective April 1, 1982.
* Item 9 was substituted by Act 26 of 1988, effective April 1, 1989.
* Items 11 to 21 were omitted by Act 16 of 1981, effective April 1, 1982.
* A new section 80-IC(2) was inserted by Act 23 of 2004, effective April 1, 2005.
* Certain entries were substituted by Act 33 of 2009, effective April 1, 2010.

It is worth noting that several other sections, items, and entries have been omitted or substituted, but they are not related to Indian laws. Therefore, they are not included in this summary.
